They are grateful to Tilman Bauer, David Gepner, and L. Meier for sharing their ongoing work. They also thank Michael Hopkins, Theo Jonhson-Freyd, Kaoru Ono, Tatsuki Kuwagaki, and Thomas Schick for helpful discussions. Ying-Hsuan Lin, Mayuko Yamashita

Abstract
. We construct Topological Elliptic Genera , homotopy-theoretic refinements of the elliptic genera for S ​ U SU -manifolds and variants including the Witten-Landweber-Ochanine genus. The codomains are genuinely G G -equivariant Topological Modular Forms developed by Gepner-Meier [ GM23 ] , twisted by G G -representations. As the first installment of a series of articles on Topological Elliptic Genera, this issue lays the mathematical foundation and discusses immediate applications. Most notably, we deduce an interesting divisibility result for the Euler numbers of S ​ p Sp -manifolds.



\CJKencfamily

UTF8mc

1.
Introduction

There is a classical construction of the
elliptic genus
for
SU
-manifolds, (e.g.,
[
Wit88
]
and
[
Gri99
]
),
which assigns, for each tangential
SU(k)
-manifold
M
with dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=2k
,

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M)\in\left\{\mbox{integral Jacobi Forms with weight}=0,\mbox{ index}=k/2\right\}.

The formula is given by (see Section
1.1
(
18
) for the convention of Jacobi Forms)

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M)(y,q)=y^{k/2}\cdot\int_{M}\mathrm{Todd}(TM)\wedge\mathrm{Ch}\left(\mathbb{TM}_{q,y}\right),

where (in the formula below all the tensor/exterior products are over
\mathbb{C}
, )

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{TM}_{q,y}:=\bigotimes_{m\geq 0}\wedge_{-q^{m}y^{-1}}T^{*}M\otimes\bigotimes_{m\geq 1}\wedge_{-q^{m}y}TM\otimes\bigotimes_{m\geq 1}\mathrm{Sym}_{q^{m}}T^{*}M\otimes\bigotimes_{m\geq 1}\mathrm{Sym}_{q^{m}}TM.

For example, in the notation of
[
Gri99
]
,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}([K3])={2\phi_{0,1}},\quad\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}([\mathrm{CY}_{3}])=(h^{1,1}-h^{1,2})\cdot\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}},

where
\mathrm{CY}_{3}
is any Calabi-Yau threefold with Hodge numbers
h^{1,1}
and
h^{1,2}
.
Related constructions include the
level-
N
genera
which produce modular forms with level structures; most notably, the case of
N=2
is called the
Witten-Landweber-Ochanine genus
for spin manifolds
[
Och91
]
.

The main construction of this paper concerns the
topological
, (or
spectral
) refinements
1
1

1

What we mean by
topological refinements
here is analogous to how the (homotopy-theoretic) sigma orientation
[
AHR10
]
refines the (classical) Witten genera
[
Wit88
]
. The following diagrams illustrate the concept.

\textstyle{MString\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
refine

\rightsquigarrow

refine
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
refine
\textstyle{\Omega^{\text{string}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Wit
\textstyle{\mathrm{MF}}

\textstyle{MTSU(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}}
refine

\rightsquigarrow

refine
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
refine
\textstyle{\Omega^{SU(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{k}}

The left square is about the sigma orientation and the right square is about our topological elliptic genera.
The bottom row consists of
classical
objects, namely maps between abelian groups, whereas the top row consists of
homotopy-theoretical
objects, namely morphism between spectra. The upper row refines the lower one.
We also remark that the classical notion of elliptic cohomology (i.e., a complex-oriented theory whose associated formal group law comes from an elliptic curve) can also be regarded as “topological refinements” of the classical elliptic genera
[
DFHH14
]
. From that point of view, what we construct here can be regarded as the
universal
topological refinement.

of those classical numerical genera; for this reason, we call them the
topological elliptic genera
.
This relies heavily on the recent developments
[
GM23
,
Lurc
]
in the
genuinely
equivariant refinements of the spectrum of
Topological Modular Forms
, or
\mathrm{TMF}
2
2

2

Our construction is closely related to the work by Ando-French-Ganter
[
AFG08
]
. As explained in Section
4.3
, the constructions in this paper are regarded as
genuine
and
unstable
versions of their construction.
.

Our exemplary case is the refinement of the classical elliptic genus (
1.1
). We define a morphism of spectra which we call the
U(1)
-topological elliptic genus
,

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MTSU(k)\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k},

for each nonnegative integer
k
, where the
U(1)
-equivariance can be understood as arising from the complex structure of
SU
-manifolds. Here,

•

MTSU(k)
is the bordism spectrum of
tangential

SU(k)
-manifolds. See Section
2.4
for the explanation.

•

\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
is a
\mathrm{TMF}
-module spectrum called
Topological Jacobi Forms
, realized as genuinely
U(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
. It can be regarded naturally as the topological refinement of Jacobi Forms with index
\frac{k}{2}
and is investigated in an upcoming paper by Bauer-Meier
[
BM25
]
. We collect the necessary facts in Appendix
A
as a user guide.

The spectrum
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
, being a refinement of the module of Jacobi Forms, comes equipped with a map

(1.14)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{JF}}\colon\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\to\left\{\mbox{integral Jacobi Forms with weight}=m/2-k,\mbox{ index}=k/2\right\}=:\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=m}.

(here
\mathrm{JF}_{k}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded module of integral Jacobi Forms with index
\frac{k}{2}
, whose degree convention is explained in Section
1.1
(
18
)),
and the topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
refines the classical elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm clas}
in the sense that, when applied to the case
m=2k
, we have

(1.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M)=e_{\mathrm{JF}}\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}(M).

Why do we care about such a topological refinement? Indeed, the refinement gives us nontrivial information that cannot be obtained from the numerical elliptic genus, as follows.

(1)

The map
e_{\mathrm{JF}}
is not injective for general
k
. The kernel consists of torsion elements which are invisible as classical Jacobi Forms.
For example, we have

(1.16)

\displaystyle\pi_{5}\mathrm{TJF}_{2}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2,\quad\pi_{7}\mathrm{TJF}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}/2.

Accordingly, our topological elliptic genus can detect torsion elements in
\pi_{*}MTSU(k)
.

(2)

The map
e_{\mathrm{JF}}
is not surjective, although it is rationally equivalent. This means that we get divisibility constraints for Jacobi Forms inside the image of
e_{\mathrm{JF}}
. For example,

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Coker}\left(e_{\mathrm{JF}}\colon\pi_{4}\mathrm{TJF}_{2}\to\mathrm{JF}_{2}|_{\deg=4}\right)=\mathbb{Z}\phi_{0,1}/(2\phi_{0,1}),

meaning that half of the
K3
elliptic genus is not in the image (see Remark
7.47
for further comments).
The general non-surjectivity of
e_{\mathrm{JF}}
, combined with (
1.15
), implies nontrivial divisibility constraints on the classical elliptic genus and consequently on various characteristic numbers for tangential
SU(k)
-manifolds. We investigate this in Section
7.2
.

(3)

Our topological elliptic genus is
unstable
, in the sense that the codomain depends on
k
.
The relations among different
k
are captured by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{MTSU(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{SU(k)\hookrightarrow SU(k+1)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{m}}
\textstyle{\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}\cdot}
\textstyle{MTSU(k+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k+1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{m}}
\textstyle{\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{k+1}|_{\deg=m}}

Interestingly, the third vertical arrow is neither injective nor surjective in general. Rather, it is part of a long exact sequence.
This is in contrast to the rightmost vertical arrow, which is injective.
Thus, our topological elliptic genus can detect nontrivial
\pi_{m}MTSU(k)
elements that vanish in
\pi_{m}MTSU(\infty)=\pi_{m}MSU
and cannot be detected by
\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}
.
Such an example is explained in Section
7.1
.

The above
U(1)
-topological elliptic genus is just a special case of a more general construction we study in this paper. The most general construction is in Section
3.2
.
Under the settings listed there, we construct a class of morphisms from certain Thom spectra to
RO(G)
-graded genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
, which we generally call
topological elliptic genera
.
Besides
U(1)
, other key cases include
3
3

3

see the last paragraph of Introduction

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{k}}\colon MTSp(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}=:\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k},

(1.23)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{O(1)_{k}}\colon MTSpin(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}.

The codomain of (
1.22
),
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
, is
defined
to be the genuinely
Sp(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
, and studied in detail in Appendix
B
. We name it the spectrum of
Topological Even Jacobi Forms
since, as explained there, it is naturally regarded as refining the following direct summand of
\mathrm{JF}_{2k}
:

(1.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}

\displaystyle:=\{\phi(z,\tau)\in\mathrm{JF}_{2k}\ |\ \phi(z,\tau)=\phi(-z,\tau)\},

The morphism (
1.23
) is a topological refinement of the Witten-Landweber-Ochanine genus
[
Och91
]
.

In the remainder of this introduction, we focus on the
Sp(1)
-topological elliptic genus (
1.22
) and illustrate why it tells us interesting things about
Sp
-manifolds beyond the
U(1)
-topological elliptic genus (
1.13
).
Note that, at the classical level, the elliptic genus for
Sp
-manifolds is just the restriction of the assignment (
1.1
), and we obtain no further information. However, after the topological refinement, we detect an interesting difference. The relationship between the
Sp(1)
and
U(1)
-topological elliptic genera is captured in a commutative diagram

\textstyle{MTSp(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{m}}
\textstyle{\pi_{m}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{EJF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=\mbox{ {\tiny or} }0}
\textstyle{MTSU(2k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{2k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{m}}
\textstyle{\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}}

What makes the diagram (
1
) interesting is that the third vertical arrow
r
is neither injective nor surjective. This is in contrast to the rightmost vertical arrow, which is simply zero or the identity depending on the degrees.
This means the following:

(4)

The genuine
Sp
-topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{k}}
can detect (necessarily torsion) elements in the tangential
Sp
-bordism groups that vanish in
SU
-bordism groups.
Examples of such elements are given in Section
7.1
.

(5)

For
m\equiv 0
(mod
4
), although the rightmost vertical arrow is an isomorphism, the map

(1.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{im}\left(e_{\mathrm{EJF}}\colon\pi_{m}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\to\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}\right)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{im}\left(e_{\mathrm{JF}}\colon\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}\to\mathrm{JF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}\right)

is generally a proper inclusion. This implies nontrivial divisibility constraints on the classical elliptic genera and the associated characteristic classes for
Sp
-manifolds. We investigate this in
7.2
.

This paper lays the basics of topological elliptic genera and discusses immediate applications. A notable application is to the divisibility of Euler numbers, such as the following result for tangential
Sp
-manifolds:

Theorem 1.30

(Theorem
7.43
(1))
.

For any closed strict
4
4

4

See Definition
2.118
.
tangential
Sp(k)
-manifold
M_{4k}
of real dimension
4k
, its Euler number satisfies

(1.31)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{24}{\gcd(24,k)}\,\right|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M_{4k}).

This comes from an elementary analysis of
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
, together with the classical relation between
\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}
and Euler numbers. As we explain in Section
7.2
, this strictly refines the divisibility constraints obtained by classical numerical methods. The base case of
k=1
for
Sp(1)=SU(2)
-manifolds gives divisibility by 24, which is saturated by the Euler number of a
K3
surface.

Section
6
discusses an interesting byproduct of our main construction, the
level-rank duality
in equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
.
The definition of the
U(1)_{k}
-topological elliptic genus uses a
\mathrm{TMF}
-module morphism

(1.32)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)},

where
\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}
is the
SU(k)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
with fundamental (“level
1
”) twist. It turns out that the morphism (
1.32
) exhibits
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
as the
dual
of
\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
(in the categorical sense).
More generally, we find the following dualities (Theorems
6.9
,
6.35
),

(1.33)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny dual}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)}]^{Sp(k)}

(1.34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny dual }}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}\quad\mbox{in }\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}.

This coincides with the
level-rank duality

[
Fre06
,
NT92
]
in affine Lie algebras and conformal field theory.
Such an agreement is naturally expected in the context of the Segal-Stolz-Teichner proposal (see Remark
6.4
).

The authors plan to explore this topological elliptic genera in a series of papers. This is the first part of the series, where we lay the basics of the theory. In Part II
[
LY
]
of the series, we plan to discuss the physical interpretations. In further volumes, we plan to explore further examples and applications.

The paper is organized as follows. After the preliminary Section
2
, in Section
3
we give the general definition and basics of topological elliptic genera. In Section
4
, we introduce an important class of our construction, the
U
,
Sp

and
O
-
topological elliptic genera. We will see that these families of topological elliptic genera organize into a unified picture, and we refer to them as the
trio
.
Section
5
gives the characteristic class formula for the equivariant Modular Forms associated with our topological elliptic genera. Section
6
discusses the level-rank duality. Finally, in Section
7
we discuss immediate applications of our construction, including the divisibility of Euler numbers mentioned above. The contents of Sections
5
,
6
, and
7

can be read independently of each other, and the reader may find it useful to read in their preferred order.

Appendices
A
and
B
are about the basics of
\mathrm{TJF}
(
=U(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
) and
\mathrm{TEJF}
(
=Sp(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
), respectively.
The authors believe these spectra are of independent interest and hope that the self-contained appendices contribute to future studies.
The content of Appendix
A
is contained in an upcoming work by Bauer-Meier
[
BM25
]
, so the authors claim no originality of the content. On the other hand, the content of Appendix
B
is a new result of this paper.

Appendix
C
explains a toy model of the main body of this paper, where we replace
\mathrm{TMF}
with
\mathrm{KO}
, resulting in
topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera
. Although the contents of that section are not used in the main body, the authors hope they serve as a warm-up to the main part.

We conclude the introduction with an important remark. This paper relies on the equivariant refinement of the sigma orientation. As explained in Section
4.1
, currently, we have partially established the equivariant sigma orientation for a nice class of compact Lie groups, but not for all compact Lie groups.
In this paper, we derive mathematical results based on the current status (Fact
2.104
). However, we would also like to present the results we can get once we assume the full establishment of the equivariant sigma orientation,
Conjecture
2.105
; this gives us a complete and unified picture of our topological elliptic genera. Therefore, in this paper, we put
shaded backgrounds
on the statements and proofs that depend on
Conjecture
2.105
. The rest of the contents are based on the current status and are completely valid.

1.1.
Notations and conventions

(1)

\mathrm{Spectra}
denotes the stable
\infty
-category of spectra,
\mathcal{S}
the
\infty
-category of spaces, and
\mathcal{S}_{*}
that of pointed spaces.
S\in\mathrm{Spectra}
the sphere spectrum, and
\mathrm{cptLie}
the category of compact Lie groups and continuous homomorphisms.

e\in\mathrm{cptLie}
denotes the trivial group.

(2)

We denote by
\eta\in\pi_{1}S
and
\nu\in\pi_{3}S
the (
integral
, not
2
-local) generators of
\pi_{1}S\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\pi_{3}S\simeq\mathbb{Z}/24
respectively, which we choose to be represented by the Lie group manifolds
U(1)
and
SU(2)
, respectively.

(3)

The notations on
G
-equivariant homotopy theory are summarized in Section
2.1
. Among others, we note that
E^{G}
denotes the
genuine

G
-fixed point spectrum of a genuine
G
-spectrum
E\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
.

(4)

For
G\in\mathrm{cptLie}
, we dentote by
\mathbf{B}G
the topological stack
\mathbf{B}G:=[*//G]
. On the other hand,
BG\in\mathcal{S}
denotes the classifying space.
Let
\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
denote the
groupoid
of orthogonal representation of
G
and isomorphisms, and
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
denote those of virtual orthogonal representations.

(5)

Given an element
\tau\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, we denote by
S^{\tau}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
the virtual representation sphere spectrum, and denote

(1.35)

\displaystyle E[\tau]:=E\otimes S^{\tau}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}.

In particular, we write, for any
E\in\mathrm{Spectra}
and any integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(1.36)

\displaystyle E[n]:=E[n\underline{\mathbb{R}}]=\Sigma^{n}E.

(6)

For an element
\tau\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, we define

(1.37)

\displaystyle\overline{\tau}:=\tau-\dim(\tau)\cdot 1\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)

where
1=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
is the class of the one-dimensional trivial representation. Similarly, for a real virtual vector bundle
\theta
over a topological space
X
, we denote

(1.38)

\displaystyle\overline{\theta}:=\theta-\mathrm{rank}(\theta)\cdot\underline{\mathbb{R}},

where
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the trivial real vector bundle over
X
; when
X=BG
, this agrees with the previous meaning of
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
.

(7)

For a real
G
-representation
V
, we define

(1.39)

\displaystyle\chi(V)\in\mathrm{Map}(S^{0},S^{V})^{G}

to be the unique nontrivial
G
-equivariant map sending
0\mapsto 0
and
\infty\mapsto\infty
. We also use the same symbol to mean the
G
-equivariant map

(1.40)

\displaystyle\chi(V):=\mathrm{id}_{E}\otimes\chi_{V}\colon E\to E\otimes S^{V}=E[V]

for any
G
-equivariant spectrum
E
.
The homotopy class of
\chi(V)
is called the
Euler class
for the representation
V
, and we also denote it by the same symbol
\chi(V)\in\pi_{0}E[V]^{G}
.

(8)

For
G=\mathcal{G}(n)
with
\mathcal{G}
being one of
U,SU,O,Sp,Spin,SO
, we denote by
V_{G}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
its fundamental (a.k.a., defining, or vector) representation.

(9)

For a space
X
, we denote by
X\to P^{n}X
the
n
-th Postnikov truncation, and by
X\langle n\rangle\to X
the
n
-connected cover.
In particular, the Whitehead towers of
BU
and
BO
are in low degrees related as follows.

\textstyle{BU\langle 6\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\langle 8\rangle=BString\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BU\langle 4\rangle=BSU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\langle 4\rangle=BSpin\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\langle 2\rangle=BSO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO.}

(10)

For an element
\tau\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, we denote by
\mathrm{tw}(\tau)
the map

(1.49)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tw}(\tau):=\dim\tau+\left(BG\xrightarrow{\overline{\tau}}BO\to P^{4}BO\right)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathrm{Map}(BG,P^{4}BO).

We also abuse the notation to denote by
\mathrm{tw}(\tau)
its homotopy class in
\mathbb{Z}\times[BG,P^{4}BO]
. The notation comes from the fact that
\mathrm{tw}(\tau)
is understood as twists of
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
associated to
\tau\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, as explained in Section
2.3.1
.

(11)

In a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
, suppose we have objects
a,b,c,d,x
and morphisms
f\colon x\to a\otimes b
,
g\colon x\to c\otimes d
,
h\colon a\to c
and
k\colon d\to b
. We say that the diagram

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{b}
\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}

is
compatible
if the square

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{a\otimes b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{b}}
\textstyle{c\otimes d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{c}\otimes k}
\textstyle{c\otimes b}

commutes.

(12)

Given a space
X
with a real vector bundle
\theta
, we denote the associated Thom spectrum by

(1.58)

\displaystyle X^{\theta}:=\Sigma^{\infty}\mathrm{Thom}(\theta\to X)\in\mathrm{Spectra}.

More generally, this notation allows
\theta
to be a virtual vector bundle, e.g.,
[
ABG18
]
.

(13)

In this article, it is important to distinguish between tangential and normal bordism Thom spectra. Given a space
\mathcal{B}
with a map
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to BO
,

(1.59)

\displaystyle M(\mathcal{B},f)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}^{f},

(1.60)

\displaystyle MT(\mathcal{B},f)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}^{-f},

where we identify
f
with a virtual real vector bundle of rank
0
over
\mathcal{B}
. The spectra
M(\mathcal{B},f)
and
MT(\mathcal{B},f)
classify the bordism (co)homology theories of manifolds with
normal
and
tangential

(\mathcal{B},f)
-structures. The details are explained in Section
2.4
.
When
f
is canonically understood, we often omit it from the notation and write, e.g.,
MSU(k)
and
MTSU(m)
.

(14)

For an
E_{\infty}
ring spectrum
R
, we denote by
u\colon S\to R
the unit map.

(15)

Let
R
be an
E_{\infty}
ring spectrum. For a dualizable object
x\in\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
, we denote by
D_{R}(x)
its dual in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
. In this article, we mostly use this notation for
R=\mathrm{TMF}
, so we adopt the shorthand
D:=D_{\mathrm{TMF}}
.

(16)

For a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded abelian group
A
and an integer
m
, we denote by
A|_{\deg=m}
the degree-
m
component of
A
.

(17)

We use the following convention on modular forms.
We denote by

\mathrm{MF}:=\mathbb{Z}[c_{4},c_{6},\Delta,\Delta^{-1}]/(c_{4}^{3}-c_{6}^{2}-1728\Delta)

the ring of weakly-holomorphic integral modular forms (i.e., holomorphic away from the cusps and having integral Fourier coefficients in the variable
q=\exp(2\pi i\tau)
).
In the text, we capitalize “Modular Forms” to mean weakly holomorphic modular forms.
We put the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring structure so that
\mathrm{MF}|_{\deg=m}
consists of those of weight
\frac{m}{2}
. This way we have a canonical map

(1.61)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{MF}}\colon\pi_{m}\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{MF}|_{\deg=m}.

Holomorphic
modular forms (holomorphic also at the cusps) figure in Section
7.2.2
. We denote by

\mathrm{mf}:=\mathbb{Z}[c_{4},c_{6},\Delta]/(c_{4}^{3}-c_{6}^{2}-1728\Delta)

the corresponding graded ring.

(18)

We use the convention on Jacobi forms following, e.g.,
[
DMZ12
,
GW20
]
.
We denote by
\mathfrak{H}:=\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathrm{Im}(\tau)>0\}
the upper half space of the complex plane.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
w\in\mathbb{Z}
, consider holomorphic functions of
(z,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathfrak{H}
satisfying the transformation properties (c.f., Definition
2.49
),

(1.62)

\displaystyle\phi\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{z}{c\tau+d}\right)

\displaystyle=(c\tau+d)^{w}e^{\frac{\pi ikcz^{2}}{c\tau+d}}\phi(\tau,z),

(1.63)

\displaystyle\phi(\tau,z+\lambda\tau+\mu)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi ik(\lambda^{2}\tau+2\lambda z)}\phi(\tau,z)

for all
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
and
(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and having Fourier expansions

(1.64)

\displaystyle\phi(q,y)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{k}{2}}\sum_{n\geq N}c(n,r)q^{n}y^{r}

where
(q,y)=(\exp(2\pi i\tau),\exp(2\pi iz))
for some integer
N
.

•

Such functions are called
weakly holomorphic
Jacobi forms of index
\frac{k}{2}
and weight
w
. We mostly deal with this type of Jacobi forms in this paper.

•

If
c(n,r)\neq 0
only when
n\geq 0
, then such functions are called
weak
Jacobi forms. This type of Jacobi forms only appear in Section
7.2.2
.

•

In addition, if
c(n,r)\neq 0
only when
r^{2}\geq 4kn
, then such functions are called
holomorphic
Jacobi forms. But in this paper we do not talk about this type of Jacobi forms.

•

If all
c(n,r)\in\mathbb{Z}
, we add the adjective
integral
in all the above cases.

In the text, we capitalize the first letters in “Jacobi Forms” to mean
weakly holomorphic Jacobi forms
, and denote by
\mathrm{JF}_{k}
the set of all integral Jacobi Forms with index
\frac{k}{2}
. We put the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on
\mathrm{JF}_{k}
so that
\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=m}
consists of Jacobi Forms with weight
w=-k+\frac{m}{2}
. This makes
\mathrm{JF}_{k}
a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded module over the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring
\mathrm{MF}
. As will be recalled in Section
A.3
, we have a canonical map

(1.65)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{JF}}\colon\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}=\pi_{m}\Gamma(\mathcal{E};\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke))\to\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=m}.

Weak
Jacobi forms figure in Section
7.2.2
. We denote by
\mathrm{jF}_{k}
the
\mathrm{mf}
-submodule consisting weak Jacobi forms, i.e.,

(1.66)

\displaystyle\mathrm{jF}_{k}:=\mathrm{JF}_{k}\cap\mathbb{Z}((y))[[q]].

(19)

For notational ease, we write

(1.67)

\displaystyle a=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{\theta_{11}(z,q)}{\eta^{3}(q)}=(e^{\pi iz}-e^{-\pi iz})\prod_{m\geq 1}\frac{(1-q^{m}e^{2\pi iz})(1-q^{m}e^{-2\pi iz})}{(1-q^{m})^{2}}.

This is an element in
\mathrm{JF}_{1}|_{\deg=0}
and a generator of the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring
\oplus_{k}\mathrm{JF}_{k}
of Jacobi Forms (
A.58
); the notation
\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}
is employed in
[
Gri99
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Generalities on genuinely equivariant spectra

Equivariant stable homotopy theory is an expansive subject, and there are various realizations of the equivariant stable homotopy category, e.g., those based on orthogonal spectra and those based on orbispaces. We refer to
[
GM23
, Appendix C]
for a nice account of those formulations and relations.
However, in this paper, we only need the basic structure of the equivariant stable homotopy category, and this section is aimed at giving a minimal account of what we need in this paper and setting up the notation.
Practically, we employ the definition
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}:=\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathcal{U}}^{G}
in
[
GM23
, Definition C.1]
, and call it the
\infty
-category of genuinely
G
-equivariant spectra. This is based on orbispaces, but it was shown in
[
GM23
, Appendix C.2]
that they are equivalent to the more classical definition based on orthogonal spectra.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
be the
\infty
-category of pointed
G
-spaces and
G
-equivariant maps, where equivalence is given by maps
f\colon X\to Y
that induce a weak equivalence on the fixed points
f\colon X^{H}\simeq Y^{H}
for all subgroups
H\subset G
.
\mathcal{S}^{G}
is a symmetric monoidal category with the smash product
\wedge
.
In particular, we have
S^{V}\in\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
for all orthogonal representations
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
.
Informally speaking, the stable
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
is obtained by formally inverting the operation
S^{V}\wedge-
on
\mathcal{S}^{G}
. The symmetric monoidal structure
\wedge
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
extends to a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
, which we denote by
\otimes
.
In particular, we have a symmetric monoidal functor

(2.1)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\infty}\colon\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}\to\mathrm{Spectra}^{G},

which preserves colimits
5
5

5

However, it does not preserve limits. So a
cofiber
sequence
X\to Y\to Z
in
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
produces a fiber=cofiber sequence

(2.2)

\displaystyle E\otimes X\to

\displaystyle E\otimes Y\to E\otimes Z,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(Z,E)\to

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(Y,E)\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(X,E)

in
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
for each
E\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
, but we could not have started from a fiber sequence in
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
.

. We abuse the notation to denote
E\otimes X:=E\otimes\Sigma^{\infty}X
for
E\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
and
X\in\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
.
The category
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
has internal homs, which we denote by

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(X,Y)\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}

for
X,Y\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}.

There are several notions of fixed point spectra for an equivariant spectrum
E\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
, and in this paper, we use the
genuine
fixed point spectra, denoted by
E^{G}\in\mathrm{Spectra}
. This assignment gives a functor of stable
\infty
-categories

(2.4)

\displaystyle(-)^{G}\colon\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}\to\mathrm{Spectra},\quad E\to E^{G}.

From this, we get the classical notion of
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
-graded equivariant (co)homology groups as follows.
For a virtual orthogonal representation
\tau\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, we denote by
S^{\tau}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
the virtual representation sphere spectrum.
6
6

6

In particular, we abuse the notation to denote
\Sigma^{\infty}S^{V}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
by
S^{V}
when
V
is a orthogonal representation.

Given another genuinely
G
-equivariant spectrum
X\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
, its
G
-equivariant
E
-cohomology groups and homology groups with degree
\tau\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
are defined as

(2.5)

\displaystyle E^{\tau}_{G}(X)

\displaystyle:=\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(X,E\otimes S^{\tau})^{G}=\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(X,E[\tau])^{G},

(2.6)

\displaystyle E_{\tau}^{G}(X)

\displaystyle:=\pi_{0}(X\otimes E\otimes S^{-\tau})^{G}=\pi_{0}(X\otimes E[-\tau])^{G},

respectively, where we have employed the notation
E[\tau]:=E\otimes S^{\tau}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
as in (
1.35
).

Another important ingredient is the
norm map
between homotopy orbit and genuine fixed points. Let us denote by
\mathrm{Ad}G\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
the adjoint representation of
G
. For each
E\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
, the norm map is a morphism in
\mathrm{Spectra}
defined as

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Nm}\colon E_{hG}\simeq(EG_{+}\otimes E[-\mathrm{Ad}G])^{G}\xrightarrow{EG_{+}\to S^{0}}E[-\mathrm{Ad}G]^{G},

where
E_{hG}
is the homotopy orbit spectrum and the first equivalence is the Adams isomorphism.

Finally, let us introduce notions related to the change of groups. Given a homomorphism of compact Lie groups
f\colon H\to G
, we have a restriction functor

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{f}\colon\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}\to\mathrm{Spectra}^{H}.

Moreover, if
f
is an inclusion of a subgroup
f\colon H\hookrightarrow G
, we often denote the restriction by
\mathrm{res}_{G}^{H}
. In this case,
\mathrm{res}_{G}^{H}
admits both the left and right adjoints. We denote the left adjoint by
\mathrm{ind}_{H}^{G}
and also use the suggestive notation
\mathrm{ind}_{H}^{G}E=E\wedge_{H}G_{+}
. By the Wirthmüller isomorphism, we get the transfer map (only along an inclusion of closed subgroups!)

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{H}^{G}\colon\left(\mathrm{res}_{G}^{H}(E)[-\mathrm{Ad}H]\right)^{H}\to E[-\mathrm{Ad}G]^{G}

2.2.
Equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
and their twists

In this subsection, we summarize the theory of genuinely equivariant elliptic cohomology developed by Gepner and Meier
[
GM23
]
.
They refine elliptic cohomology theory, in particular
\mathrm{TMF}
, to a globally equivariant spectrum, in the sense that
\mathrm{TMF}
is refined to objects in
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
for all compact Lie groups
G
all at once, functorially in
G
.
First, we briefly summarize their construction in Section
2.2.1
, and then we relate it with the more elementary complex analytic story in Section
2.2.2
.

Remark 2.10
.

The Gepner-Meier work is based on spectral algebraic geometry, so Section
2.2.1
below necessarily involves that language. However, we do not assume the reader to have
any
knowledge of spectral algebraic geometry at all; all we need in this paper is the consequence of the Gepner-Meier construction, that we obtain a genuinely equivariant refinement of
\mathrm{TMF}
with nice dualizability properties, as summarized below.

2.2.1.
The construction of Gepner-Meier
[
GM23
]

For details of the following content, we refer to the original paper
[
GM23
]
.
As developed in the works of Lurie
[
Lura
,
Lurb
,
Lurc
]
, spectral algebraic geometry gives a conceptual framework of elliptic cohomology.
Given a preoriented spectral elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
over a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack
\mathcal{M}
(the term “spectral algebraic” is henceforth often omitted), the associated
elliptic spectrum
is simply defined as

(2.11)

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{E}}:=\Gamma(\mathcal{M};\mathcal{O})\in\mathrm{CAlg},

the global section of the structure sheaf of the moduli
\mathcal{M}
.
In particular, if we apply it to the universal elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{uinv}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uinv}}
, we get the spectrum of Topological Modular Forms,
\mathrm{TMF}:=R_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{uinv}}}=\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uinv}};\mathcal{O})
.

Gepner and Meier’s work refines the elliptic spectrum (
2.11
) into a globally equivariant
E_{\infty}
-spectrum, as follows. Their main construction is the
equivariant elliptic cohomology functor

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ell}\colon\mathcal{S}_{\rm Orb}\to\mathrm{Shv}(\mathcal{M}),

for each
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{\rm Orb}
is the category of orbispaces regarded as a setting of globally equivariant homotopy theory.
The category
\mathcal{S}_{\rm Orb}
includes the object
\mathbf{B}G=[*//G]
(see Section
1.1
(
4
)) for each compact Lie group
G
, and the functor (
2.12
) is defined so that
\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)
is regarded as a spectral algebraic counterpart of the complex analytic moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}
(see (
2.44
) below) of flat
G
-bundles on dual elliptic curves; namely, we have a canonical identification
\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}A)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}(\widehat{A},\mathcal{E})
for each compact abelian Lie group
A
with its Pontryagin dual
\widehat{A}
, so in particular

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}U(1))\simeq\mathcal{E},\quad\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}C_{n})\simeq\mathcal{E}[n]

where
\mathcal{E}[n]\subset\mathcal{E}
is the
n
-torsion of elliptic curves, and the functor (
2.12
) is given by the left Kan extension from the above cases.

For each compact Lie group
G
, we have the Yoneda inclusion functor
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Orb}}{}_{/\mathbf{B}G}
. Precomposing this with the functor
\mathrm{Ell}
, we get a colimit-preserving functor

(2.14)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}ll}_{G}\colon\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}\to\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G))^{\mathrm{op}}.

We further compose with the functor
\Gamma
taking the global sections to get a colimit-preserving functor

(2.15)

\displaystyle\Gamma\widetilde{\mathcal{E}ll}_{G}\colon\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}\to\mathrm{Spectra}^{\mathrm{op}},\quad X\mapsto\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G);\widetilde{\mathcal{E}ll}_{G}(X))\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{M};\mathrm{Ell}(X//G)).

Furthermore, they show that the functor (
2.15
) is represented by a genuine
G
-spectrum, also denoted by
R_{\mathcal{E}}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
in a way that is functorial in
G
. This means that we have canonical identifications

(2.16)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(X,R_{\mathcal{E}})^{G}\simeq\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G);\widetilde{\mathcal{E}ll}_{G}(X))\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{M};\mathrm{Ell}(X//G)),

for each
X\in\mathcal{S}^{G}_{*}
so that the equivariant cohomology group is identified as

(2.17)

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{E},G}^{*}(X)\simeq\pi_{-*}\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G);\widetilde{\mathcal{E}ll}_{G}(X))\simeq\pi_{-*}\Gamma(\mathcal{M};\mathrm{Ell}(X//G)).

In particular, we have

(2.18)

\displaystyle(R_{\mathcal{E}})^{G}\simeq\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G);\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)})\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{M};\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)).

This gives the desired globally equivariant refinement of
R_{\mathcal{E}}
in (
2.12
).

For each orthogonal representation
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
of
G
, we set

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(V):=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}ll}_{G}(S^{V})\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)):=\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G))^{\times},

which is shown to be the
invertible
elements in
\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G))
. This allows us to more generally denote, for each virtual representation
V=W_{1}-W_{2}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
with
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(V):=\mathcal{L}(W_{1})\otimes\mathcal{L}(W_{2})^{-1}.

We get

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}:=(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes S^{V})^{G}=\mathrm{TMF}(S^{-V})^{G}=\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G);\mathcal{L}(-V)).

If
G,H\in\mathrm{cptLie}
with
V_{G}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
and
V_{H}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(H)
, we have an isomorphism of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules
[
GM
]
,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}\left[\mathrm{res}_{G}^{G\times H}V_{G}\oplus\mathrm{res}_{H}^{G\times H}V_{H}\right]^{G\times H}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[V_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{H}]^{H}.

Remark 2.23
.

The isomorphism (
2.22
) does not necessarily mean that we have the corresponding tensor product decomposition of homotopy groups. Rather, the computation of the homotopy group of (
2.22
) involves the Kunneth spectral sequence.
But
rationally
, it is often true that
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V_{G}]^{G}
is flat over
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
This is true for
\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V]^{U(1)^{n}}
for arbitrary
n
and
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(1)^{n})
, as well as for
\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}
and
\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[kV_{SU(n)}]^{SU(n)}
for arbitrary
n
and
k
, for example
7
7

7

The outline of the proof is as follows.
The
G=U(1)
-case follows because
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is free over
\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}
for any
k
. This implies by (
2.24
) that the
\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-module of the form
\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[W_{1}\oplus W_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus W_{n}]^{U(1)^{n}}
also satisfies this property where
W_{i}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(1))
. For general
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(1))
, we use Fact
2.104
and the classification of bilinear forms on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by Witt groups to reduce the previous diagonal case.
The case for
G=SU(n)
and
G=U(n)
follows from the
U(1)^{n}
-case by taking the Weyl group invariant part.

.
In such cases, we have

(2.24)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\mathrm{res}_{G}^{G\times H}V_{G}\oplus\mathrm{res}_{H}^{G\times H}V_{H}\right]^{G\times H}\simeq\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V_{H}]^{H}

\displaystyle\mbox{if }\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V_{G}]^{G}\mbox{ or }\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V_{H}]^{H}\mbox{ is flat over }\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}

Example 2.25

(
G=U(1)
: Topological Jacobi Forms)
.

The case of
G=U(1)
is fundamental, and plays an important role in this paper. It is called
Topological Jacobi Forms
and studied in detail in an upcoming paper by Bauer-Meier
[
BM25
]
, and we have summarized the necessary results in Appendix
A
. In this paper, we employ the definition (Definition
A.1
) that, for each integer
k
,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k}:=\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{E};\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke)),

where we have used
\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}U(1))\simeq\mathcal{E}
(
2.13
) and the fact that
\mathcal{L}(-kV_{U(1)})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(e)^{\otimes k}\in\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathcal{E})^{\times}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(e)
is the (SAG-version of the) sheaf of meromorphic functions on
\mathcal{E}
having pole of order at most
1
at the zero section
e\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{E}
.
As explained below and in more detail in Appendix
A
,
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
is regarded as a spectral refinement of the module of integral Jacobi Forms of index
k/2
.

Example 2.27

(
G=Sp(1)
: Topological Even Jacobi Forms)
.

The case of
G=Sp(1)
is also of particular importance for us. The twisted
Sp(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
is surprisingly nicely understood, and we give a detailed account in Appendix
B
.
We employ the notation (Definition
B.2
)

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}:=\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}

for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and call it
Topological Even Jacobi Forms
, by the reason explained in Example
2.69
below and in more detail in Appendix
B
.

An important feature of the genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
is the following dualizability statement:

Fact 2.29

(Dualizability of
\mathrm{TMF}^{G}

[
GM
]
)
.

For any compact Lie group
G
,
\mathrm{TMF}^{G}
is dualizable in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
, with its dual (see Section
1.1
(
15
)) canonically identified as

D(\mathrm{TMF}^{G})\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}(G)]^{G}
.

We remark that this is a special feature of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
; indeed, for example in the case of genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{KU}
-theory (with the usual equivariance), this dualizability does not hold.
This allows us to define, for
any
homomorphism
f\colon G\to H
of compact Lie groups, the
transfer map along
f
,

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{f}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}(G)]^{G}\to\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}(H)]^{H}

to be the dual of the restriction map

\mathrm{res}_{f}\colon\mathrm{TMF}^{H}\to\mathrm{TMF}^{G}
.
This extends the transfer map along inclusions
G\hookrightarrow H
in (
2.9
), which exists for any genuinely
H
-equivariant spectra. The existence of this general transfer is a special feature of the genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
8
8

8

As we will explain in detail in Part II of this series of the papers, these transfer maps should correspond to
gauging
in quantum field theories.
.

We also note that, for every
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
,
\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}
is also dualizable in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
whose dual is identified as

(2.31)

\displaystyle D(\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G})\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[-V-\mathrm{Ad}(G)]^{G},

by the coevaluation being

(2.32)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}\otimes\mathrm{TMF}[-V-\mathrm{Ad}(G)]^{G}\xrightarrow{\rm multi}\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}(G)]^{G}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}_{G}^{e}}\mathrm{TMF}.

Finally, let us remark on the Atiyah-Segal completion in this context. We have an adjunction (upper=left adjoint)

\textstyle{|\bullet|\colon\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Orb}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{X}\mapsto|\mathsf{X}|}
\scriptstyle{\perp}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\colon y}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Map}(\bullet,Y)\leftarrow Y}

For example, we have
|\mathbf{B}G|\simeq BG
for
G\in\mathrm{cptLie}
.
We recover the usual
\mathrm{TMF}
-cohomology from the equivariant elliptic cohomology functor
\mathrm{Ell}
in (
2.12
) by the fact that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{Y\mapsto\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(\Sigma^{\infty}Y,\mathrm{TMF})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Orb}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ell}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Shv}(\mathcal{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}}

Let us denote the unit of the adjunction (
2.2.1
) by

(2.39)

\displaystyle u_{\mathsf{X}}\colon\mathsf{X}\to y(|\mathsf{X}|)

for each
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{Orb}}
. Then we get, for each pointed
G
-space
X\in\mathcal{S}_{*}^{G}
, the map

(2.40)

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle\colon\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}X,\mathrm{TMF})^{G}\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{M},\mathrm{Ell}(X//G))

(2.41)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{u_{X//G}}\Gamma(\mathcal{M},\mathrm{Ell}(y(|X//G|)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{diag_Ell_TMF}}}{{\simeq}}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(\Sigma^{\infty}X\wedge_{G}EG_{+},\mathrm{TMF})\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}X,\mathrm{TMF})^{hG}

This coincides with the canonical map from the genuine to homotopy fixed points and is regarded as a generalization of the Atiyah-Segal completion map. In particular, we get the following map of the homotopy groups.

(2.42)

\displaystyle\zeta\colon\mathrm{TMF}_{G}^{*}(X)\to\mathrm{TMF}^{*}(X\wedge_{G}EG_{+}).

2.2.2.
Specialization to elliptic curves over
\mathbb{C}

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the classical Deligne-Mumford stack of elliptic curves over
\mathbb{C}
, and
p\colon\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the universal elliptic curve over it. We use the usual identification (where we use the notation
\mathfrak{H}:=\{\tau\mid\mathrm{Im}(\tau)>0\}
)

(2.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\mathfrak{H}//SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),\quad\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq(\mathbb{C}\times\mathfrak{H})//(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rtimes SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})).

For
G
connected and
\pi_{1}G
torsion-free, we have an identification
[
GM
]
,

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}\simeq\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)^{{\heartsuit}}_{\mathbb{C}},

where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}
is the moduli stack of flat
G
-bundles over the dual elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and
\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)^{{\heartsuit}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the underlying Deligne-Mumford stack of
\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G)
after taking
\mathbb{C}
-points. So a virtual representation
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
produces a line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V):=\mathcal{L}(-V)_{\mathbb{C}}^{\heartsuit}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G})
.
By the functoriality of the Gepner-Meier construction, we have a canonical map

(2.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{red}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V)\otimes p^{*}\omega^{\bullet/2}).

In the case where
G
is connected and
\pi_{1}G
is torsion-free
9
9

9

This condition is sufficient for the identification (
2.47
) to hold.
, the right hand side of (
2.45
) can be nicely understood in terms of multivalued Jacobi Forms as follows.
For each compact connected
abelian
Lie group
T
, we have a canonical identification

(2.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Hom}(S^{1},T),

and an identification
T\simeq U(1)^{r}
gives the corresponding identification
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}\simeq(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\times r}
where the product is taken over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Furthermore, for each connected compact Lie group
G
with
\pi_{1}G
being torsion free, choosing a maximal torus
T\subset G
with the Weyl group
W
, we have a canonical identification

(2.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}/W\quad\mbox{ for }G\mbox{ connected, }\pi_{1}G\mbox{ torsion-free}

This allows us to identify sections of sheaves over
\mathcal{M}^{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
in terms of those over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}
. In particular, given
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
we have a canonical identification

(2.48)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V))\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T};\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-\mathrm{res}_{G}^{T}V))^{W}.

In this setting,
The line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
is related to the line bundles constructed by Looijenga and its generalization
[
GKMP25
]
:

Definition 2.49

(Looijenga’s line bundle
\mathcal{A}(\xi)

[
GKMP25
]
)
.

(1)

For each nonnegative integer
r
, we have a canonical bijection

(2.50)

\displaystyle[BU(1)^{r},P^{4}BO]\simeq\left\{b(-,-)\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}:\mbox{symmetric bilinear form}\right\}

(2)

For each element
\xi\in[BU(1)^{r},P^{4}BO]
we define the Looijenga’s line bundle
\mathcal{A}(\xi)
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{U(1)^{r}}\simeq\mathcal{E}^{\times r}_{\mathbb{C}}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times r}\times\mathfrak{H})//((\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{r}\rtimes SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}))
by

(2.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\xi):=\mathbb{C}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times r}\times\mathfrak{H})//((\mathbb{Z}^{r})^{2}\rtimes SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})),

where
\mathfrak{H}
is the upper half plane and the action is given by (we use the coordinates
z=(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{r})\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
and
u\in\mathbb{C}
)

(2.52)

\displaystyle A\cdot(u,z,\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(e^{\pi i(c(c\tau+d)^{-1}\xi(z,z))}u,(c\tau+d)^{-1}z,\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right),

(2.53)

\displaystyle(m_{1},m_{2})\cdot(u,z,\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(e^{-2\pi i(\xi(z,m_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\xi(m_{1},m_{1}))}u,z+m_{1}+m_{2},\tau\right),

for each
A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
(m_{1},m_{2})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{r})^{2}
.
Here we have denoted by
\xi(-,-)\colon\mathbb{C}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{r}\to\mathbb{C}
the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of the symmetric bilinear form on
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
corresponding to
\xi
by the bijection (
2.50
).

(3)

More generally, let
G
be a connected compact Lie group with
\pi_{1}G
torsion free. Choose a maximal torus
\iota\colon U(1)^{r}\hookrightarrow G
with the Weyl group
W
, and identify
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}/W
.
Given an element
\xi\in[BG,P^{4}BO]
, we define the line bundle
\mathcal{A}(\xi)
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}
by the following:
Consider the line bundle
\mathcal{A}(\iota^{*}\xi)
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{U(1)^{r}}
constructed in (2), and observe that the
W
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{U(1)^{r}}
naturally lifts to
\mathcal{A}(\iota^{*}\xi)
. Thus it descends to a line bundle
\mathcal{A}(\xi)/W
on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{A}(\xi)
.
10
10

10

We note that the definition does not depend on the choice of the maximal torus, in the sense that, any two different maximal torus are conjugate to each other, and a choice of a conjugating element associates isomorphism of the line bundle constructed.

(4)

We also extend the notation to denote, given
n+\xi:=(n,\xi)\in\mathbb{Z}\times[BG,P^{4}B\mathrm{O}]
,

(2.54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(n+\xi):=\mathcal{A}(\xi)\otimes p^{*}\omega^{-\frac{n}{2}},

where
p\colon\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the projection and
\omega
is the cotangent sheaf on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
For a virtual representation,
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
we denote
\mathcal{A}(V):=\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{tw}(V))
, where
\mathrm{tw}(V)
is defined in Section
1.1
(
10
)

This means that a holomorphic section
\phi\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times r},\mathcal{A}(\xi))
can be written as a multivariable function
\phi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},\tau)
with
(z_{1},\cdots,z_{r},\tau)\in\mathbb{C}^{r}\times\mathfrak{H}
and the transformation rule induced by (
2.51
) + the weight factor appearing in the definition of Modular Forms.
We also use the coordinate
(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{r},q)
with
y_{a}:=e^{2\pi iz_{a}}
and
q:=e^{2\pi i\tau}
interchangeably.
A holomorphic section
\phi\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{A}(\xi))\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times r};\mathcal{A}(\iota^{*}\xi))^{W}
is expressed as those
\phi(\bm{z},\tau)
that are additionally invariant under the action of
W
.

Definition 2.55

(multi-variable Jacobi Forms and
G
-equivariant Modular Forms)
.

(1)

Let
r
be a positive integer. Given a class
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}\times[BU(1)^{r},P^{4}BO]
, we define a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathrm{MF}
-module
\mathrm{MF}[{\xi}]^{U(1)^{r}}
by setting

(2.56)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{MF}[{{\xi}}]^{U(1)^{r}}\right)_{\deg=m}:=\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{A}(-m+\xi))\cap\mathbb{Z}((y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{r},q)),

for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Here we have used the coordinates
y_{a}:=e^{2\pi iz_{a}}
and
q:=e^{2\pi i\tau}
as above.
In the case
r=1
, we also denote

(2.57)

\displaystyle\mathrm{JF}_{k}:=\mathrm{MF}[\mathrm{tw}(kV_{U(1)})]^{U(1)}=\mathrm{MF}[2k+k\xi_{U(1)}]^{U(1)},

where
\xi_{U(1)}\in[BU(1),P^{4}BO]\simeq\mathbb{Z}
is the generator represented by the (normalized) fundamental representation
\overline{V}_{U(1)}
.
Following the usual convention, we call an element in
\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=m}=\left(\mathrm{MF}[k\xi_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\right)_{\deg=m-2k}
an integral Jacobi Form of index
\frac{k}{2}
and weight
\frac{m}{2}-k
.

(2)

Let
G
be a compact connected Lie group with
\pi_{1}G
torsion-free. Choose a maximal torus
\iota\colon U(1)^{r}\hookrightarrow G
with the Weyl group
W
.
Given an element
\xi\in[BG,P^{4}BO]
, we define a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathrm{MF}
-module
\mathrm{MF}[{\xi}]^{G}
by setting

(2.58)

\displaystyle\mathrm{MF}[\xi]^{G}:=\left(\mathrm{MF}[\iota^{*}\xi]^{U(1)^{r}}\right)^{W}\subset\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{A}(\xi)),

where
\left(-\right)^{W}
means the
W
-invariant part.
For
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, we also denote
\mathrm{MF}[V]^{G}:=\mathrm{MF}[\mathrm{tw}(V)]^{G}
.
We call an element in
\mathrm{MF}[\xi]^{G}
an integral
G
-equivariant
\xi
-twisted Modular Form.

The relation between
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V)
and
\mathcal{A}(V)
is the following.

Lemma 2.59

(
[
AG07
]
and
[
GKMP25
]
)
.

For each compact connected
G
with
\pi_{1}G
torsion-free and
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, we have an isomorphism

(2.60)

\displaystyle\Phi_{V}\cdot\colon\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V)\simeq\mathcal{A}(V)\mbox{ in }\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}),

equivalently an invertible holomorphic section
\Phi_{V}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)\otimes\mathcal{A}(V))^{\times}
, characterized by the following properties:

•

Functorial in
(G,V)
.

•

compatible with the monoidal structure in
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
.

•

In the case
G=U(1)
and
V=V_{U(1)}
, the section
\Phi_{V_{U(1)}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}}(-e)\otimes\mathcal{A}(V_{U(1)}))^{\times}
is given by the Jacobi theta function as

(2.61)

\displaystyle\Phi_{V_{U(1)}}=a=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{\theta_{11}(z,q)}{\eta^{3}(q)}=(e^{\pi iz}-e^{-\pi iz})\prod_{m\geq 1}\frac{(1-q^{m}e^{2\pi iz})(1-q^{m}e^{-2\pi iz})}{(1-q^{m})^{2}}.

Here the notation
a
follows our shorthand notation introduced in (
1.67
).

The map (
2.45
) factors through integral
G
-equivariant Modular Forms as

(2.62)

\displaystyle\mathrm{red}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}\xrightarrow{e}\left(\mathrm{MF}[V]^{G}\right)|_{\deg=\bullet}\subset\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{A}(V)\otimes p^{*}\omega^{\bullet/2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{V}}}{{\simeq}}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V)\otimes p^{*}\omega^{\bullet/2}).

We call the first map
e
as the
G
-equivariant character map
.

Remark 2.63

(The relation between Euler class
\chi(V)\in\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}
and
\Phi_{V}
)
.

In the case where
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G)
, i.e.,
V
is not virtual but a genuine representation, we have a natural map
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}}
in
\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G})
by applying the
G
-equivariant elliptic cohomology functor (
2.14
) to the map
\chi(V)\colon S^{0}\hookrightarrow S^{V}
.
We abuse the notation to also denote by
\Phi_{V}\in\mathrm{MF}[V]^{G}\subset\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G};\mathcal{A}(V))
the section corresponding to the composition

(2.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{G}}\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(-V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_theta_V}}}{{\simeq}}\mathcal{A}(V)

For example, we regard
\Phi_{V_{U(1)}}=a\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{JF}_{1}
. In general,
\Phi_{V}
is essentially the generalization of the Theta functions studied in
[
AG07
]
.
This means that the
G
-equivariant Euler class
\chi(V)\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}
in (
1.40
) satisfies

(2.65)

\displaystyle e(\chi(V))=\Phi_{V}.

The Euler class
\chi(V)
is of particular importance in our paper. Physically, it is supposed to correspond to “
G
-symmetric
V
-valued Majorana fermions”.

Example 2.66

(
G=U(n)
)
.

In the case of
G=U(1)
, the character map (
2.62
) becomes

(2.67)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{JF}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\to\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=\bullet},

which allows us to regard
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
as spectral refinement of
\mathrm{JF}_{k}
as promised in Example
2.25
.
More generally, for
G=U(n)
, we use the standard diagonal maximal torus
U(1)^{n}\xhookrightarrow{\rm diag}U(n)
with the Weyl group
W=\Sigma_{n}
, the symmetric group permuting the factors. So a
U(n)
-equivariant Modular Forms are expressed as
n
-variable Jacobi Forms
\phi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n},\tau)
which are symmetric in
z_{i}
.
For any nonnegative integer
k
, we have (see (
2.24
))

(2.68)

\displaystyle\mathrm{MF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\left(\bigotimes^{\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}}_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathrm{JF}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)^{\Sigma_{n}}

where the tensor product is formed over
\mathrm{MF}
.

Example 2.69

(
G=Sp(1)
: Even Jacobi Forms)
.

In the case of
G=Sp(1)=SU(2)
, we choose a maximal torus
T=U(1)\subset Sp(1)
. Then the Weyl group
W=\mathbb{Z}/2
acts on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
by the inverse involution of abelian varieties; in terms of the coordinate
(z,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathfrak{H}
, the involution becomes
(z,\tau)\mapsto(-z,\tau)
. Thus the
SU(2)
-equivariant Modular Forms are identified as the Jacobi Forms that are
even
in
z
; so we employ the following notation:

(2.70)

\displaystyle\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{MF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}=\{\phi(z,\tau)\in\mathrm{JF}_{2k}\ |\ \phi(z,\tau)=\phi(-z,\tau)\}.

See Appendix
B
for more detailed descriptions.
The
Sp(1)
-equivariant character map (
2.62
) becomes

(2.71)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{EJF}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny Def. }\ref{def_TEJF_app}}}{{:=}}\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}\to\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}|_{\deg=\bullet},

verifying our notation
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
.

Example 2.72

(
G=Sp(n)
)
.

More generally, in the case of
G=Sp(n)
, we choose the maximal torus to be
U(1)^{n}\xhookrightarrow{\rm diag}U(n)\hookrightarrow Sp(n)
, the image of the standard maximal torus of
U(n)
under the canonical inclusion
U(n)\hookrightarrow Sp(n)
.
Then the Weyl group is
W=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{n}\rtimes\Sigma_{n}
, where each
\mathbb{Z}/2
flips the sign of the coordinate
z_{i}\mapsto-z_{i}
and
\Sigma_{n}
permutes the factors.
Hence,
Sp(n)
-equivariant Modular Forms are regarded as
U(n)
-equivariant Modular Forms that are even in each variable
z_{i}
.

Example 2.73

(
G=SU(n)
)
.

For
G=SU(n)
, we follow the conventional approach that, rather than using the maximal torus of
SU(n)
, we first regard
SU(n)\subset U(n)
and use the maximal torus
U(1)^{n}\hookrightarrow U(n)
to identify

(2.74)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{SU(n)}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}^{U(n)}\cap\{z_{1}+z_{2}+\cdots+z_{n}=0\}.

This means that we have (see (
2.68
))

(2.75)

\displaystyle\mathrm{MF}[kV_{SU(n)}]^{SU(n)}\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\left(\frac{\bigotimes^{\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}}_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathrm{JF}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{Q}}{(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{n})}\right)^{\Sigma_{n}}

2.3.
Genuinely equivariant refinement of the sigma orientation

In
[
AHR10
]
, an
E_{\infty}
ring map

(2.76)

\displaystyle\sigma\colon\mathrm{MString}\to\mathrm{TMF}

was constructed and called the
sigma orientation
of
\mathrm{TMF}
.
In this article, we use an equivariant refinement of the sigma orientation which we now explain.
In order to state it, first let us set the notation. Let
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to BO
be a continuous map. Given a compact Lie group
G
with a virtual representation
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
, a
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure

\mathfrak{s}
on
V
is a lift of the classifying map
\overline{V}\colon BG\to BO
to
\mathcal{B}
along
f
11
11

11

It is equivalent to the stable
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure on the associated virtual vector bundle
EG\times_{G}V\to BG
in the sense of Definition
2.116
.
. We are particularly interested in
string structures
, which is classified by the map
\varrho\colon BString=BO\langle 8\rangle\to BO
.

First, recall the Thom isomorphism in
\mathrm{TMF}
induced by the usual sigma orientation. Consider the following map,

(2.77)

\displaystyle\mathrm{th}\colon\mathcal{S}_{/BO}\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}},\quad(\theta\colon X\to BO)\mapsto\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(X^{\theta},\mathrm{TMF}).

The sigma orientation (
2.76
) induces a natural isomorphism, also denoted by
\sigma
, in the following diagram,

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{/BString}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{fgt}=(BString\to\mathrm{pt})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X\mapsto\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(\Sigma^{\infty}X_{+},\mathrm{TMF})}

\Longrightarrow

\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{/BO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{th}}
(
2.77
)
\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}.}

This homotopy is equivalent to the data of the functorial assignment of the Thom isomorphism for string-oriented vector bundles.

Now we introduce our formulation of the sigma orientation for the genuinely equivariant setting.
For each compact Lie group
G
, recall that we have defined
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
to be the
groupoid
consisting of virtual orthogonal
G
-representations and isomorphisms. Let
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}(G)
denote the full subgroupoid consisting of those with virtual dimension
0
. Now define the groupoid
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0}(G)
to be the pullback,

\textstyle{\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Map}(BG,BString)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Map}(BG,BO).}

This gives us functors

(2.86)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0},\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}\colon\mathrm{cptLie}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Gpds},

where
\mathrm{Gpds}
is the category of groupoids.
For any subcategory
\mathfrak{C}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
, we can perform the Grothendieck construction,

(2.87)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0},\quad\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0}\in\mathrm{Cat}

The former is the groupoid whose objects are pairs
(G,V)
with
G\in\mathfrak{C}
and
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}(G)
, and morphism
(G,V)\to(H,W)
consists of a pair
(f,\psi)
where
f\colon G\to H
is a group homomorphism in
\mathfrak{C}
and
\psi\colon V\simeq\mathrm{res}_{f}W
in
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}(G)
.
The latter is the groupoid whose objects are triples
(G,V,\mathfrak{s})
where
\mathfrak{s}
is a string structure on
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}(G)
, and morphisms are
(f,\psi)
as above where
\psi
is required to be compatible with the string structures.

Definition 2.88

(A sigma orientation on a subcategory
\mathfrak{C}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
be a subcategory.
A sigma orientation on
\mathfrak{C}
is a natural isomorphism
\widetilde{\sigma}
in the following diagram of categories,

\textstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(G,V,\mathfrak{s})\mapsto G}
\scriptstyle{\varrho}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\mapsto\mathrm{TMF}^{G}=\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(BG),\mathcal{O})}

\Longrightarrow

\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\sigma}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{th}}
(
2.93
)
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}).}

where
\mathbf{th}
is defined by

(2.93)

\displaystyle\mathbf{th}\colon\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}},\quad(G,{V})\mapsto\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G),\mathcal{L}(V))\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{M},\mathrm{Ell}(S^{V}//G)).

We require it to be compatible with the natural isomorphism
\sigma
in (
2.3
) via the Atiyah-Segal completion map

in (

). More precisely, this condition is stated as follows. Consider the following diagram,

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{/BString}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{fgt}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X\mapsto\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(\Sigma^{\infty}X_{+},\mathrm{TMF})}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\textstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho}
\scriptstyle{(G,V,\mathfrak{s})\mapsto(BG\xrightarrow{V,\mathfrak{s}}BString)}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\Longrightarrow

\scriptstyle{\zeta}

\Longrightarrow

\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{G\mapsto BG}
\textstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}^{d=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{th}}
\scriptstyle{(G,V)\mapsto(BG\xrightarrow{V}BO)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{/BO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

.

Here the middle square is (
2.88
),
and the top and left square canonically commutes. The remaining two triangles are not commutative but equipped with the natural transformation by (
2.40
) as indicated.
We require that, the natural transformation between the two outer compositions
\int_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{String}^{d=0}\to\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}})
, obtained by composing the natural transformations in (
2.88
), conicides with the natural isomorphism obtained by composing the leftup arrow in (
