) with the natural isomorphism
\sigma
in (
2.3
).

Remark 2.102
.

The data of sigma orientation in the Definition
2.88
can be concretely understood as follows. For each element
G\in\mathfrak{S}
and each virtual representation
V\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
equipped with a string structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
V
, the equivalence of
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-module spectra,

(2.103)

\displaystyle\sigma(V,\mathfrak{s})\colon\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}.

is assigned (up to homotopy), and this assignment satisfies the following.

(1)

functoriality in
G\in\mathfrak{S}
.

(2)

compatiblility with the monoidal structure in
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
.

(3)

compatibility with the usual Thom isomorphism induced by the sigma orientation after the Atiyah-Segal completion.

The following statement is proved in an upcoming paper
[
MY
]
by L. Meier and the second author of this article.

Fact 2.104

(
[
MY
]
)
.

There exists a full subcategory
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
with a preferred string orientation (in the sense of Definition
2.88
), which satisfies

(1)

\mathfrak{S}
contains
U(1)^{n}
,
SU(n)
,
Sp(n)
and
U(n)
for all
n
.

(2)

\mathfrak{S}
is closed under taking finite products.

The authors expect the following conjecture to be true.

Conjecture 2.105

(Conjecture on equivariant sigma orientation)
.

There exists a sigma orientation on the whole category
\mathrm{cptLie}
(in the sense of Definition
2.88
). Moreover, there is a preferred choice of sigma orientation, which restricts to the sigma orientation on
\mathfrak{S}
supplied by Fact
2.104
.

The formalism of this paper works once for all we fix a sigma-oriented subcategory
\mathfrak{C}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
. We are basically based on the subcategory
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
with the sigma orientation given in Fact
2.104
, and derive mathematical results at the current status. However, we also would like to present the results we can get once we assume the whole establishment of the equivariant sigma orientation, Conjecture
2.105
; if we assume that, we can get rid of technical restrictions and get a complete and unified picture of our topological elliptic genera. Therefore, in this paper we put
shaded backgrounds
on the statements and proofs which depend on Conjecture
2.105
.

Remark 2.106
.

The authors believe that the difficulties which are currently preventing us from fully establishing the equivariant sigma orientation is only technical, and Conjecture
2.105
should be eventually proved. We plan to update this article as we progress on the equivariant sigma orientation, and hoping that we completely remove
the shade
soon.

2.3.1.
A remark on
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
-grading versus
G
-equivariant twists

In general, for genuinely
G
-equivariant commutative ring spectrum, the
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
-grading is naturally regarded as special cases of
G
-equivariant twists generally classified by
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{E})
. Namely, we have the map

(2.107)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)\to\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Mod}_{E}),\quad\tau\mapsto E\otimes S^{\tau}.

In the case of
E=\mathrm{TMF}
, non-equivariantly we have a map
[
ABG10
]

(2.108)

\displaystyle P^{4}BO\to BGL_{1}(\mathrm{TMF}),

which allows us to twist
\mathrm{TMF}
-comomology by a map to
P^{4}BO
.
It is widely expected, from mathematical point of views
[
Lur09
]
as well as physical point of views
[
TY23
, Appendix A]

[
LY
]
, that the twists by
P^{4}BO
canonically refines to the twists of genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
. More precisely we expect that there is a map
\mathfrak{t}_{G}\colon\mathrm{Map}(BG,P^{4}BO)\to\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G))
, functorial in
G
, which makes the following diagram commute

\textstyle{\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau\mapsto\mathcal{L}(V)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tw}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Ell}(\mathbf{B}G))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{\mathbf{B}G}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathrm{Map}(BG,P^{4}BO)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{t}_{G}}
[
ABG10
]
\textstyle{\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Ell}(BG))\simeq\mathrm{Map}(BG,\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{TMF}))}

Note that this claim is stronger than Conjecture
2.105
; indeed, Conjecture
2.105
follows by the commutativity of the diagram (
2.3.1
), but the existence of the map
\mathfrak{t}_{G}
implies that we can twist genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
by maps
BG\to P^{4}BO
which does not come from
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
.

Then, a natural question is how much of the expected twists come from
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
-grading. Fortunately, for
G=\mathbb{Z}/p,U(1)^{n},U(n),SU(n),Sp(n),O(n),SO(n),Spin(n)
, the map

(2.113)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tw}\colon\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)\to\mathbb{Z}\times[BG,P^{4}BO]

is surjective, so all the expected twists are realized by
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
-gradings up to equivalence.
On the other hand, for example in the case
G=E_{8}
, the map (
2.113
) is known to be non-surjective.

2.4.
On tangential and normal Thom spectra

In this article, it is important to distinguish
tangential
and
normal
bordism Thom spectra, and also to distinguish
stable
and
strict
=
unstable
structures, as we now explain. For a detailed account, we refer to
[
Fre19
, Section 6.6]
.
We follow the notation in Section
1.1
(
12
) to denote by
X^{\theta}
the Thom spectrum associated to a virtual vector bundle
\theta
over a space
X
.
As written in (
13
) there, for a map
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to BO
which is regarded as a virtual vector bundle with rank
0
, we denote

(2.114)

\displaystyle M(\mathcal{B},f):=\mathcal{B}^{f},\quad MT(\mathcal{B},f):=M(\mathcal{B},-f)=\mathcal{B}^{-f},

and call them the
normal Thom spectrum
and the
tangential Thom spectrum
, respectively. These notations are justified below.
When
\mathcal{B}
is of the form
\mathcal{B}=BH
with a compact Lie group
H
, we also use the conventional notation

(2.115)

\displaystyle M(G,f):=M(BG,f),\quad MT(G,f):=MT(BG,f).

We employ the following general definition of
stable
tangential and normal structures.

Definition 2.116

(stable (
\mathcal{B},f
)-structures and bordism groups)
.

Suppose we are given a space
\mathcal{B}
with a map
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to BO
.

•

For a space
X
with a virtual vector bundle
\theta
, a stable
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\theta
is a map of spectra
12
12

12

Note that giving a map
X^{\theta}\to\mathcal{B}^{f}
is equivalent to giving a map
X^{-\theta}\to\mathcal{B}^{-f}
since
BO
is an infinity loop space.
,

(2.117)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}\colon X^{\overline{\theta}}\to\mathcal{B}^{f}:=M(\mathcal{B},f).

•

For a manifold
M
with tangent bundle
TM
,

–

a stable tangential
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure is a
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure on
TM
.

–

a stable normal
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure is a stable
(\mathcal{B},-f)
-structure on
TM
, equivalently a
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure on
(-TM)
(see footnote
12
).

•

We denote by
\Omega^{(\mathcal{B},f)}_{m}
the bordism group of closed
m
-dimensional manifolds with stable tangential
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structures, and by
\Omega^{(\mathcal{B},f)^{\perp}}_{m}
the bordism group of those manifolds with stable normal
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structures.

We also utilize the notion of
strict
=
unstable
structures.
We employ the following definition.

Definition 2.118

(strict
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)
-structures)
.

Let
n
be a nonnegative integer, and suppose we are given a space
\mathcal{B}(d)
with a Serre fibration
f\colon\mathcal{B}(d)\to BO(d)
.

•

For a space
X
with a vector bundle
\theta
of real rank
n
, a strict
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)
-structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\theta
is a map
\mathfrak{s}\colon X\to\mathcal{B}(d)
which makes the following diagram commute.

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{s}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\theta}}
\textstyle{BO(d).}

•

For a manifold
M
with tangent bundle
TM
, A strict tangential
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)
-structure is a
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)
-structure on
TM
.
13
13

13

The existence of a strict tangential
(\mathcal{B}(d)),f)
-structure in particular implies that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=d
.

Of course, a strict
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)
-structure canonically induces a stable
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)
-structure, where we abuse the notation to denote by
f
the composition
\mathcal{B}(d)\xrightarrow{f}BO(d)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}BO
.

An important class of structures in this paper are those of the form
(\mathcal{B}(d),f)=(BH,{V})
, where
H
is a compact Lie group and
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(H)
is a real representation of dimension
d
, with induced map
\overline{V}\colon BH\to BO(d)
.
In this case, unpacking the above definition, we can concretely understand the
stable
tangential and normal structures as follows. Let
M
be an
m
-dimensional manifold.

•

A
stable
tangential
(BH,V)
-structure on
M
is represented by a pair
(P,\psi)
, where
P
is a principal
H
-bundle over
M
, and
\psi
is an isomorphism of vector bundles over
M
,

(2.123)

\displaystyle\psi\colon TM\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\simeq(P\times_{H}V)\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{m+N-d}

where
N
is a large enough integer.

•

A
stable
normal
(BH,V)
-structure on
M
is represented by a pair
(P,\psi)
, where
P
is a principal
H
-bundle over
M
, and
\psi
is an isomorphism of vector bundles over
M
,

(2.124)

\displaystyle\psi\colon TM\oplus(P\times_{H}V)\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{m+d+N}.

where
N
is a large enough integer.

•

A
strict
tangential
(BH,V)
-structure on
M
exists only when
m=d
, and is represented by a pair
(P,\psi)
, where
P
is a principal
H
-bundle over
M
, and
\psi
is an isomorphism of vector bundles over
M
,

(2.125)

\displaystyle\psi\colon TM\simeq(P\times_{H}V).

Notice that, by the above definition, it makes sense to talk about a stable tangential
SU(k)
-structure on an
m
-dimensional manifold with
2k<m
.

Important cases of
(H,V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(H))
come in series,
\{(\mathcal{H}(k),V_{\mathcal{H}(k)})\}_{k}
, where examples include
\mathcal{H}=U,SU,O,Sp,Spin
with their
fundamental
representations. For these cases, we simply call a tangential/normal
(B\mathcal{H}(k),\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})
-structure a tangential/normal
\mathcal{H}(k)
-structure, respectively, and denote

(2.126)

\displaystyle M\mathcal{H}(k):=M(\mathcal{H}(k),\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})=B\mathcal{H}(k)^{\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}},~~MT\mathcal{H}(k):=MT(\mathcal{H}(k),\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})=B\mathcal{H}(k)^{-\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}}.

These representations stably restrict to each other by the inclusions
\mathcal{H}(k)\subset\mathcal{H}(k+1)
, so that we have the stabilization sequences,

(2.127)

\displaystyle\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}M\mathcal{H}(k-1)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}

\displaystyle M\mathcal{H}(k)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}M\mathcal{H}(k+1)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\cdots

(2.128)

\displaystyle\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}MT\mathcal{H}(k-1)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}

\displaystyle MT\mathcal{H}(k)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}MT\mathcal{H}(k+1)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\cdots

and a tangential/normal
\mathcal{H}(k)
-structure canonically induces a tangential/normal
H(k^{\prime})
-structure for
k^{\prime}>k
, respectively.
More generally we also use the stabilization sequence

(2.129)

\displaystyle\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}MT(\mathcal{H}(k-1),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k-1)})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}

\displaystyle MT(\mathcal{H}(k),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}MT(\mathcal{H}(k+1),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k+1)})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\cdots

for each integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Taking the colimit of the above stablilization sequences, we define

(2.130)

\displaystyle M\mathcal{H}:=\varinjlim_{k}M\mathcal{H}(k),\quad MT\mathcal{H}:=\varinjlim_{k}MT\mathcal{H}(k).

and call the corresponding structures
H
-structures
.

Remark 2.131

(stable versus unstable)
.

The word “stable” needs to be taken with care, since there are two distinct senses of stability here. The notion of stability in Definition
2.116
has nothing to do with the stabilizing sequence (
2.129
). In other words, although the structure classified by
MT\mathcal{H}(k)
or
M\mathcal{H}(k)
could be regarded as
unstable
in the sense that we are not taking colimit of the stabilization sequence (
2.127
), it is stable in the sense of Definition
2.118
.
This distinction is very important for us, since our main construction, the topological elliptic genus, is of the form, e.g., (
1.13
)

(2.132)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MTSU(k)\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k},

defined for each
k
, and detects sensitively the information that is lost after stabilization
k\to\infty
.

Remark 2.133
.

It is important to distinguish tangential and normal structures. Typically, we have

(2.134)

\displaystyle M\mathcal{H}\simeq MT\mathcal{H}\quad\mbox{(for many cases)}

after stabilizing
k\to\infty
. This is the case for the examples listed above. However, we do have counterexamples, such as
MPin^{+}\simeq MTPin^{-}
.
Moreover, it is important for us that, even if we have (
2.134
) after stabilization, we have

(2.135)

\displaystyle M\mathcal{H}(k)\not\simeq MT\mathcal{H}(k)\quad\mbox{(for almost all cases!)}

for finite
k
. In fact, there is no natural map between
M\mathcal{H}(k)
and
MTH(k^{\prime})
for any pair
(k,k^{\prime})
.

Example 2.136
.

Consider the manifold
S^{k}
for an integer
k\geq 2
. On
S^{k}
, we can consider

•

The stable tangential framing (i.e., the stable
(\mathcal{B},f)=(\mathrm{pt},0)
-structure)
\mathfrak{s}_{\rm BB}^{\mathrm{fr}}:=(P=\underline{e},\psi_{\rm BB})
, commonly called the “blackboard framing”, where
\psi_{\rm BB}\colon TS^{k}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k+1}
is given by the standard embedding
S^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
. We have

(2.137)

\displaystyle[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\rm BB}]=0\in\Omega^{fr}_{k}\simeq\pi_{k}S.

This stable tangential framing induces a stable tangential
(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure for any
(\mathcal{B},f)
by the unit map
S\to MT(\mathcal{B},f)
. In particular, we get the
stable

Spin(k)
-structure on
S^{k}
, which we denote by
\mathfrak{s}_{\rm BB}^{Spin(k)}
.

•

The strict tangential
Spin(k)
-structure which we denote by
\mathfrak{s}^{Spin(k)}_{\rm str}=(P_{\rm str},\psi_{\rm str})
. Here, we put the orientation of
S^{k}
to coincide with the one induced by the blackboard one to get the strict tangential
SO(k)
-structure, and lift it uniquely to a strict tangential
Spin(k)
-structure using the fact that
\pi_{1}S^{k}=\{*\}
.

It is important to note that we have

(2.138)

\displaystyle[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}^{Spin(k)}_{\rm str}]\neq[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}^{Spin(k)}_{\rm BB}]=0\in\Omega_{k}^{Spin(k)}

Indeed, as a principal
Spin(k)
-bundle,
P_{\rm str}
is not isomorphic to the trivial one.
On the other hand, after the stabilization, we have

(2.139)

\displaystyle\mathrm{stab}\left([S^{k},\mathfrak{s}^{Spin(k)}_{\rm str}]\right)=\mathrm{stab}\left([S^{k},\mathfrak{s}^{Spin(k)}_{\rm BB}]\right)=0\in\Omega_{k}^{Spin}\simeq\pi_{k}MTSpin\simeq\pi_{k}MSpin.

We will come back to this example in Remark
4.134
.

Now let us recall the Pontryagin-Thom isomorphism in this context.
Given a closed manifold
M
, the
Pontryagin-Thom collapse map
is the map of spectra

(2.140)

\displaystyle\mathrm{coll}\colon S\to M^{-TM},

which is defined by embedding
M
into
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for large enough
N
and collapsing the complement of a tubular neighborhood.
If furthermore
M
is equipped with a stable
tangential

(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure
\mathfrak{s}
, we compose

(2.141)

\displaystyle S\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coll}}M^{-TM}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}\mathcal{B}^{-f}[-m]:=MT(\mathcal{B},f)[-m],

to get an element in
\pi_{m}MT(\mathcal{B},f)
.
On the other hand, if
M
is equipped with a
normal

(\mathcal{B},f)
-structure
\mathfrak{s}^{\perp}
, we compose (see footnote
12
)

(2.142)

\displaystyle S\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coll}}M^{-TM}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}^{\perp}}\mathcal{B}^{f}[-m]:=MT(\mathcal{B},f)[-m],

to get an element in
\pi_{m}M(\mathcal{B},f)
.

Fact 2.143

(Pontryagin-Thom isomorphism)
.

The above procedure, called the Pontryagin-Thom construction, gives isomorphisms

(2.144)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}\colon\Omega_{m}^{(\mathcal{B},f)}

\displaystyle\simeq\pi_{m}MT(\mathcal{B},f),\quad[M,\mathfrak{s}]\mapsto\eqref{eq_PT_tangential}

(2.145)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}\colon\Omega_{m}^{(\mathcal{B},f)^{\perp}}

\displaystyle\simeq\pi_{m}M(\mathcal{B},f),\quad[M,\mathfrak{s}^{\perp}]\mapsto\eqref{eq_PT_normal}.

This justifies the terminology introduced in Section
1.1
(
13
).

3.
The definitions of topological elliptic genera

In this section we introduce our main construction, the
topological elliptic genera
. As explained in Introduction, we produce a class of maps of the form (here
G,H
are compact Lie groups,
\tau_{G}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
,
\tau_{H}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(H)
, and
\mathcal{D}
is the appropriate data explained in Section
3.2
)

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon MT(H,\tau_{H})\to\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}

which refine the classical elliptic genera for
SU
-manifolds as well as the Witten-Landweber-Ochanine genus for Spin manifolds, and generalizes them further.
This section is organized as follows. In Section
3.1
, as a warm-up to illustrate our ideas, we explain the construction in the most basic case
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
, which refines the classical elliptic genera
\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}
(
1.1
).
Then in Section
3.2
we introduce the general construction.

3.1.
The
U(1)
-topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon MTSU(k)\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k}

Here we introduce the construction of
U(1)
-topological elliptic genus
, which is a map of spectra

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon MTSU(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TJF}_{k}.

Here
V_{U(1)}
denotes the fundamental representation of
U(1)
.
The left hand side is the tangential
SU(k)
-bordism spectrum in Section
2.4
, and the right hand side is the spectrum of Topological Jacobi Forms with index
\frac{k}{2}
, explained in detail in Appendix
A
.

Let us set the notation:
We denote by
V_{SU(k)}
and
V_{U(k)}
the fundamental complex representations of the indicated group. They are of real rank
2k
, but it is important that we can, and do, canonically regard them as complex representations of rank
k
.
Let us consider the following representation of
U(1)\times SU(k)
of real dimension
2n
,

(3.3)

\displaystyle V_{\phi}:=V_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(U(1)\times SU(k)).

The following proposition is crucial for our main construction.

Proposition 3.4
.

The virtual representation

(3.5)

\displaystyle\overline{V}_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)}=(V_{U(1)}-\underline{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{SU(k)}-k\underline{\mathbb{C}})\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(1)\times SU(k))

has a
BU\langle 6\rangle
-structure
\mathfrak{s}
(see (
9
), in particular it induces a string structure), and it is unique up to homotopy.

Proof.

There exists
BU\langle 6\rangle
-structure because
c_{i}(\overline{V}_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)})=0
for
i=1,2
.
Moreover, since
H^{i}(BU(1)\times BSU(k);\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i=3,5
, the choice of such a lift is unique up to homotopy.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

As explained in
[
BYb
]
, another more conceptual definition of the
BU\langle 6\rangle
-structure in Proposition
3.4
is to deduce from the multiplication on
\mathrm{ku}\langle\bullet\rangle
,

\mathrm{ku}\langle 2\rangle\wedge\mathrm{ku}\langle 4\rangle\to\mathrm{ku}\langle 6\rangle,

where
\mathrm{ku}\langle n\rangle
denote the
n
-connective cover of
\mathrm{ku}
.

Let us denote

(3.7)

\displaystyle\Theta:=\overline{V}_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)}.

By Proposition
3.4
and the equivariant sigma orientation (Fact
2.104
), we get an equivalence of
U(1)\times SU(k)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-module spectra,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\sigma(\Theta,\mathfrak{s})\colon\mathrm{TMF}[\Theta]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}.

Combining with the following equivalence in
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(1)\times SU(k))
,

\Theta\simeq V_{\phi}-k\cdot\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{U(1)\times SU(k)}(V_{U(1)})-\mathrm{res}_{SU(k)}^{U(1)\times SU(k)}(\overline{V}_{SU(k)}),

we get the following equivalence of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules, also denoted by the same symbol,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\sigma(\Theta,\mathfrak{s})\colon\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\phi}]^{U(1)\times SU(k)}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)},

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}.

The following is our main construction.

Definition 3.11

(The coevaluation map
\mathcal{F}_{U(1)_{k}}
)
.

We define a morphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)},

to be the following composition.

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\phi})\cdot}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\phi}]^{U(1)\times SU(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta,\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}.

Using the dualizability result (
2.31
) the
\mathrm{TMF}
-linear dual to
\mathrm{TMF}[\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}
is canonically identified with
\mathrm{TMF}[-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}-\mathrm{Ad}(SU(k))]^{SU(k)}
. Thus the morphism (
3.12
) is equivalently regarded as the following morphism,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}-\mathrm{Ad}(SU(k))]^{SU(k)}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k}

Definition 3.15

(The topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
)
.

We define
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
to be the composition

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MTSU(k)=BSU(k)^{-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}}\simeq(S^{-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}})_{hSU(k)}

(3.17)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{u}\mathrm{TMF}[-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]_{hSU(k)}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Nm}}\mathrm{TMF}[-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}-\mathrm{Ad}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}

(3.19)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{U(1)_{k}}}\mathrm{TJF}_{k},

where
u\colon S\to\mathrm{TMF}
is the unit map.

An alternative definition is available as follows.

Proposition 3.20

(Alternative definition of
\mathrm{Jac}_{{U(1)}_{k}}
)
.

Consider the following map in
\mathrm{Spectra}^{U(1)}
:

(3.21)

\displaystyle MTSU(k)=BSU(k)^{-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}}\xhookrightarrow{\chi(V_{\phi})\cdot}BSU(k)^{V_{\phi}-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}}.

Here,
MTSU(k)
is regarded as a spectrum with trivial
U(1)
-equivariance, and
V_{\phi}=V_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)}
is regarded as a
U(1)
-equivariant vector bundle over
BSU(k)
. The map is given by the inclusion of the zero section of
V_{\phi}
.
After tensoring with
\mathrm{TMF}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{U(1)}
, we get, again in
\mathrm{Spectra}^{U(1)}
,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq_alternative_1_U(1)}\xrightarrow{u\otimes\mathrm{id}}\mathrm{TMF}\otimes BSU(k)^{V_{\phi}-\overline{V}_{SU(k)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta,\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}\otimes BSU(k)_{+}\otimes S^{kV_{U(1)}},

by the
U(1)
-equivariant sigma orientation,
since the virtual vector bundle
\Theta=\overline{V}_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)}
, regarded as a
U(1)
-equivariant virtual vector bundle over
BSU(k)
, is equipped with a
U(1)
-equivariant
BU\langle 6\rangle
-structure
\mathfrak{s}
by Proposition
3.4
.
Take the genuine
U(1)
-fixed point of the composition of (
3.21
) and (
3.22
), and further consider the following:

\textstyle{MTSU(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq_alternative_2_U(1)}\circ\eqref{eq_alternative_1_U(1)}}
\textstyle{\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes BSU(k)_{+}\otimes S^{kV_{U(1)}}\right)^{U(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(BSU(k)\to\mathrm{pt})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}=\mathrm{TJF}_{k}.}

We claim that the diagram (
3.20
) commutes; i.e, we can take the composition in that diagram as an alternative definition of
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
.

Proof.

This directly follows from the definition of
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
.
∎

Remark 3.27
.

Notice that the alternative definition of
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
in Proposition
3.20
only use genuine equivariance with respect to
U(1)
and not to
SU(k)
. Moreover, we do not use the dualizability of the genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
. Nevertheless, we employ Definition
3.15
as the main definition because the coevaluation map
\mathcal{F}_{U(1)_{k}}
(Definition
3.11
) is essential in the level-rank duality we will explore in Section
6
.

Remark 3.28

(Geometric description of
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
)
.

Having the alternative definition of
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
in Proposition
3.20
at hand, we can easily get the following geometric description of the composition

(3.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}\circ\mathrm{PT}\colon\Omega_{m}^{SU(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{PT}}}{{\simeq}}\pi_{m}MTSU(k)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}}\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}

as follows. Recall (Section
2.4
) that a class in
\Omega_{m}^{SU(k)}
is represented by a data
(M,P,\psi)
of closed
m
-dimensional manifold
M
and a stable tangential
SU(k)
-structure
(P,\psi)
on
M
.
Given such an
(M,P,\psi)
, denote by
V_{P}:=P\times_{SU(k)}V_{SU(k)}
be the associated bundle to the principal
SU(k)
-bundle
P
, and
consider the following map of
U(1)
-equivariant Thom spectra,

(3.30)

\displaystyle S^{2k-m}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coll}}\Sigma^{2k-m}M^{-TM}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\simeq}}M^{-V_{P}}\xhookrightarrow{\chi(V_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(1)})}M^{V_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(1)}-V_{P}},

where we are equipping
V_{P}
with a trivial
U(1)
-action, and
V_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(1)}
is isomorphic to
V_{P}
as a vector bundle but equipped with the nontrivial
U(1)
-action.
The first map in (
3.30
) is the Pontryagin-Thom collapse map in (
2.140
), and
he last map is the inclusion of the zero section of
V_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(1)}
.
Note that the following
U(1)
-equivariant virtual vector bundle over
M
,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\Theta_{P}:=\overline{V}_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{U(1)}=V_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(1)}-V_{P}-k\overline{V}_{U(1)}

is equipped with a
U(1)
-equivariant
BU\langle 6\rangle
-structure
\mathfrak{s}
by using the
SU(k)
-structure on
V
and Proposition
3.4
.
Thus we have the Thom isomorphism in
U(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-homology,

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}_{*}^{U(1)}(M^{V_{P}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(1)}-V_{P}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta_{P},\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}_{*+2k}(M_{+}\wedge S^{kV_{U(1)}}).

We get the composition

(3.33)

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq_geometric_Jac_Theta}\circ\eqref{eq_geometric_Jac}}\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}_{(m-2k)+2k}(M_{+}\wedge S^{kV_{U(1)}})

(3.34)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(M\to\mathrm{pt})_{*}}\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}_{m}(S^{kV_{U(1)}})=\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}

It directly follows from Proposition
3.20
that we have

Claim 3.35
.

The unit
1\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}
maps to
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}[M,P,\psi]\in\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
by the composition (
3.33
).

The topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
has the following functoriality in increasing
k
.

Proposition 3.36
.

The following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{MTSU(k-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(SU(k-1)\hookrightarrow SU(k))_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot}
\textstyle{MTSU(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}}

Proof.

This is a special case of Proposition
4.144
.
∎

3.2.
The general construction

The idea in the construction of the topological elliptic genus in the last subsection works quite generally.
Here we explain the construction in the most general setting.
Assume we are given a set of data as follows, symbolically denoted by
\mathcal{D}
.

•

Fix compact Lie groups
G
and
H
contained in the subcategory
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathrm{cptLie}
in Fact
2.104

(or simply
G,H\in\mathrm{cptLie}
, if we assume Conjecture
2.105
; see the last paragraph of Section
4.1
)
, together with
\tau_{G}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
and
\tau_{H}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(H)
.

•

Fix an integer
d
and a group homomorphism
\phi\colon G\times H\to O(d)
. We denote the corresponding
d
-dimensional orthogonal representation by
V_{\phi}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G\times H)
.

•

We assume that
\dim\tau_{H}=0
and
d=\dim\tau_{G}
14
14

14

This assumption is technical. In general, we can just add trivial representations to
\tau_{G}
or
\tau_{H}
to reduce to this case.
.

•

We fix a string structure
\mathfrak{s}
on the virtual representation

(3.41)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathcal{D}}:=V_{\phi}-\mathrm{res}_{G}^{G\times H}(\tau_{G})-\mathrm{res}_{H}^{G\times H}(\tau_{H})\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G\times H),

i.e., we assume that the composition

(3.42)

\displaystyle BG\times BH\xrightarrow{\Theta_{\mathcal{D}}}BO\to P^{4}BO

is nullhomotopic and
\mathfrak{s}
is a choice of its nullhomotopy.

By Fact
2.104
on the equivariant sigma orientation, the string structure
\mathfrak{s}
induces an equivalence of
G\times H
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-module spectra,

(3.43)

\displaystyle\sigma(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{s})\colon\mathrm{TMF}[\Theta_{\mathcal{D}}]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}

This induces the following equivalence in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
also denoted by the same symbol,

(3.44)

\displaystyle\sigma(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{s})\colon\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\phi}]^{G\times H}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}.

Example 3.45
.

To recover the construction in the last subsection, we set
G:=U(1)
,
H:=SU(k)
with
\tau_{G}:=kV_{U(1)}
,
\tau_{H}:=\overline{V}_{SU(k)}=V_{SU(k)}-k\underline{\mathbb{C}}
and
V_{\phi}:=V_{U(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)}
, and the equivalence (
3.43
) is given by Proposition
3.4
.

In this general setting, we construct a map of spectra

(3.46)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon MT(H,\tau_{H})\to\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}

as follows.

Definition 3.47

(
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
)
.

We define a morphism in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
,

(3.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G},

to be the following composition.

(3.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\phi})\cdot}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\phi}]^{G\times H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}.

The last step uses (
3.44
).
Using the dualizability result (
2.31
), the
\mathrm{TMF}
-linear dual to
\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}
is canonically identified with
\mathrm{TMF}[-\tau_{H}-\mathrm{Ad}(H)]^{H}
. Thus the morphism (
3.12
) is equivalently regarded as the following morphism,

(3.50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[-\tau_{H}-\mathrm{Ad}(H)]^{H}\to\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}

Remark 3.51
.

For some examples of
\mathcal{D}
we give in Section
4
(including Example
3.45
above), we prove in Section
6
that the morphism
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}
provides a
\mathrm{TMF}
-module duality isomorphism

(3.52)

\displaystyle D(\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G})\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H},

which corresponds to the level-rank duality in physics. But the definition of topological elliptic genus below does NOT use the fact that it is an isomorphism, but only uses the morphism (
3.50
) (which is not in general an isomorphism).

Definition 3.53

(The topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}
)
.

In the above settings, we define
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}
to be the composition

(3.54)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\colon MT(H,\tau_{H})=BH^{-\tau_{H}}\simeq(S^{-\tau_{H}})_{hH}

(3.55)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{u}\mathrm{TMF}[-\tau_{H}]_{hH}

(3.56)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\eqref{eq_norm_equivariant}]{\mathrm{Nm}}\mathrm{TMF}[-\tau_{H}-\mathrm{Ad}(H)]^{H}

(3.57)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G},

where
u\colon S\to\mathrm{TMF}
is the unit map.

Remark 3.58

(Alternative definitions and geometric descriptions)
.

Recall that in the case of
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
we have explained in Proposition
3.20
and Remark
3.28
that an alternative definition and the corresponding geometric description for
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}
are available.
In this general case here, we also have an analogous re-phrasing of the definition which only uses genuine
G
-equivariance and not using genuine
H
-equivariance nor the dualizability of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
. We also get the corresponding geometric description.
We leave the details to the reader.

Remark 3.59
.

Let us remark what happens if we take trivial choices of representations. We will see that the associated topological elliptic genus are something trivial.
Let
G
and
H
be compact Lie groups,
d=0
,
\tau_{G}=0
and
\tau_{H}=0
. Then we have a trivial choice of the string orientation
\mathfrak{s}
in (
3.41
). Let us denote those data as
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{triv}}
.

Then, the map
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{triv}}}
in Definition
3.47
factors as

(3.60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{triv}}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}=\mathrm{TMF}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{e}^{G}\otimes\mathrm{res}_{e}^{H}}\mathrm{TMF}^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}^{H}.

So the map in (
3.50
) factors as

(3.61)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{triv}}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}(H)]^{H}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}_{H}^{e}}\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{e}^{G}}\mathrm{TMF}^{G}.

Now notice that the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}BH_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{(H\to e)_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\otimes BH_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Nm}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}(H)]^{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}_{H}^{e}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF},}

Then the resulting topological elliptic genus in Definition
3.53
just becomes the composition

(3.66)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{triv}}}\colon MT(H,0)=\Sigma^{\infty}BH_{+}\xrightarrow{(H\to e)_{*}}S\xrightarrow{u}\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{e}^{G}}\mathrm{TMF}^{G}.

In the next subsection, we will see further examples of this construction.

3.2.1.
Functoriality

Here we discuss an easy functoriality of the construction above. Suppose we have two sets of data
\mathcal{D}=(G,H,\tau_{G},\tau_{H},V_{\phi},\mathfrak{s})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},H^{\prime},\tau_{G^{\prime}},\tau_{H^{\prime}},V_{\phi^{\prime}},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
as above.
Assume that
d=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\phi}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\phi^{\prime}}
.
We define a
morphism

(3.67)

\displaystyle\alpha\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

to consist of the following data:

•

Group homomorphisms (note the directions!)

(3.68)

\displaystyle\alpha_{G}

\displaystyle\colon G^{\prime}\to G

(3.69)

\displaystyle\alpha_{H}

\displaystyle\colon H\to H^{\prime}.

•

Equivalences of (virtual) representations,

(3.70)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\tau_{G}}\colon\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}}(\tau_{G})

\displaystyle\simeq\tau_{G^{\prime}}\mbox{ in }\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G^{\prime}),

(3.71)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\tau_{H}}\colon\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{H}}(\tau_{H^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\simeq\tau_{H}\mbox{ in }\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(H),

(3.72)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\phi}\colon\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}_{H}}(V_{\phi})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}_{G^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{H}}(V_{\phi})\mbox{ in }\mathrm{Rep}_{O(d)}(G^{\prime}\times H).

•

An equivalence

(3.73)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathfrak{s}}\colon\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}_{H}}(\mathfrak{s})\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}_{G^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{H}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})

of string structures on

(3.74)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}_{H}}(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}})\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}_{G^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{H}}(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}})\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G^{\prime}\times H).

Proposition 3.75
.

If we have a morphism
\alpha\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

The maps
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
are compatible in the sense that the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H^{\prime}}]^{H^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G^{\prime}}]^{G^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{H}}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G^{\prime}}]^{G^{\prime}}}

(2)

The topological elliptic genera
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
are compatible in the sense that the following diagram commute.

\textstyle{MT(H,\tau_{H})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\tau_{H}}\circ\alpha_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\tau_{G}}\circ\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}}}
\textstyle{MT(H^{\prime},\tau_{H^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G^{\prime}}]^{G^{\prime}}.}

Proof.

(1) follows from the functoriality of the Euler classes and the isomorphism of string structures.
(2) is a direct consequence of (1).
∎

4.
Examples: The trio of
U
-
Sp

and
O
-topological elliptic genera

In this section, we introduce a
trio
of examples—
(U,SU)
,
(Sp,Sp)
,
(O,Spin)
—where the general construction of Section
3.2
applies. Those classes come in families.

Definition 4.1

(The topological elliptic genera
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(n)_{k}}
,
\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(n)_{k}}

and
\mathrm{Jac}_{O(n)_{k}}
)
.

We define the morphisms

(4.2)

\displaystyle~\mathrm{Jac}_{U(n)_{k}}\colon MT(SU(k),n\overline{V}_{SU(k)})\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)},

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(n)_{k}}\colon MT(Sp(k),n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)})\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{O(n)_{k}}\colon MT(Spin(k),n\overline{V}_{Spin(k)})\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{O(n)}]^{O(n)}.

for each
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, by applying the general construction to the following data. Here, for each group
K
appearing below, the notation
V_{K}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(K)
denotes the fundamental (a.k.a. defining, or vector) representation.

•

For (
4.2
), the data
\mathcal{D}=U(n)_{k}
consists of

(4.5)

\displaystyle G:=U(n),\ H:=SU(k),\ \tau_{G}:=kV_{U(n)},\ \tau_{H}:=n\overline{V}_{SU(k)},\ V_{\phi}:=V_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)}

so that
\Theta_{U(n)_{k}}=\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(n)\times SU(k))
15
15

15

We compute

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Theta_{U(n)_{k}}

\displaystyle=V_{\phi}-\mathrm{res}_{G}^{G\times H}(\tau_{G})-\mathrm{res}_{H}^{G\times H}(\tau_{H})

(4.7)

\displaystyle=V_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)}-V_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}k\underline{\mathbb{C}}-n\underline{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{SU(k)}-k\underline{\mathbb{C}})

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)}.

Similar computations replacing
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{H}
produce the corresponding formulas for
\Theta_{O(n)_{k}}
and
\Theta_{Sp(n)_{k}}
.

, with its string structure obtained by Proposition
4.16
below.

•

For (
4.3
), the data
\mathcal{D}=Sp(n)_{k}
consists of

(4.9)

\displaystyle G:=Sp(n),\ H:=Sp(k),\ \tau_{G}:=kV_{Sp(n)},\ \tau_{H}:=n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)},\ V_{\phi}:=V_{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}V^{*}_{Sp(k)}

Here
V_{Sp(k)}^{*}
denotes the quarternionic dual representation so that
(g,h)\in Sp(n)\times_{\mathbb{H}}Sp(k)
acts on
V_{\phi}
by
w\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}v^{*}\mapsto gw\otimes v^{*}h^{*}
.
16
16

16

Note that
V_{\phi}
is no longer a quaternionic representation, but just a real representation. This corresponds to the standard homomorphism
\phi\colon Sp(n)\times Sp(k)\to SO(4nk)
.
Since
V_{Sp(k)}^{*}\simeq V_{Sp(k)}
in the orthogonal representation ring
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(Sp(k))
, the same computation as footnote
15
is valid,
so that
\Theta_{Sp(n)_{k}}=\overline{V}_{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}\overline{V^{*}}_{Sp(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(Sp(n)\times Sp(k))
, with its string structure obtained by Proposition
4.18
below.

•

For (
4.4
), the data
\mathcal{D}=O(n)_{k}
consists of

(4.10)

\displaystyle G:=O(n),\ H:=Spin(k),\ \tau_{G}:=kV_{O(n)},\ \tau_{H}:=n\overline{V}_{Spin(k)},\ V_{\phi}:=V_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}V_{Spin(k)}

so that
\Theta_{O(n)_{k}}=\overline{V}_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{V}_{Spin(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(O(n)\times Spin(k))
,
with its string structure obtained by Proposition
4.26
below.

Notation 4.11
.

In the text, we generally refer to
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(n)_{k}}
,
\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(n)_{k}}

and
\mathrm{Jac}_{O(n)_{k}}
as the
U
-,
Sp
,
and
O
-
topological elliptic genera, respectively. When we want to specify
n
, we also use the term “
U(n)
-topological elliptic genera”, and so on.

The particularly important case is
n=1
. We get
U(1)
,
Sp(1)

and
O(1)
-topological elliptic genera from the familiar tangential bordism spectra,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon MTSU(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TJF}_{k}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{k}}\colon MTSp(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}:=\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{O(1)_{k}}\colon MTSpin(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\textstyle\mathrm{Jac}_{O(1)_{k}}\colon MTSpin(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Jac}_{O(1)_{k}}\colon MTSpin(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Jac}_{O(1)_{k}}\colon MTSpin(k)\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}$}},

Also, it is important that we have obtained the
coevaluation maps

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}]^{\mathcal{H}(k)}

which have been used in Definition
3.53
of the topological elliptic genera. This is the subject of Section
6
: In the cases of
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})=(U,SU)
and
(Sp,Sp)
, we show that the above coevaluation map exhibits the duality between
\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}
and
\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}]^{\mathcal{H}(k)}
in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
, which reflects the
level-rank duality
in physics.

The rest of this section is organized as follows. In Section
4.1
, we complete Definition
4.1
by showing the existence of a canonical choice of string structures on
\Theta_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}
above.
Then, in Section
4.2
we explain the relations among
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}
for different
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
and for different
(n,k)
, to illustrate that the trio of topological elliptic genera are organized in one coherent picture.

4.1.
The string structures on
\Theta_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}

Proposition 4.16
.

The virtual representation

(4.17)

\displaystyle\Theta_{U(n),SU(k)}=\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(n)\times SU(k))

has a
BU\langle 6\rangle
-structure
\mathfrak{s}_{U,SU}
, and it is unique up to homotopy. This induces a string structure by (
9
).

Proof.

The proof is exactly parallel to that of Proposition
3.4
(Also see Remark
3.6
).
∎

Proposition 4.18
.

The virtual representation

(4.19)

\displaystyle\Theta_{Sp(n),Sp(k)}=\overline{V}_{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}\overline{V^{*}}_{Sp(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(Sp(n)\times Sp(k))

has a string structure
\mathfrak{s}_{Sp,Sp}
, and it is unique up to homotopy.

Proof.

Since
H^{i}(BSp(n)\times BSp(k);\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i=1,2
, we get a spin structure automatically. We have
H^{4}(BSp(n)\times BSp(k);\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, so the string obstruction class
p_{1}/2
for the representation in question is measured by
c_{2}
after complexification. Now we have the following canonical identification for any pair of symplectic vector bundles
V
and
W
over a space
X
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle(V\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}W)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\simeq V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W,

where on the right hand side we used the underlying complex structures of
V
and
W
. This means that

(4.21)

\displaystyle c_{2}((V\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}W)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})=c_{2}(V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W).

Since forgetting symplectic structure to complex structure gives the map
Sp(n)\to SU(2n)
, we get
c_{2}(\overline{V}_{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V^{*}}_{Sp(k)})=0
by Proposition
4.16
. Thus we have a string structure as desired.
The uniqueness follows from
H^{3}(BSp(n)\times BSp(k);\mathbb{Z})=0
.
∎

In order to state the proposition regarding the string orientation of
\Theta_{O(n)_{k}}
, we need a little preparation.
Consider the following group homomorphisms,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\alpha_{G}

\displaystyle\colon O(n)\hookrightarrow U(n),

(4.23)

\displaystyle\beta_{H}

\displaystyle\colon SU\left(\lfloor k/2\rfloor\right)\hookrightarrow Spin(2\lfloor k/2\rfloor)\hookrightarrow Spin(k),

where
\alpha_{G}
is induced by
\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and
\beta_{H}
is induced by forgetting the complex structure of
\mathbb{C}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
to regard it as the real vector space
\mathbb{R}^{2\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
, and the second arrow is nontrivial only for
k
odd.
Then we can easily verify that

Lemma 4.24
.

We have the following canonical isomorphism in
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}\left(O(n)\times SU\left(\lfloor k/2\rfloor\right)\right)
,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}\left(\overline{V}_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{V}_{Spin(k)}\right)\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}}\left(\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{SU\left(\lfloor k/2\rfloor\right)}\right).

The virtual representation appearing on the right hand side of (
4.25
) is equipped with a string structure
\mathfrak{s}_{U,SU}
by Proposition
4.16
.
Now we can state the proposition for the string structure on
\Theta_{O(n),Spin(k)}
.

Proposition 4.26
.

The virtual representation

(4.27)

\displaystyle\Theta_{O(n),Spin(k)}=\overline{V}_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{V}_{Spin(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(O(n)\times Spin(k))

admits a string structure, and there is, up to homotopy, a unique choice
\mathfrak{s}_{O,Spin}
which admits the following equivalence of string structures when restricted to
O(n)\times Spin(k)
,

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}(\mathfrak{s}_{O,Spin})\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}}(\mathfrak{s}_{U,SU}).

Here we are using Lemma
4.24
, and the string structure
\mathfrak{s}_{U,SU}
on
\Theta_{U(n),SU(\lfloor k/2\rfloor)}
is the one in Proposition
4.16
.

Proof.

The existence of string structures follows by checking the vanishing of
\frac{p_{1}}{2}
.
The second claim follows by the fact that the map

(4.29)

\displaystyle BO(n)\times BSU(k^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}BO(n)\times BSpin(2k^{\prime})

for any
k^{\prime}\geq 1
is
5
-connected, so that giving a string structure on
\Theta_{O(n),Spin(k)}
is equivalent to giving a string structure on
\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}(\Theta_{O(n),Spin(k)})
.
∎

4.2.
Structures of the trio

Now we explain the relations among the trio of topological elliptic genera we have constructed, unifying the above constructions into a coherent picture. There are the
external
structure relating different
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, and the
internal
structure relating different
(n,k)
.

4.2.1.
External structure: change of
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})

Recall we have set up the notion of
morphisms
between the defining data of the general topological elliptic genera in Section
3.2.1
.
We have a natural choice of morphisms

(4.30)

\displaystyle\alpha_{Sp}^{U}\colon Sp(n)_{k}=(Sp(n),Sp(k),\cdots)\to U(n)_{2k}=(U(n),SU(2k),\cdots)

(4.31)

\displaystyle\alpha_{U}^{O}\colon U(n)_{k}=(U(n),SU(k),\cdots)\to O(n)_{2k}=(O(n),Spin(2k),\cdots)

(where we abbreviated rest of the data by “
\cdots
”), given by the group homomorphisms

(4.32)

\displaystyle\alpha_{G}\colon U(n)\hookrightarrow Sp(n),\quad\alpha_{H}\colon Sp(k)\hookrightarrow SU(2k),\quad\mbox{ for }\alpha_{Sp}^{U}

(4.33)

\displaystyle\alpha_{G}\colon O(n)\hookrightarrow U(n),\quad\alpha_{H}\colon SU(k)\hookrightarrow Spin(2k),\quad\mbox{ for }\alpha_{U}^{O}

It is easy to complete the remaining ingredients listed in Section
3.2.1
, to get morphisms (
4.30
).
Note that the string structure in the data
(O,Spin)
is chosen so that we get a morphism
\alpha_{U}^{O}
above.

From the above morphisms, we get the following maps, which we call the
external structure maps
, in the domains and codomains of the topological elliptic genera,

(4.34)

\displaystyle MT(Sp(k),n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)})\xrightarrow{(Sp(k)\hookrightarrow SU(2k))_{*}}MT(SU(2k),n\overline{V}_{SU(2k)}),

(4.35)

\displaystyle MT(SU(k),n\overline{V}_{U(k)})\xrightarrow{(SU(k)\hookrightarrow Spin(2k))_{*}}MT(Spin(2k),n\overline{V}_{Spin(2k)}),

(4.36)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}^{U(n)}_{Sp(n)}}\mathrm{TMF}[2kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)},

(4.37)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}^{O(n)}_{U(n)}}\mathrm{TMF}[2kV_{O(n)}]^{O(n)}

By Proposition
3.75
, we see that our topological elliptic genera are compatible with the above structure maps, as follows.

Proposition 4.38

(Compatibility of
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}
for different
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
)
.

The
U
,
Sp

and
O
-topological elliptic genera are compatible in the sense that the following diagrams commute.

\textstyle{MT(SU(2k),n\overline{V}_{SU(2k)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

4.2.2.
Internal structure: Change of
(n,k)

Now we introduce the
internal
structures in the trio, which relates different pairs of parameters
(n,k)
. In this case we fix

to be any one of
(SU,U)
,
(Sp,Sp)

and
(Spin,O)
. Set

in each case, respectively.
In contrast to the previous structure maps, the internal structure maps relating different

do NOT come from morphisms of the defining data in Section

.

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\phantom{X}\\
\phantom{X}~~\text{fib seq}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{(k+1,n-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

and each
(k-1,n)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}(k,n)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}}(k,n-1)
forms a fiber sequence of corresponding equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
and of tangential Thom spectra, as we will see below.

Remark 4.75
.

We do NOT use equivariant sigma orientation (Section
4.1
) for definition of the internal structures on equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
and the bordism spectra, so the contents from below until Remark (
4.137
) does NOT rely on Fact
2.104
nor Conjecture
2.105
. So in particular we can apply Propositions
4.78
,
4.116
and
20
to
\mathcal{K}=O,Spin
, WITHOUT assuming
Conjecture
2.105
.

The internal structure in equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}

First, let us introduce the structure maps in the equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
s appearing in the trio. Let
\mathcal{K}
be any one of
U,SU,Sp,O,Spin
, where we set
N=2,2,4,1,1
, respectively.
For each pair of integers
i\geq 1
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
(in the case of
\mathcal{K}=SU,Spin
, we impose
i\geq 2
), consider the maps

(4.76)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{TMF}[(j-1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\to\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)},

(4.77)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\to\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+Nj]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}

which we call the
internal structure maps
in the trio of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
. We often call the maps (
4.76
) and (
4.77
)
stabilization
and
restriction
, respectively.

Proposition 4.78

(The stabilization-restriction fiber sequence of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
)
.
17
17

17

The authors thank Lennart Meier for noting this lemma.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be any one of
U,SU,Sp,O,Spin
18
18

18

See Remark
4.75
.
, where we set
N=2,2,4,1,1
, respectively. Let
i\geq 1
(in the case
\mathcal{K}=SU,Spin
we impose
i\geq 2
) and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The maps (
4.76
) and (
4.77
) form a fiber sequence of
\mathrm{TMF}
-module spectra,

(4.79)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[(j-1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{stab}]{\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot}\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{res}]{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}}\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+Nj]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}.

Remark 4.80
.

This proposition applies to any
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{K}(i))
-graded spectrum, and not just
\mathrm{TMF}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.78
.

For each integer
i
in that range,
the homogeneous space
\mathcal{K}(i)/\mathcal{K}(i-1)
is identified, as a
\mathcal{K}(i)
-space, with the unit sphere
S(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})
of the fundamental representation.
Thus we have a cofiber sequence of pointed
\mathcal{K}(i)
-spaces,

(4.81)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(i)/\mathcal{K}(i-1)_{+}\to S^{0}\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})}S^{V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}}.

For any integer
j
, wedging with
S^{jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}}
gives

(4.82)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\left(S^{-jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}-Nj}\right)\simeq\mathcal{K}(i)/\mathcal{K}(i-1)_{+}\otimes S^{-jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}}\to S^{-jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}}\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\wedge\mathrm{id}}S^{(-j+1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}}.

Here, the first isomorphism used the following general fact: for any inclusion
H\subset G
between compact Lie groups and any
G
-spectrum
X
, we have an isomorphism of
G
-spectra,

(4.83)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}\circ\mathrm{Res}_{G}^{H}(X)\simeq(G/H)_{+}\otimes X.

Applying
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}(-,\mathrm{TMF})^{\mathcal{K}(i)}
to this, we get the fiber sequence (
4.79
).
∎

Here, let us make an interesting observation that the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence in Proposition
4.78
is
self-dual
in the following sense:

Proposition 4.84

(The self-duality of stabilization-restriction fiber sequences)
.

In the setting of Proposition
4.78
, the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+Nj-1]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{D\left(\mathrm{TMF}[-jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}-Ni-1-\mathrm{Ad}(\mathcal{K}(i-1))]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D(\mathrm{res})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(j-1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{D\left(\mathrm{TMF}[-(j-1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}-\mathrm{Ad}(\mathcal{K}(i))]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D(\mathrm{stab})}
\scriptstyle{D(\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{D\left(\mathrm{TMF}[-jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}-\mathrm{Ad}(\mathcal{K}(i))]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+Nj]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{D\left(\mathrm{TMF}[-jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}-Ni-\mathrm{Ad}(\mathcal{K}(i-1))]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}\right)}

Here both columns are fiber sequences of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules in Proposition
4.78
.
D
denotes the dual in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
, and we are using the dualizability result in (
2.31
).
In particular, the connecting map in the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence (the topleft vertical arrow in (
4.84
)) is identified with the dual to the restriction map, i.e., the transfer map

(4.93)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+Nj-1]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}\to\mathrm{TMF}[(j-1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}.

Proof.

Since we have identified the fiber of stabilization map as the restriction map in Proposition
4.78
, it is enough that the middle square in (
4.84
) commutes. But this follows from the fact that the multiplication by an element in
\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}
is a self-dual operation, since the coevaluation map of the duality data is provided by (
2.32
).
∎

We get the diagram consisting of the structure maps,

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(j-1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[jV_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(j+1)V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}]^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(j-1)(V_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+N)]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[j(V_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+N)]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(j+1)(V_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}+N)]^{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}}

where each pair of consecutive horizontal and vertical arrows form a fiber sequence.
Particularly important cases are the following.

\mathrm{TJF}
)
.

Setting
\mathcal{K}=U
and
i=1
we get (here
\mathrm{stab}:=\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot
)

(4.103)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}\mathrm{TMF}[2k].

This fiber sequence is regarded as constructing
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
by attaching a single
2k
-dimensional
\mathrm{TMF}
-cell to
\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}
.
The sequence (
4.98
) is regarded as building
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
by starting from
\mathrm{TJF}_{1}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}
(see Appendix Section
A.2
) and attaching even dimensional
\mathrm{TMF}
-cells one by one.
We also employ the notation

(4.104)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{\infty}=\mathrm{colim}_{k}\left(\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{TJF}_{k+1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\cdots\right).

For more on
\mathrm{TJF}
, see Appendix
A
.

where each consecutive pair of horizontal and virtical arrows form a fiber sequence

(4.110)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{e}}\mathrm{TMF}[4k].

\mathrm{TEJF}_{0}=\mathrm{TMF}^{Sp(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}
as indicated by the first vertical arrow in (

) is the consequence of Fact

below.
This fiber sequence is regarded as constructing
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
by attaching a single
4k
-dimensional
\mathrm{TMF}
-cell to
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-2}
.
The sequence (
4.105
) is regarded as building
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
by starting from
\mathrm{TEJF}_{0}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}
and attaching
4k
-dimensional
\mathrm{TMF}
-cells one by one.
We study
\mathrm{TEJF}
in more detail in Appendix

. We show, in Proposition

, that we have (note that we are using
\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}
, NOT
\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}_{+}

(4.111)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}[-4],

and the stabilization sequence (
4.105
) is identified as the cell-attaching sequence of
\mathbb{HP}^{k}
.
We also use the notation

(4.112)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}:=\mathrm{colim}_{k}\left(\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k+2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\cdots\right).

For more on
\mathrm{TEJF}
, see Appendix

.

The internal structure in tangential Thom spectra

Next, we introduce the internal structure maps in the tangential Thom spectra. We continue to set
\mathcal{K}
be any one of
U,SU,Sp,O,Spin
, where we set
N=2,2,4,1,1
, respectively, and
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
(
i\geq 2
for
G=SU,Spin
),
j\in\mathbb{Z}
as before. We consider

(4.113)

\displaystyle\mathrm{stab}

\displaystyle\colon MT(\mathcal{K}(i-1),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)})\to MT(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}),

(4.114)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot

\displaystyle\colon MT(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\to MT(\mathcal{K}(i),(j-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})[Ni]

and call them the
internal structure maps
in the trio of tangential Thom spectra.
Here, the map (
4.113
) is the stabilization map (
2.129
) induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{K}(i-1)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{K}(i)
, and (
4.114
) is the composition

(4.115)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot

\displaystyle\colon MT(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\simeq(S^{-j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}})_{h\mathcal{K}(i)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot}(S^{-j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}+V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}})_{h\mathcal{K}(i)}\simeq MT(\mathcal{K}(i),(j-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})[Ni].

By the analogy with the
\mathrm{TMF}
-case, we call (
4.114
) as
restriction map
in the tangential Thom spectra in the trio.
Geometric meaning of this map is explained after the next proposition.

Exactly similarly to Proposition
4.78
, we get

Proposition 4.116

(The stabilization-restriction fiber sequence of tangential Thom spectra
19
19

19

See Remark
4.75
.
)
.

In the setting above, the maps (
4.113
) and (
4.114
) form a fiber sequence

(4.117)

\displaystyle MT(\mathcal{K}(i-1),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}MT(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\xrightarrow[\mathrm{res}]{\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})}MT(\mathcal{K}(i),(j-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})[Ni].

Proof.

The proof is exactly similar to that of Proposition
4.78
. In this case, we apply
(-)_{h\mathcal{K}(i)}
to the sequence (
4.82
) to get the result.
∎

Thus we get the diagram consisting of the structure maps,

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{K}(i-1),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where each pair of consecutive horizontal and vertical arrows form a fiber sequence.
Now we explain the geometric meaning of those structure maps. By the Pontryagin-Thom isomorphism in Fact
2.143
, applying
\pi_{m}
to (
4.113
) and (
4.114
), we get the maps in the tangential bordism groups,

(4.122)

\displaystyle\mathrm{stab}

\displaystyle\colon\Omega_{m}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i-1),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)}\right)}\to\Omega_{m}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)},

(4.123)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}=\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot

\displaystyle\colon\Omega_{m}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)}\to\Omega_{m-Ni}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i),(j-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)}

The geometric meaning of the stabilization map (
4.122
) should be clear: a tangential
(\mathcal{K}(i-1),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i-1)})
-structure canonically induces a tangential
(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)})
-structure by the inclusion
\mathcal{K}(i-1)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{K}(i)
.
On the other hand, the restriction map (
4.122
) is the interesting one, nicely explained as follows.
By (
2.123
), an element of the tangential bordism group
\Omega_{m}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)}
is represented by a triple
(M,P,\psi)
, where
M
is a closed
m
-dimensional manifold,
P
is a principal
\mathcal{K}(i)
-bundle and
\psi
is an isomorphism of vector bundles over
M
,

(4.124)

\displaystyle\psi\colon TM\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{L}

\displaystyle\simeq(P\times_{\mathcal{K}(i)}V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}^{\oplus j})\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{m+L-Nij}

(4.125)

\displaystyle=V_{P}^{(1)}\oplus V_{P}^{(2)}\oplus V_{P}^{(3)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{P}^{(j)}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{m+L-Nij}.

with
L\geq 0
a large enough integer; we also and denoted
V_{P}:=P\times_{\mathcal{K}(i)}V_{\mathcal{K}(i)}
, and each
V_{P}^{(\bullet)}
is a copy of
V_{P}
.
Given such
(M,P,\psi)
, let us take a transverse section
s\in C^{\infty}(M;V_{P}^{(j)})
of the
j
-th copy of
V_{P}
in the splitting. Then, by the transversality, the zero locus
M^{\prime}:=s^{-1}(0)\subset M
is a smooth closed manifold of dimension
(m-Ni)
with an isomorphism

(4.126)

\displaystyle\psi|_{TM^{\prime}}\colon TM^{\prime}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{L}

\displaystyle\simeq V_{P}^{(1)}\oplus V_{P}^{(2)}\oplus V_{P}^{(3)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{P}^{(j-1)}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{m+L-Nij}.

This equips
M^{\prime}
with a tangential
\left(\mathcal{K}(i),(j-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)
-structure.

Proposition 4.127
.
20
20

20

See Remark
4.75
.

The map (
4.123
) is given by

(4.128)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot\colon\left([M,P,\psi]\in\Omega_{m}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i),j\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)}\right)\mapsto\left([M^{\prime},P_{M^{\prime}},\psi|_{TM^{\prime}}]\in\Omega_{m-Ni}^{\left(\mathcal{K}(i),(j-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{K}(i)}\right)}\right),

where the right hand side is the element just explained above.

Proof.

This is the direct consequence of applying the Pontryagin-Thom construction to the map (
4.115
).
∎

Example 4.129

(The case of
j=1
)
.

The case of
j=1
is of particular importance for us, especially in relation to Euler numbers and topological elliptic genera (Corollary
4.153
below).
In this case, the restriction map in (
4.114
) becomes

(4.130)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot\colon MT\mathcal{K}(i)\to S[Ni],

resulting in the map of bordism groups in Proposition
20

(4.131)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot\colon\Omega_{m}^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\to\Omega_{m-Ni}^{fr},

where
\Omega_{*}^{\mathrm{fr}}
is the stably framed bordism group. In particular, if we set
m=Ni
, we have

Claim 4.132
.
21
21

21

See Remark
4.75
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold with a
strict
tangential
\mathcal{K}(i)
-structure
\psi
(Definition
2.118
—so that in particular
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=Ni
).
Then the map (
4.131
) for
m=Ni
,

(4.133)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{\mathcal{K}(i)})\cdot\colon\Omega_{Ni}^{\mathcal{K}(i)}\to\Omega_{0}^{fr}=\mathbb{Z},

maps the element
[M,\psi]
to its Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
20
. The procedure in that proposition, applied to this case, produces the formula expressing the Euler number of
M
in terms of vanishing points of generic vector fields.
∎

Remark 4.134
.

The strictness assumption in Claim
21
is essential. Indeed, recall Example
2.136
, where we intrduced two distinct tangential
Spin(k)
-structures on
S^{k}
: the one is the
stable
tangential
Spin(k)
-structure
\mathfrak{s}_{\rm BB}^{Spin(k)}
which is given by the blackboard framing, and the other is the
strict
tangential
Spin(k)
-structure
\mathfrak{s}_{\rm str}^{Spin(k)}
.

Let
k
be an even integer. We already know that
\mathrm{Euler}(S^{k})=2
. So Claim
21
applied here implies that

(4.135)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{Spin(k)})\cdot[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}_{\rm str}^{Spin(k)}]=2\in\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{fr}}=\mathbb{Z}.

On the other hand, since we already know that
[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}_{\rm BB}^{Spin(k)}]=0\in\Omega_{2k}^{Spin(k)}
, we have

(4.136)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{Spin(k)})\cdot[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}_{\rm str}^{Spin(k)}]=0.

This is not a contradiction, since we have
[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}_{\rm str}^{Spin(k)}]\neq[S^{k},\mathfrak{s}_{\rm BB}^{Spin(k)}]
in
\Omega^{Spin(k)}_{k}
.
However, after stabilization those two tangential
Spin
-structures become bordant to each other. This example shows that our topological Elliptic genera are sensitive to
unstable
information.

Remark 4.137
.

The restriction map (
4.123
) can be regarded as a variant of the
Landweber-Novikov operations

[
Lan67
]
,
[
Nov67
]
on bordism homology theories. In general, for a multiplicative
B\mathcal{K}\to BO
, given a map of the form
\gamma\colon\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathcal{K}_{+}\to M\mathcal{K}[d]
, by the universal Thom isomorphism for
\mathcal{K}
-bundles, we can canonically associate an
M\mathcal{K}
-module morphism
lf(\gamma)\colon M\mathcal{K}\to M\mathcal{K}[d]
, which is called the Landweber-Novikov operation associated to
\gamma
.

One concrete relation which we will use in our analysis of examples in Section
7.1
is the following, concerning the case of
j=1
explained in Example
4.129
above. Let
\mathcal{K}
be one of
U,SU,O,Spin,Sp
.
Denote by
\overline{e}_{i}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}B\mathcal{K}_{+}\to M\mathcal{K}[Ni]
the characteristic class which assigns

(4.138)

\displaystyle\overline{e}_{i}(\xi)=e_{i}(-\xi)\in M\mathcal{K}^{Ni}(X),

for an
\mathcal{K}
-vector bundle
\xi
over
X
, where
\{e_{l}\}_{l=1}^{\infty}
is the restriction of the standard generators
\{E_{l}\}_{l=1}^{\infty}
of the
MU
,
MO
,
MSp
-characteristic classes in, e.g.,
[
Lan67
, (4.1)]
.

.

The following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{MT\mathcal{K}(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}:=\chi(V_{H(i)})\cdot}
(
4.130
)
\textstyle{S[Ni]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{MT\mathcal{K}\simeq M\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{lf({\overline{e}_{i}})}
\textstyle{M\mathcal{K}[Ni].}

The proof is straightforward by comparing the definitions of two horizontal arrows.

The compatibility of the topological elliptic genera with the internal structure maps

Now we proceed to show that our topological elliptic genera are compatible with the internal structure maps introduced above.

Proposition 4.144

.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
be any one of
(U,SU),(Sp,Sp)\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\displaystyle,(O,Spin)$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\textstyle,(O,Spin)$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\scriptstyle,(O,Spin)$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{conjcolor}$\scriptscriptstyle,(O,Spin)$}}
.

\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{H}(k-1),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k-1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(k-1)V_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{G}(n)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{H}(k),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{H}(k)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}}

The compatibility with the stabilization maps immediately implies, for example, that the

-and

) and (

Before proving Proposition

, we deduce an important corollary of this proposition, which relates Euler numbers and topological elliptic genera. This is important in Section

, where we deduce interesting divisibility results of Euler numbers by way of our topological elliptic genera.

Corollary 4.153

(The restriction of

.

be any one of

,

, and

Remark 4.159
.

As in Remark

, the strictness assumption in the second statement is essential.

Proof of Corollary

.

.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to proving Proposition

.
It is in fact an easy corollary of the following proposition, which we also use in Section

on the level-rank duality.

Proposition 4.160
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
be one of
(U,SU)
,
(Sp,Sp)

,
(O,Spin)
. Consider the following diagram in

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(n-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}-Nk]^{\mathcal{H}(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{H}(k)})}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}]^{\mathcal{H}(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}^{\mathcal{H}(k-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k-1)}]^{\mathcal{H}(k-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}+Nk]^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{G}(n)}^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(k-1)V_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{G}(n)})}

4.3.

It is the universal version
22

22

.
of what was called the
