5.
The character formula

Note: The contents of Sections
5
,
6
, and
7

can be read independently of each other, and the reader may find it useful to skip to their section of interest.

In this section, we deduce the integration formula for the composition

(5.1)

\displaystyle e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}MT(H,\tau_{H})\to\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq_character}}\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}|_{\deg=\bullet},

producing
G
-equivariant integral Modular Forms (Definition
2.55
).
We first derive the general formula in Proposition
5.11
, and specialize to the case of the
U
-and
Sp
-topological elliptic genera in Section
4
to derive the concrete formula (Proposition
5.23
, Proposition
5.35
). In this section, we always assume that
G
and
H
are connected and
\pi_{1}G
and
\pi_{1}H
are torsion-free
.

Remark 5.2
.

The formula we produce in this section is written in terms of functions in variables
z_{i}
and
x_{j}
. In this subsection, we always use the convention that
z_{i}
are associated to
G
and
x_{j}
are associated to
H
. They are defined after the choice of the maximal torus, and play the following a priori two distinct roles;

•

variables of equivariant Modular Forms as explained in Definition
2.55
,

•

generators of the ordinary cohomology ring of the maximal torus.

The two are canonically identified by the map (
5.17
), but keeping track of the equivalence is essential in the following.

Recall that
\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}
is defined using the
coevaluation map
in Definition
3.47
,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\phi})\cdot}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\phi}]^{G\times H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}.

and used the canonical pairing between
\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}
and
MT(H,\tau_{H})
.
At this point, it is convenient to convert (
5.3
) into equivariant Modular Forms.
For simplicity, let us from now on assume at least one of
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\tau_{G}]^{G}
or
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\tau_{H}]^{H}
are flat over
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}
23
23

23

The formula in this section still works without this assumption. This assumption is satisfied for all the cases of the groups of our interest as we remarked in Remark
2.23
.

We have (here we denote
\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V]^{G}:=\mathrm{MF}[V]^{G}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and so on)

(5.4)

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})\colon\mathrm{MF}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{V_{\phi}}\cdot}\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[V_{\phi}]^{G\times H}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\mathrm{res}_{G}^{G\times H}\tau_{G}+\mathrm{res}_{H}^{G\times H}\tau_{H}]^{G\times H}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\tau_{H}]^{H}.

Here, we have used
e(\chi(V))=\Phi_{V}
in (
2.65
) for the first arrow. For the second, recall that we have defined the ring of equivariant Modular Forms so that we have the
equality
between the source and target. The third equality uses
The second arrow in (
5.3
) is converted into this equality because of Fact
2.104

(
3
).

To get the integration formula in terms of characteristic polynomials, we need to translate the
\mathrm{TMF}
-valued characteristic classes to the rational ordinary cohomology. In our case, we denote the Chern-Dold character map for
\mathrm{TMF}
by

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHD}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\to H\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}},

where
H\mathrm{MF}^{\mathbb{Q}}
is the ordinary cohomology theory with coefficients in the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded abelian group
\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Since we are assuming
H
is connected, the element
\tau_{H}\in RO(H)
is equipped with an (
SO
-)orientation
\mathfrak{o}
.
Then we have the composition

(5.7)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{CHD}}H^{-*}(BH^{-\tau_{H}};\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda(\tau_{H},\mathfrak{o})}}{{\simeq}}H^{-*+\dim\tau_{H}}(BH;\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}),

where we denote by
\lambda(\tau_{H},\mathfrak{o})
the Thom isomorphism in the ordinary cohomology induced by the orientation
\mathfrak{o}
.
Furthermore, if
H
is connected, we choose a maximal torus
U(1)^{r}\simeq T\subset H
with the Weyl group
W
to identify

(5.8)

\displaystyle H^{-*+\dim\tau_{H}}(BH;\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq H^{-*+\dim\tau_{H}}(BU(1)^{r};\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}})^{W}\simeq\left.\left(\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{r}]]\right)^{W}\right|_{\deg=*-\dim\tau_{H}},

where we have used the convention that
x\in H^{2}(BU(1);\mathbb{Q})
denotes the Thom class of the fundamental representation
V_{U(1)}
.
We denote the composition of (
5.7
) and (
5.8
) by

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{H}\colon\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}\to\left.\left(\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{r}]]\right)^{W}\right|_{\deg=*-\dim\tau_{H}}

Since the second arrow in (
5.7
) canonically factors through
\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}
, we can also define

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{H}\colon\left.\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}\right|_{\deg=*}\to\left.\left(\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{r}]]\right)^{W}\right|_{\deg=*-\dim\tau_{H}}

so that we have
\mathfrak{K}_{H}=\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{H}\circ e
.
Now we can state the general characteristic class formula.

Proposition 5.11

(Characteristic class formula for
e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}
)
.

The characteristic polynomial associated to
e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}MT(H,\tau_{H})\to\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}|_{\deg=\bullet}
is given by

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}}\otimes\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{H}\right)\Phi_{V_{\phi}}\in\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes\left(\mathbb{Q}[[x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}]]\right)^{W},

where we are regarding
\Phi_{V_{\phi}}
as an element in
\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{MF}}\mathrm{MF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}
.

Proof.

A priori, the characteristic class is obtained by the formula

\left(e\otimes\mathfrak{K}_{H}\right)\circ\eqref{eq_CHD1}.

Converting the equivariant Modular Forms from the beginning and using (
5.8
), we get the result.
∎

Let us work out how
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{H}
looks like. First we work on the most fundamental case where
H=U(1)
and
\tau_{H}=nV_{U(1)}
for an integer

. The Thom isomorphism in the ordinary cohomology is identified as follows.

\textstyle{H^{*}(BU(1)^{-nV_{U(1)}};\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(nV_{U(1)},\mathfrak{o})}
\textstyle{H^{*}(BU;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x^{-n}\mathbb{Q}[[x]]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{n}\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}[[x]]}

The Chern-Dold character map is simply taking the formal expansion at the origin of the elliptic curves,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHD}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[nV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\xrightarrow{}\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{O}(ne))\xhookrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}{}_{\mathbb{C}}}}x^{-n}\mathrm{MF}^{\mathbb{C}}[[x]]=H^{*}(BU(1)^{-nV_{U(1)}};\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{C}}).

where
x
is regarded as the coordinate of the elliptic curve (which we had been denoted as
z
, but here we intentionally use a different letter).
On the other hand, if we were to factor through Jacobi Forms, we should include the multiplication by the Theta function

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHD}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[nV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\xrightarrow{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}\mathrm{JF}_{n}\xrightarrow{a(x,\tau)^{-n}}\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{O}(ne))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}{}_{\mathbb{C}}}}x^{-n}\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{C}}[[x]],

by Lemma
2.59
. Thus, composing the latter two arrows of (
5.18
) and (
5
), the map
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{U(1)}
(
5.10
) in this case becomes the composition

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{U(1)}\colon\mathrm{MF}[nV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}=\mathrm{JF}_{n}\xrightarrow{\cdot\left(\frac{x}{a(x,\tau)}\right)^{n}}\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[x]].

In the case
H=SU(k)
, we follow the conventional approach that, rather than using the maximal torus of

, first regard

and use the maximal torus

to identify

\displaystyle H^{*}(BSU(k);\mathbb{Q})=\left(\mathbb{Q}[[x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{k}]]/(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{k})\right)^{\Sigma_{k}}.

Then the map

for

becomes (see Example

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}_{SU(k)}=\cdot\prod_{1\leq j\leq k}\left(\frac{x_{j}}{a(x_{j},\tau)}\right)^{k}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\quad\colon\mathrm{MF}[nV_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}\xrightarrow{\otimes\mathbb{Q}}\left(\frac{\bigotimes^{\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}}_{1\leq j\leq k}\mathrm{JF}_{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}}{(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{k})}\right)^{\Sigma_{k}}\to\left(\frac{\mathrm{MF}_{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}]]}{(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{k})}\right)^{\Sigma_{k}}

where the tensor product is formed over the graded ring
\mathrm{MF}
.

Proposition 5.23

(The formula for the characteristic polynomial of
e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{SU(k)_{n}}
)
.

The characteristic polynomial

\mathcal{K}_{U(n)_{k}}\in H^{*}(BSU(k);\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathrm{MF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}

of the composition

is given by the formula

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{U(n)_{k}}\left(\{x_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq k}\right)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq k}\frac{x_{j}\theta(z_{i}+x_{j},q)}{\theta(x_{j},q)}

(5.25)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i}e^{z_{i}/2}\right)^{k}\cdot\left(\prod_{j}\frac{x_{j}}{1-e^{-x_{j}}}\right)^{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 1,\ i,j\end{subarray}}\frac{(1-q^{m}e^{z_{i}+x_{j}})(1-q^{m-1}e^{-z_{i}-x_{j}})}{(1-q^{m}e^{x_{j}})(1-q^{m}e^{-x_{j}})}.

Here,
\{z_{i}\}_{i}
are the variables of
U(n)
-equivariant Modular Forms given by the canonical choice of the maximal torus
U(1)^{n}\hookrightarrow U(n)
, and
\{x_{j}\}_{j}
are the variables of
H^{*}(BSU(k);\mathbb{Q})
in (
5.20
).

Proof.

We have

(5.26)

\displaystyle\Phi_{V_{U(n)}\otimes V_{SU(k)}}=\prod_{i,j}a(z_{i}+x_{j})\in\mathrm{MF}[V_{U(n)}\otimes V_{SU(k)}]^{U(n)\times SU(k)}

where we recall that
a(z,\tau)=\Phi_{V_{U(1)}}(z,\tau)=\theta(z,\tau)/\eta(\tau)^{3}
(
1.67
).
The formula in Proposition
5.11
becomes

(5.27)

\displaystyle\prod_{j}\left(\frac{x_{j}}{a(x_{j},\tau)}\right)^{k}\cdot\prod_{i,j}a(z_{i}+x_{j},\tau)=\prod_{1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq k}\frac{x_{j}\theta(z_{i}+x_{j},q)}{\theta(x_{j},q)}.

∎

By Proposition
5.23
we get the following integration formula for the character of the
U
-topological elliptic genera,

Corollary 5.28

(The integration formula for
e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{U(n)_{k}}
)
.

For
[M,\psi]\in\Omega_{m}^{(BSU(k),nV_{SU(k)})}
we have

(5.29)

\displaystyle e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{U(n)_{k}}[M,\psi]=\left(\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}y_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{k}\cdot\int_{M}\mathrm{Todd}(TM)^{n}\wedge\mathrm{Ch}\left(\otimes_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{TM}_{q,y_{i}}\right),

where we used the variable
y_{i}=e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}z_{i}}
, and set (in the formula below all the tensor/exterior products are over
\mathbb{C}
, )

(5.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{TM}_{q,y}:=\bigotimes_{m\geq 0}\wedge_{-q^{m}y^{-1}}T^{*}M\otimes\bigotimes_{m\geq 1}\wedge_{-q^{m}y}TM\otimes\bigotimes_{m\geq 1}\mathrm{Sym}_{q^{m}}T^{*}M\otimes\bigotimes_{m\geq 1}\mathrm{Sym}_{q^{m}}TM.

When
n=1
, this specializes to the formula (
1.1
) for the classical elliptic genera
\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}
of tangential
SU(k)
-manifolds.

Remark 5.31

(Comparison with other literatures)
.

In some literatures including
[
AFG08
]
and
[
Tot00
]
, the elliptic genus for a tangential
SU(k)
-manifold
M
is defined to be

(5.32)

\displaystyle a^{-k}\cdot\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M)\in\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{O}(ke)\otimes\omega^{\bullet}).

This is because they define elliptic genera as a map from the stable
SU
-bordism group. See Section
4.3
.

The character formula for the
Sp
-topological elliptic genus directly folllows from the above result on the
U
-topological elliptic genus. This is because the map of equivariant Modular Forms,

(5.33)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(n)}^{U(n)}}\colon\mathrm{MF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}\to\mathrm{MF}[2kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}

is an injection, as we have seen in Example
2.72
: the
Sp(n)
-equivariant Modular Forms are the
U(n)
-equivariant Modular Forms even in all the variables
z_{i}
.
By the above injectivity and the functoriality of the topological elliptic genera with respect to the external structure map, as in Proposition
4.38
, we see that the composition

(5.34)

\displaystyle e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(n)_{k}}\colon MT(Sp(k),n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)})\to\mathrm{MF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}

is simply given by retaining the
SU(2k)
-structure underlying the
Sp(k)
-structure and applying the formula we have obtained for the
U
-topological elliptic genus.
Thus we get the following.

Proposition 5.35

(The formulas for
e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(n)_{k}}
)
.

The restriction along
Sp(k)\hookrightarrow SU(2k)
of the element
\mathcal{K}_{U(2n)_{k}}
in Proposition
5.23
is contained in
H^{*}(BSp(k);\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathrm{MF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]
, which gives the characteristic polynomial of the composition (
5.34
),

(5.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Sp(n)_{k}}=\mathrm{res}_{SU(2k)}^{Sp(k)}\mathcal{K}_{U(n)_{k}}\in H^{*}(BSp(k);\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathrm{MF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}\subset H^{*}(BSU(2k);\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathrm{MF}[2kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}

Here, we have used the injectivity of (
5.33
).
In particular, for
[M,\psi]\in\Omega_{m}^{(BSp(k),nV_{Sp(k)})}
, the integration formula for
e\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(n)_{k}}[M,\psi]
is simply given by retaining the
SU(2k)
-structure underlying the
Sp(k)
-structure and applying the formula (
5.29
).

6.
Level-rank duality isomorphisms in
\mathrm{TMF}

As explained in Section
3.2
, in the general settings there, we get a composition of
\mathrm{TMF}
-module morphisms

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{\phi})}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{\phi}]^{G\times H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta_{\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{H}]^{H}.

In the setting of the trio we presented in Section
4
, we expect the above map to be related to the
level-rank duality
in physics. While initially discovered in the context of affine Lie algebras and conformal field theory
[
Fre06
,
NT92
]
, the level-rank duality can be formulated in the closely related frameworks of Chern-Simons theories
[
NRS90
,
MNRS91
,
NS07
,
HS16
]
and tensor categories
[
OS14
,
ORS20
]
.
In this section, we verify mathematically that, indeed among our trio, in the cases of
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})=(U,SU)
and
(Sp,Sp)
, the map
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
exhibits the duality in
\mathrm{TMF}
-module spectra:
24
24

24

The authors acknowledge Du Pei and Lennart Meier for providing the idea of the contents in this section.

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny dual}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)}]^{Sp(k)}

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny dual }}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}\quad\mbox{in }\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}.

Remark 6.4
.

In this article, we do not go further into the level-rank duality itself, especially with physical explorations, though the authors certainly encourage explorations in this direction. Nevertheless, we include the relevant mathematical proofs here, since these results show that our generalized topological elliptic genera are highly nontrivial.

We heavily use the following fact, which will appear in an upcoming paper by Gepner-Meier
[
GM
]
:

Fact 6.5

(Gepner-Meier,
[
GM
]
)
.

(1)

For any positive integer
k
, the restriction map provides an isomorphism,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{SU(k)}^{e}\colon\mathrm{TMF}^{SU(k)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{Sp(k)}^{e}\colon\mathrm{TMF}^{Sp(k)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}

(2)

For any positive integer
k
, the restriction map along
\det\colon U(k)\to U(1)
provides an isomorphism,

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}^{U(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{res}_{\det}}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e},\mathrm{tr}_{U(1)}^{e})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}\oplus\mathrm{TMF}[1].

The rest of this section is organized as follows. In Section
6.1
and
6.2
, we show the duality statement for
(Sp,Sp)
and
(SU,U)
, respectively. Section
6.3
is devoted to the proof of a general lemma on the duality in symmetric monoidal categories, which we use in the proofs of main theorems in the earlier subsections.

6.1.
The level-rank duality between
Sp(n)_{k}
and
Sp(k)_{n}

First, we analyze the case of
\mathcal{D}=Sp(n)_{k}
, where the argument is simpler than the case of
\mathcal{D}=U(n)_{k}
.
We show the following.

Theorem 6.9

(The level-rank duality between
Sp(n)_{k}
and
Sp(k)_{n}
)
.

Let
n
and
k
be nonnegative integers. The coevaluation map in Definition
3.47
applied to the data
\mathcal{D}=Sp(n)_{k}
in Definition
4.1
,

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{Sp(n)_{k}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}V^{*}_{Sp(k)})}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}V^{*}_{Sp(k)}]^{Sp(n)\times Sp(k)}

(6.11)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta_{Sp(n)_{k}},\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)}]^{Sp(k)},

exhibits

\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}
and
\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)}]^{Sp(k)}
as duals to each other in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
,

Proof.

We prove the theorem by induction. First, as the base step, we check that the statement holds when either one of
n
or
k
is
0
; but this is simply implied by Fact
6.5
(1), (
6.7
).

Now, recall the following diagram for
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
in (
4.160
) specialized to our case.

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{Sp(n-1)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{Sp(n)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{Sp(n)_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(n-1)\overline{V}_{Sp(k)}-4k]^{Sp(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{Sp(k)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{Sp(k)}]^{Sp(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{Sp(k-1)}]^{Sp(k-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n-1)}+4k]^{Sp(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(k-1)V_{Sp(n)}]^{Sp(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{Sp(n)})\cdot}

Here the second and third rows are the stabilization-restriction fiber sequences in Proposition
4.78
.
By Proposition
4.160
, both the left and the right half of the diagram (
6.2
) is
compatible
, in the sense of Section
1.1
(
11
).
Using this result and a general Lemma
6.44
below, we prove the statement of Theorem by induction on
n
, and within that, we induct on
k
. The base case
n=0
has already been checked above.

Now, suppose that we have verified the claim for all
(n,k)\in[0,N-1]\times[0,\infty)
. Then let us set
n=N
, and prove the statement inductively in
k\geq 0
. The base case
k=0
has already been checked above. Assuming the case for
(n,k)=(N,K-1)
is proven, we apply Lemma
6.44
to the compatible diagram
6.2
, we get the desired statement for the case
(n,k)=(N,K)
.
This finishes the inductive step and completes the proof of Theorem
6.9
.
∎

6.2.
The level-rank duality between
U(n)_{k}
and
SU(k)_{n}

We now move on to the case of
\mathcal{D}=U(n)_{k}
.
The inductive strategy is exactly the same as the case of
\mathcal{D}=Sp(n)_{k}
proved above, but the verification of the base case is a little more complicated.

Before proceeding to prove the duality between
\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}
and
\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}
for general
n
and
k
, we start by proving the extreme case, which will be used as a part of base step in our inductive proof of the general statement (Theorem
6.35
).

Proposition 6.18

(The level-rank duality between
U(n)
and
SU(1)=e
)
.

Let
n
be a nonnegative integer. The map

(6.19)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{U(n)})\cdot\colon\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
.

Proof.

We already know the case of
n=1
by Appendix
A.2
. The stabilization-restriction fiber sequence in Proposition
4.78
looks like

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}_{e}^{U(1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[2]}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[1]\oplus\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot}

i.e., split at
\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}
, and the third vertical arrow provides the isomorphism claimed in the proposition for
n=1
.

Now, for each integer
n\geq 2
, consider the inclusion of the standard maximal torus
\iota_{n}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\hookrightarrow U(n)
where we denoted
\mathbb{T}:=U(1)
. It induces the restriction map

(6.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\iota_{n}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\to\mathrm{TMF}\left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{\mathbb{T}_{i}}\right]^{\mathbb{T}^{n}}

Here, the we indicated the
i
-th copy of
\mathbb{T}
in the group
\mathbb{T}^{n}
by
\mathbb{T}_{i}
.
The following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(n)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathbb{T}})^{\otimes n}\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\iota_{n}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{\mathbb{T}_{i}}\right]^{\mathbb{T}^{n}}}

and the right vertical arrow is an isomorphism because of the statement of the proposition already checked for
n=1
above (i.e., (
6.2
)). Thus, to prove the proposition, it is enough to prove that (
6.24
) is an isomorphism. We prove it by induction on
n
.

The base case
n=1
is trivial. So assume that (
6.24
) for
n-1
is isomorphism. We use the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}^{U(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(n)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\simeq\text{\tiny by Fact }\ref{factSU}(2)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\det}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\iota_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(n)}^{U(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(n-1)}+2]^{U(n-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\iota_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}^{\mathbb{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq\text{\tiny by \eqref{seq_TJF1}}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathbb{T}})^{\otimes(n-1)}\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n-1}V_{\mathbb{T}_{i}}\right]^{\mathbb{T}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathbb{T}})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{\mathbb{T}_{i}}\right]^{\mathbb{T}^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}^{\mathbb{T}^{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n-1}V_{\mathbb{T}_{i}}+2\right]^{\mathbb{T}^{n-1}}}

The top row is a fiber sequence by Proposition
4.78
, and the bottom row is also a fiber sequence by tensoring
\mathrm{TMF}\left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n-1}V_{\mathbb{T}_{i}}\right]^{\mathbb{T}^{n-1}}
to the sequence (
6.2
).
The left vertical arrows are equivalences as indicated.
The right vertical arrow is an equivalence by the induction hypothesis. Thus the middle vertical arrow is an equivalence, and this completes the inductive step for
n
. This completes the proof of Proposition
6.18
.

∎

Theorem 6.35

(The level-rank duality between
U
and
SU
)
.

Let
n
and
k
be integers with
n\geq 0
and
k\geq 1
. Then

\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}
and
\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}
are duals to each other in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
, and the coevaluation map in Definition
3.47
applied to the data
\mathcal{D}=U(n)_{k}
in Definition
4.1
,

(6.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{U(n)_{k}}\colon\mathrm{TMF}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\chi(V_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)})}\mathrm{TMF}[V_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{SU(k)}]^{U(n)\times SU(k)}

(6.37)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma(\Theta_{U(n)_{k}},\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)},

is the coevaluation map of the duality.

Proof.

We use an inductive argument, which is exactly parallel to the proof of Theorem
6.9
.
For this case, we use the following diagram for
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
in (
4.160
) specialized to our case.

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{U(n-1)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{U(n)_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}_{U(n)_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(n-1)\overline{V}_{SU(k)}-2k]^{SU(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{SU(k)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k)}]^{SU(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[n\overline{V}_{SU(k-1)}]^{SU(k-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n-1)}+2k]^{U(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[(k-1)V_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(n)})\cdot}

Here the second and third rows are the stabilization-restriction fiber sequences in Proposition
4.78
.
By Proposition
4.160
, both the left and the right half of the diagram (
6.2
) is
compatible
, in the sense of Section
1.1
(
11
).
Using this result and a general Lemma
6.44
below, we prove the desired statement by induction on
n
, and within that, we induct on
k
. The base case
n=0
easily follows by Fact
6.5
.

Now, suppose that we have verified the claim for all
(n,k)\in[0,N-1]\times[1,\infty)
. Then let us set
n=N
, and prove the statement inductively in
k\geq 1
. The base case
k=1
is done by Proposition
6.18
. Assuming the case for
(n,k)=(N,K-1)
is proven, we apply Lemma
6.44
to the compatible diagram
6.2
, we get the desired statement for the case
(n,k)=(N,K)
.
This finishes the inductive step, and completes the proof of Theorem
6.35
.

∎

6.3.
A lemma on duality

Here, we prove a general lemma which was used in our inductive proof of Theorem
6.9
and Theorem
6.35
above.

Lemma 6.44
.

Let
R
be an
E_{\infty}
ring spectrum, and suppose that we are given two fiber sequences in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
,

(6.45)

\displaystyle a_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha}a_{2}\xrightarrow{\beta}a_{3},

(6.46)

\displaystyle b_{1}\xleftarrow{\gamma}b_{2}\xleftarrow{\delta}b_{3}.

Assume that
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
are dual to each other in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
for
i=1
and
3
, with coevaluation maps

(6.47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{coev}_{i}\colon R\to a_{i}\otimes_{R}b_{i},\ i=1,3.

Furthermore, assume that we are given a morphism

(6.48)

\displaystyle f\colon R\to a_{2}\otimes_{R}b_{2}

with which both the left and the right half of the following diagram

\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{coev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{coev}_{3}}
\textstyle{a_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{a_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{a_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes_{R}}
\textstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{b_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{b_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}

is compatible in the sense of Section
1.1
(
11
).
Then
a_{2}
and
b_{2}
are duals to each other (in particular they are dualizable), and
f
is the coevaluation map associated to the duality.

Proof.

Since we know that
b_{1}
and
b_{3}
are dualizable and we have a fiber sequence (
6.46
), we conclude that
b_{2}
is also dualizable. For dualizable objects
x
, let us denote its dual by
D_{R}(x)
and use notation
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}(1,x\otimes_{R}y)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{R}}(D_{R}(x),y),\ g\mapsto g^{\prime}
.
Then the compatibility of the diagram (
6.44
) is equivalent to the commutativity of the following diagram.

\textstyle{D_{R}(b_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{coev}_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{D_{R}(b_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{D_{R}(b_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{coev}_{3}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{a_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{a_{3}}

Since the rows are fiber sequences and
\mathrm{coev}_{i}^{\prime}
are equivalences for
i=1,3
, we see that
f^{\prime}
is an equivalence, exhibiting the duality between
a_{2}
and
b_{2}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
6.44
.
∎

7.
Applications

In the Introduction, we explained why we can expect our genuinely equivariant topological elliptic genera to be more interesting than the classical elliptic genera.
In this section, we give examples to show that all the expected interesting phenomena listed there indeed happen.

7.1.
The first interesting example: the detection of the
2
-torsion in
\pi_{5}MSp

First, we give the easiest example which illustrates the various interesting aspects of our topological elliptic genera. Specifically, we construct an example which simultaneously realizes the following items in the Introduction: (1) detecting torsions, (3) detecting unstable elements, and (4) detecting the difference between
Sp
and
SU
.

The manifold we consider is the standard generator
\mu_{1}
of
\pi_{5}MSp

[
Ray72
]

[
Ale72
]
. It is represented by a
5
-sphere
S^{5}
equipped with a nontrivial tangential
Sp(1)=SU(2)
-structure as follows.
Recall that we have
\pi_{5}BSp(1)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
. Take a representative
P\colon S^{5}\to BSp(1)
in the nontrivial class. We know that the composition

J\circ P\colon S^{5}\to BO
is nullhomotopic, with a unique nullhomotopy
\psi
up to homotopy. We trivialize the stable tangent bundle of
S^{5}
in the usual way, so that the triple
(S^{5},P,\psi)
is a tangential
Sp(1)=SU(2)
-manifold.

Definition 7.1
.

We define
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\in\pi_{5}MTSp(1)=\pi_{5}MTSU(2)
to be the element represented by the triple
(S^{5},P,\psi)
above.

Let us consider the following commutative diagram.

\textstyle{MSU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{\infty}}}
\textstyle{MTSU(2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}_{MTSU}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{2}}}
\textstyle{MTSp(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}_{MTSp}}
\textstyle{MSp\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{\infty}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{\infty}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}_{\mathrm{TJF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq\ \mbox{\tiny by Cor.}\ref{cor_TEJF2=TJF2}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}_{\mathrm{TEJF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}}

Here, four of the horizontal arrows are stabilization maps in the internal structure of the trio.
Let us investigate the images of
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}
in the
\pi_{5}
of each component of the diagram (
7.1
).
This element is known to show an interesting behavior in the bordism groups in the upper row, as follows.

Fact 7.6

(Bordism classes of images of
\widetilde{\mu_{1}}
)
.

(7.7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\neq 0

\displaystyle\in\pi_{5}MTSp(1)=\pi_{5}MTSU(2)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2,

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}:=\mathrm{stab}_{MTSp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{1})\neq 0

\displaystyle\in\pi_{5}MSp\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2,

(7.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{stab}_{MTSU}(\widetilde{\mu}_{1})=0

\displaystyle\in\pi_{5}MSU=0

The goal of this subsection is to show that all the vertical arrows in (
7.1
) are injective, so that the topological elliptic genera exactly detect this behavior (Proposition
7.18
and Corollary
7.24
).
First, the bottom row of (
7.1
) is understood as follows.

Proposition 7.10
.

(1)

The following restriction map is an isomorphism.

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{e}\colon\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\to\pi_{1}\mathrm{TMF}=\eta\cdot\pi_{0}\mathrm{MF}/(2\eta).

In this subsection, we denote by
\widehat{\eta}{}\in\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}
the unique element that maps to
\eta
under the isomorphism (
7.11
).

(2)

We have

(7.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}{}\notin\ker\left(\mathrm{stab}_{\mathrm{TEJF}}\colon\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\to\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}\right).

(3)

We have
\pi_{5}\mathrm{TJF}_{\infty}=0
.

Proof.

(1) follows from
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}/\nu
in (
B.64
).
For (2), we use Proposition
B.34
in Appendix which gives an identification

(7.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}[-4],\quad\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{\infty}[-4],

By this identification, the stabilization map
\mathrm{stab}_{\mathrm{TEJF}}\colon\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\to\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}
corresponds to the map induced by the inclusion
i\colon\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{HP}^{\infty}
.
Consider the following commutative diagram.

\textstyle{\eta\cdot\pi_{0}\mathrm{MF}/(2\eta)=\pi_{1}\mathrm{TMF}}
\textstyle{\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{e}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}_{\mathrm{TEJF}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\eta/(2\eta)=\pi_{1}\mathrm{tmf}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\Delta^{-24}]}
\textstyle{\pi_{5}\mathrm{tmf}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{2}[-4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{HP}^{2}\to S^{8})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{HP}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{HP}^{\infty})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{[\Delta^{-24}]}
\textstyle{\pi_{5}\mathrm{tmf}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{\infty}[-4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\Delta^{-24}]}

Here, the top left horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by (1) of this proposition, proved above. So the bottom left horizontal arrow is also an isomorphism. Moreover, the bottom right horizontal arrow is also an isomorphism, because the map
\mathbb{HP}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{HP}^{\infty}
is
10
-connected. The vertical arrows are injective.
By this diagram and the definition of
\widehat{\eta}{}\in\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}
, we get (2).
(3) follows from
\pi_{5}\mathrm{TJF}_{3}=0
which is easily checked by (
A.35
). This completes the proof of Proposition

.
∎

We can now specify the images of
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}
in the bottom row of (

).

Proposition 7.18
.

We have

(7.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{1}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{1})=\widehat{\eta}{}\in\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\simeq\widehat{\eta}{}\cdot\pi_{0}\mathrm{MF}/(2\widehat{\eta}{}).

Proof.

We use Corollary
4.153
, which gives us the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{5}MTSp(1)\simeq\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{\mu}_{1}/(2\widetilde{\mu}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}=\chi(V_{Sp(1)})\cdot}
(
4.130
)
\textstyle{\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}=\widehat{\eta}{}\cdot\pi_{0}\mathrm{MF}/(2\widehat{\eta}{})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{e}\colon\widehat{\eta}{}\mapsto\eta}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}S\simeq\mathbb{Z}\eta/(2\eta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}\mathrm{TMF}=\eta\cdot\pi_{0}\mathrm{MF}/(2\eta)}

The right vertical arrow is an isomorphism by Proposition

(1), and maps
\widehat{\eta}{}
to
\eta
. The isomorphism in the upper left corner used Fact

.
Furthermore, we claim that the left vertical arrow maps

to

: this is not difficult to prove directly using Proposition

, but we may also use Claim

and the classical result in

]
that the corresponding Landweber-Novikov operation applied to

is the element
\eta\in\pi_{1}MSp
.
This means that the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism. This completes the proof of Proposition

.
∎

Corollary 7.24
.

We have

(7.25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{2}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{1})=\widehat{\eta}{}\neq 0

\displaystyle\in\pi_{5}\mathrm{TJF}_{2}\simeq\widehat{\eta}{}\cdot\pi_{0}\mathrm{MF}/(2\widehat{\eta}{}),

(7.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{\infty}}(\mu_{1})=\mathrm{stab}_{\mathrm{TEJF}}(\widehat{\eta}{})\neq 0

\displaystyle\in\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty},

(7.27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{\infty}}\circ\mathrm{stab}_{MTSU}(\widetilde{\mu}_{1})=0

\displaystyle\in\pi_{5}\mathrm{TJF}_{\infty}=0

In particular, all the vertical arrows of diagram
7.1
are injective.

Proof.

The three equalities follow from Proposition
7.18
, commutativity of the diagram (
7.1
) and Proposition
7.10
. The last claim uses Fact
7.6
.
∎

Remark 7.28

(The update on arXiv:v2)
.

In the arXiv version 1 of this article, we claimed the generalization of the results of this subsection to a family of
2
-torsion elements
\mu_{k}\in\pi_{8k-3}MSp
claimed to exist by the work of Alexander
[
Ale72
]
. However, we had not been aware that the original paper of Alexander contained an error and a correction was made in
[
Ale77
]
. Our previous analysis used the wrong construction of
Sp
-manifolds which actually do not work. Since the generalization was not essential for the main purpose of this paper, we chose to delete our analysis on the torsion elements in
\pi_{8k-3}MSp
. However, the authors believe that it is still interesting to analyze the images of topological elliptic genera for torsion elements in
\pi_{*}MSp
. We hope to adress it in future works.

7.2.
Divisibility constraints for Euler numbers

In this subsection, we present a major application of our topological elliptic genus: novel divisibility constraints on Euler numbers.
This corresponds to the items (2) and (5) in the Introduction.
The main result is Theorem
7.43
, the idea behind which is to use the relation with Euler numbers and
U
and
Sp
-topological elliptic genera as shown in Corollary
4.153
above.

7.2.1.
The divisibility constraints via the topological elliptic genera

Recall the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence in Proposition
4.78
for
U(1)
and
Sp(1)
(
4.98
), (
4.105
) :

\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[2k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x(k)\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[4k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y(k)\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-2}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}[1]}

Here we have defined

(7.33)

\displaystyle x(k)\in\pi_{2k-1}\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1},\quad y(k)\in\pi_{4k-1}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-2}

to be the element that specifies the cofibers of the restriction maps in (
7.2.1
). We call them
attaching element
(c.f., Examples
4.98
,
4.105
).
Let us introduce the following notations.

Definition 7.34
.

For each positive integer
k
, define
d_{SU}(k),d_{Sp}(k)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}
to be the order of the elements
x(k)
and
y(k)
in (
7.33
), respectively.

The integers
d_{SU}(k)
and
d_{Sp}(k)
capture information of the image of the first arrows in (
7.2.1
) as follows.

Proposition 7.35
.

For each positive integer
k
, we have the following.

(7.36)

\displaystyle d_{SU}(k)\cdot\mathbb{Z}=\mathrm{im}\left(\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}\colon\pi_{2k}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}\right)\bigcap\mathrm{im}\left(u\colon\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}\right),

(7.37)

\displaystyle d_{Sp}(k)\cdot\mathbb{Z}=\mathrm{im}\left(\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{e}\colon\pi_{4k}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}\right)\bigcap\mathrm{im}\left(u\colon\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}\right).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the fact that the sequences in (
7.2.1
) are fiber sequences.
∎

The following is the first main result of this subsection.

Theorem 7.38

(Genuine divisibility constraints of the Euler numbers)
.

Let
k
be any positive integer.

(1)

For any closed manifold
M
which admits a
strict
tangential
SU(k)
-structure (Definition
2.118
; in particular we necessarily have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=2k
), its Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
is divisible by
d_{SU}(k)
.

(2)

For any closed manifold
M
which admits a
strict
tangential
Sp(k)
-structure (so that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=4k
),
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
is divisible by
d_{Sp}(k)
.

Proof.

The proof is exacty the same for both (1) and (2). Let
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
be
(U,SU)
and
(Sp,Sp)
for the cases (1) and (2), and set
N=2,4
, respectively. Given an
Nk
-dimensional manifold
M
with a strict tangential
\mathcal{H}(k)
-structure
\psi
, by Corollary
4.153
we have

(7.39)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Euler}(M)=\mathrm{res}_{G(1)}^{e}\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{G(1)_{k}}[M,\psi]\in\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}.

In particular, we have

(7.40)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Euler}(M)\in\mathrm{im}\left(\mathrm{res}_{G(1)}^{e}\colon\pi_{Nk}\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{G(1)}]^{G(1)}\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}\right)\bigcap\mathrm{im}\left(u\colon\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}\right).

From this Proposition
7.35
, we get the desired result.
∎

This theorem allows us to deduce divisibility constraints of Euler numbers by analyzing the numbers
d_{\mathcal{H}}(k)
, which is purely a question about the trio of equivariant TMF.

T.Bauer has worked out the computation and determined the numbers
d_{\mathrm{Sp}}(k)
and
d_{\mathrm{SU}}(k)
. The results are summarized in Propositions
B.58
and
A.44
, and the proof will be given in
[
Bau
]
. Especially, we have

(7.41)

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{Sp}}(k)=\frac{24}{\gcd(k,24)}.

These results rely on the computation using spectral sequences.
However, actually we can give an elementary proof of the lower estimate (Proposition
B.55
)

(7.42)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{24}{\gcd(k,24)}\right|d_{Sp}(k).

This estimate is enough for the proof of the divisiblity result in Theorem
7.43
(1).
In order for the self-containedness, we include the proof of the result (
7.42
) in Appendix
B.3
.

Thus we get the following concrete divisibility results.

Theorem 7.43

(Concrete divisibility constraints on the Euler numbers)
.

(1)

Let
k
be any positive integer. For any closed manifold
M
which admits a strict tangential
Sp(k)
-structure (so that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=4k
), its Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
satisfies

(7.44)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{24}{\gcd(k,24)}\,\right|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

(2)

For any closed manifold
M
which admits a strict tangential
SU(k)
-structure, its Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
satisfies the following.

(a)

If
k=1
, we have
\mathrm{Euler}(M)=0
.

(b)

If
k=2
, we have
24\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M)
.

(c)

For
k\geq 2
, we have

(7.45)

\displaystyle\left.2^{\alpha(k)}\cdot 3^{\beta(k)}\,\right|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M)

with

(7.46)

\displaystyle\alpha(k)=\begin{cases}3&k\equiv 1,2,5\pmod{8}\\
2&k\equiv 6,7\pmod{8}\\
1&k\equiv 3,4\pmod{8},\\
0&k\equiv 0\pmod{8}.\end{cases}\quad\beta(k)=\begin{cases}1&k\equiv 1,2\pmod{3}\\
0&k\equiv 0\pmod{3}.\end{cases}

Proof.
25
25

25

The proof of Proposition
A.44
about the exact determination of the numbers
d_{SU}(k)
is deferred to the work by T.Bauer
[
Bau
]
in preparation. However, in this paper, we have provided some estimates, with self-contained proof, of the numbers
d_{SU}(k)
in Claim
A.49
. This implies the divisibility result in Theorem
7.43
(2) except for the case of
k\equiv 2
(mod
8
) with
k\geq 10
. For those cases, Claim
A.49
gives the estimate off by a factor of
2
compared to the estimate in Theorem
7.43
.

This is obtained by combining Theorem
7.38
, Propositions
B.55
and
A.44
.
∎

Remark 7.47

(
K3
and its Enriques involutions)
.

The divisibility for
Sp(1)=SU(2)
-manifolds is saturated by the Euler number of
K3
surfaces. A subset of
K3
surfaces enjoy certain fixed-point-free Enriques involutions, the quotients by which give the surfaces
\frac{1}{2}K3
.
While a
\frac{1}{2}K3
is not an
SU
-manifold (the Enriques quotient does not preserve the complex structure of
K3
) and hence outside the domain of (
1.1
), the formula (
1.2
) was originally formulated for almost complex manifolds, and when applied to
\frac{1}{2}K3
gives
\phi_{0,1}
. However, because
\mathrm{TJF}
is defined as the genuinely
U(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
, a local
U(1)
action coming from a nonintegrable almost complex structure of
\frac{1}{2}K3
is “not good enough”, and indeed
\phi_{0,1}
does not lie in the image of
e_{\mathrm{JF}}
given in (
1.14
). What is true is that a double cover of the almost complex structure of
\frac{1}{2}K3
gives a global
U(1)
action, and so
\phi_{0,1}|_{y\to y^{2}}
does lie in the image of
e_{\mathrm{JF}}
with
k=4
and
m=8
.

7.2.2.
Comparison with classical divisibility constraints

The classical elliptic genera (
1.1
) already imply divisibility constraints on Euler numbers, which we call the
classical divisibility constraints
. This section explains this and compares those constraints with our divisibility results in Section
7.2.1
. We show that indeed our divisibility constraints strictly refine the classical constraints, especially for strict tangential
SU(k)
-manifolds with
k\equiv 2
(mod
8
) and strict tangential
Sp(k)
-manifolds for all
k
.

The classical elliptic genera
\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}
in (
1.1
) take values in integral
weak
Jacobi forms
[
Gri99
]
. We use the notation
\mathrm{jF}_{k}
introduced in Section
1.1
(
18
).
Let us define

Definition 7.48
.

For each nonnegative integer
k
,
define
d_{\text{clas}}(k)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

(7.49)

\displaystyle d_{\text{clas}}(k):=\gcd\left\{\mathrm{im}\left(\mathrm{ev}_{z=0}\colon\mathrm{jF}_{k}|_{\deg=2k}\to\mathrm{mf}|_{\deg=0}=\mathbb{Z}\right)\right\}

Correspondingly to Theorem
7.38
, we get

Proposition 7.50

(Classical divisibility constraints)
.

For any
strict
tangential
SU(k)
-manifold
M
(of real dimension
2k
), its Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
is divisible by
d_{\text{clas}}(k)
.

Proof.

This follows from the classical fact
[
Gri99
]
that

(7.51)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Euler}(M)=\mathrm{ev}_{z=0}\circ\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M).

∎

Remark 7.52

(No further classical divisibility for
Sp
)
.

Here it is important to remark that the classical elliptic genera do not give any further refinement of the divisibility results for
Sp
-manifolds, since for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, all the elements in
\mathrm{jF}_{2k}|_{\deg=4k}
are even in the elliptic coordinate
z
.

The collection
\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\left(\mathrm{jF}_{k}|_{\deg=2k}\right)
of jacobi forms with weight
0
forms a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded subring of
\oplus_{k}\mathrm{JF}_{k}
, and expressed as,

(7.53)

\displaystyle\oplus_{k}\left(\mathrm{jF}_{k}|_{\deg=2k}\right)=\mathbb{Z}[\phi_{0,1},\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}},\phi_{0,2},\phi_{0,4}]/\left(4\phi_{0,4}=\phi_{0,1}\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}}^{2}-\phi_{0,2}^{2}\right)\subset\eqref{eq_JF_generators},

where the lower indices of each generator correspond to its weight and index.
The number
d_{\text{clas}}(k)
is explicitly computable by looking at the generators in (
7.53
). Under
\mathrm{ev}_{z=0}
, the generators are mapped as

(7.54)

\displaystyle\phi_{0,1}\mapsto 12,\quad\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}}\mapsto 2,\quad\phi_{0,2}\mapsto 6,\quad\phi_{0,4}\mapsto 3,

We can deduce

Proposition 7.55

(Computation of the classical divisibility constraints)
.

We have

(1)

We have
d_{\text{clas}}(1)=\infty
.

(2)

For even integer
k=2k^{\prime}
with
k^{\prime}\geq 1
, we have

(7.56)

\displaystyle d_{\text{clas}}(2k^{\prime})=\frac{12}{\gcd(k^{\prime},12)}

(3)

For odd integer
k=2k^{\prime}+3
with
k^{\prime}\geq 0
, we have

(7.57)

\displaystyle d_{\text{clas}}(2k^{\prime}+3)=\frac{24}{\gcd(k^{\prime},12)}.

Proof.

This follows from elementary computation using (
7.54
). Details are left to the reader.
∎

Now let us compare the classical divisibility constraints
d_{\text{clas}}
in Proposition
7.55
with our divisibility constraints in Theorem
7.43
and
7.38
.
We observe that, for the
SU
-case, we have

(7.58)

\displaystyle d_{SU}(k)=\begin{cases}2d_{\text{clas}}(k)&k\equiv 2\pmod{8},\\
d_{\text{clas}}(k)&k\not\equiv 2\pmod{8}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, we also see that our result for the
Sp
-case strictly refines the divisibility constraints by the factor of
2
for
all

k
, (also see Remark
7.52
).

(7.59)

\displaystyle d_{Sp}(k)=2d_{\text{clas}}(2k)\quad\forall k.

To the best knowledge of the authors, this refined divisibility result was not known in the literature.

Remark 7.60

(Gritsenko’s results by
[
Gri99
]
)
.

In
[
Gri99
, Theorem 2.4]
(see also the review article
[
Gri20
, Proposition 3.1 and the text below]
), Gritsenko gives the following divisibility results by classical methods.

26
26

26

Both
[
Gri99
, Theorem 2.4]
and the review article
[
Gri20
, Proposition 3.1 and the text below]
contained misprints, and the correct statement is presented in our main text.
The authors thank V. Gritsenko for confirming this.

For any almost complex manifold
M
of even complex dimension
k=2k^{\prime}
with
k^{\prime}\geq 1
, such that the rational first Chern class
c_{1}(M)_{\mathbb{Q}}\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Q})
vanishes, its Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
satisfies

(7.61)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{12}{\gcd(k^{\prime},12)}\,\right|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

If furthermore
k\equiv 2
(mod
8
) and the integral first Chern class
c_{1}(M)\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z})
vanishes, making
M
an
SU(k)
-manifold, then we further have

(7.62)

\displaystyle 8\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M)\quad\mbox{if }k\equiv 2\pmod{8}

Restricted to the
SU(k)
-manifolds, we see that this divisibility result coincides with our statement in Theorem
7.43
in the case
k
even.

Remark 7.63

(Classical divisibility constraints for irreducible hyperkähler manifolds of low dimensions)
.

If we restrict ourselves to irreducible hyperkähler manifolds, which furnish a very special class of strict tangential
Sp(k)
-manifolds, we can use the known divisibility constraints on the Hodge numbers to refine the classical divisibility constraints obtained in Proposition
7.55
. We illustrate it by the case of
k=2
and
k=3
.
As we will see, for those cases we achieve our divisibility constraints in Theorem
7.43
(1). But the method is already complicated there, and gets more and more complicated as
k
is increased.
Moreover, we emphasize that such analysis does not work for general strict tangential
Sp
-manifolds, since we cannot write Euler numbers in terms of an almost complex version of the Hodge numbers.
This should be compared with our simple and conceptual proof of the corresponding divisibility, treating general strict tangential
Sp
-manifolds all at once. The authors believe this illustrates the power of topological refinements of classical concepts.

We begin with the following facts:

•

An irreducible hyperkähler manifold
M
of real dimension
4k
has Hodge numbers
[
Eno90
]

h^{0,q}=\begin{cases}1,&q\text{ is even and }0\leq q\leq 2k,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and symmetry
h^{p,q}=h^{q,p}=h^{p,2k-q}
.

•

The constant Fourier component (in
\tau
) of the elliptic genus of a complex manifold
M
of real dimension
2k
is related to its Hodge numbers by

(7.64)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M)=\sum_{p=0}^{k}c_{p}y^{p-\frac{k}{2}}+\mathcal{O}(q),\quad c_{p}=\sum_{q=0}^{k}(-)^{p+q}h^{p,q}.

The case of
k=2

For
k=2
, there are three independent Hodge numbers
h^{1,1},h^{1,2},h^{2,2}
. The Betti and Euler numbers are

(7.65)

\displaystyle b_{0}=b_{8}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle b_{1}=b_{7}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle b_{2}=b_{6}

\displaystyle=2+h^{1,1}

\displaystyle b_{3}=b_{5}

\displaystyle=2h^{1,2}

\displaystyle b_{4}

\displaystyle=2+2h^{1,1}+h^{2,2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Euler}(M)

\displaystyle=2+2b_{2}-2b_{3}+b_{4}=8+4h^{1,1}-4h^{1,2}+h^{2,2}.

The elliptic genus is written as

(7.66)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}(M)=3\phi_{0,1}^{2}+\left(\frac{\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{6}-72\right)\phi_{0,2},

which by (
7.54
) already shows

(7.67)

\displaystyle 6\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

Expanding the elliptic genus in
q,y
, we find

(7.68)

\displaystyle 2h^{1,1}-h^{1,2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{6}-12\right),

\displaystyle 2-2h^{1,2}+h^{2,2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\,\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{3}+18\right),

and eliminating
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
gives

(7.69)

\displaystyle h^{2,2}

\displaystyle=64+8h^{1,1}-2h^{1,2}.

By
[
Wak58
, Corollary 8.1]
,
4\,|\,b_{3}=2h^{1,2}
, so
4\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M)
. Combining this with (
7.67
), we deduce

(7.70)

\displaystyle 12\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

This divisibility result coincides with our divisibility result in Theorem
7.43
(1).

The case of
k=3

For
k=3
, we compute

(7.71)

\displaystyle b_{0}=b_{12}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle b_{1}=b_{11}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle b_{2}=b_{10}

\displaystyle=2+h^{1,1}

\displaystyle b_{3}=b_{9}

\displaystyle=2h^{1,2}

\displaystyle b_{4}=b_{8}

\displaystyle=2+2h^{1,3}+h^{2,2}

\displaystyle b_{5}=b_{7}

\displaystyle=2h^{1,2}+2h^{2,3}

\displaystyle b_{6}

\displaystyle=2+2h^{1,1}+2h^{2,2}+h^{3,3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Euler}(M)

\displaystyle=2+2b_{2}-2b_{3}+2b_{4}-2b_{5}+b_{6}

\displaystyle=12+4h^{1,1}-8h^{1,2}+4h^{2,2}+4h^{1,3}-4h^{2,3}+h^{3,3}.

The elliptic genus is parameterized by
A\in\mathbb{Z}
as

(7.72)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}_{\text{clas}}([M])=4\phi_{0,1}^{3}+A\phi_{0,1}\phi_{0,2}+\left(\frac{\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{4}-18A-1728\right)\phi_{0,3}.

Expanding the elliptic genus in
q,y
, we find

(7.73)

\displaystyle 2h^{1,1}-2h^{1,2}+h^{1,3}

\displaystyle=120+A,

\displaystyle 2-2h^{1,2}+2h^{2,2}-h^{2,3}

\displaystyle=-516-4A+\frac{\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{4},

\displaystyle 2h^{1,3}-2h^{2,3}+h^{3,3}

\displaystyle=784+6A+\frac{\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{2}.

Eliminating
A
using the last two equations gives

(7.74)

\displaystyle-14-6h^{1,2}+6h^{2,2}-3h^{2,3}+4h^{1,3}-4h^{2,3}+2h^{3,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{7\,\mathrm{Euler}(M)}{4}.

Furthermore, we have
4\,|\,b_{3},b_{5}
by
[
Wak58
, Corollary 8.1]
, which implies
2\,|\,h^{2,3}
.
This shows we have

(7.75)

\displaystyle 8\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

This divisibility result coincides with our divisibility result in Theorem
7.43
(1).

Appendix A
A user guide to
\mathrm{TJF}

The theory of
Topological Jacobi Forms
is developed in an upcoming work by Bauer-Meier
[
BM25
]
. It is defined as the genuinely
U(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
, and regarded as a spectral refinement of the classical ring of integral Jacobi Forms.
It is an essential tool for us, being the domain of the
U(1)
-topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{U(1)_{k}}\colon MTSU(k)\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
.
In this section, we collect the necessary results on
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
, which we heavily use in the main text.

A.1.
Definition and basic properties

We employ the following as the definition of Topological Jacobi Forms.

Definition A.1

(
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
)
.

Let
k
be any integer. We define

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k}:=\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)},

where
V_{U(1)}
is the fundamental representation of
U(1)
.

As explained in Section
2.2
, the right hand side is by definition identified as

(A.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}=\Gamma\left(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{L}(-kV_{U(1)})\right),

where
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{M}
is the universal oriented elliptic curve (in the spectral algebro-geometric sense), and
\mathcal{L}(-kV_{U(1)})\in\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathcal{E})^{\times}
is the result of applying the
U(1)
-equivariant elliptic cohomology functor (
2.14
) to the corresponding representation sphere.
As explained in
[
BM25
]
, it is easy to verify that we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{L}(-kV_{U(1)})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(e)^{\otimes k}\in\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathcal{E})^{\times}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(e)
is the (SAG-version of the) sheaf of meromorphic functions on
\mathcal{E}
having poles of order at most
1
at the zero section
e\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{E}
. Thus we have a canonical identification

(A.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k}:=\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(1)}]^{U(1)}\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke)),

The equivariant Euler class of the fundamental representation,

(A.5)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{U(1)})\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{TJF}_{1}

is of particular importance. As we will see in Section
A.3
(
A.71
), this element corresponds to one of the generators
a:=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}=\theta_{11}(z,q)/\eta(q)^{3}
of the integral Jacobi Forms.
27
27

27

Also of physical importance because it is expected to correspond to the complex Majorana fermion.

It is also important to note that we have the multiplication map

(A.6)

\displaystyle\cdot\colon\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TJF}_{m}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k+m},

so that
\oplus_{k}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
can be regarded as a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring object in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
.

Recall that we have seen in our main text (Section
4.2.2
Example
4.98
) that the
internal structure maps
for the trio of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
specialize to produce the
stabilization sequence
of
\mathrm{TJF}
(see Section
1.1
(
7
) for the notation
\chi(V_{U(1)})
),

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 267.79211pt\raise-15.375pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.29085pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 267.79211pt\raise-23.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 317.5283pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-19.73615pt\raise-31.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[1]}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 49.96536pt\raise-31.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 109.1113pt\raise-31.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[2]}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 178.58363pt\raise-31.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[4]}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 248.05597pt\raise-31.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[6]}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 317.5283pt\raise-31.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where each consecutive pair of horizontal and vertical arrows forms a fiber sequence which we call the
stabilization-restriction fiber sequence
.
This sequence is regarded as building
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
by attaching even dimensional
\mathrm{TMF}
-cells one by one.
In view of the identification
A.4
, the algebro-geometric meaning of this sequence is nicely understood by the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[2k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x(k)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}[1]}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}((k-1)e))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{M},\omega^{-k})}
,

where the first bottom horizontal arrow comes from the canonical map
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}((k-1)e)\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke)
, and the second one is the residue pairing.
We have defined, in (
7.33
), the
attaching element

(A.15)

\displaystyle x(k)\in\pi_{2k-1}\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}

to be the cofiber of
\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}
in (
A.1
). This is the attaching map of the top
\mathrm{TMF}
-cell of
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
, which can also be identified with the transfer map
\mathrm{tr}_{e}^{U(1)}
(see (
A.1
) below). The analysis of this element played a key role in our application to Euler numbers in Section

.
Moreover, it is important to note that the stabilization-restriction sequence is
dual
to that of
k
replaced by
1-k
, in the sense that the following diagram commutes by Proposition
4.84
.

\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot}

).
In particular, the commutativity of the right square allows us to identify the top right horizontal arrow with the transfer map as indicated in the diagram.

A.2.
The cell structure

The following explicit knowledge of the cell structure of
\mathrm{TJF}
is key to our analysis.

Fact A.20
.

Let
k\geq 1
be any positive integer.
Let
\mathrm{tr}\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}_{+}\to S^{0}
be the
U(1)
-equivariant transfer map. Define

(A.21)

\displaystyle P_{k}:=\mathrm{cofib}\left(\mathrm{tr}|_{\Sigma\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}}\colon\Sigma\mathbb{C}P^{k-1}\to S^{0}\right)

Then we have an isomorphism of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules

(A.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes P_{k}.

Moreover, the isomorphism (
A.22
) is compatible with the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence in (
A.1
) in the sense that the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[2k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x(k)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}[1]}
\textstyle{P_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\otimes-}
\textstyle{P_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\otimes-}
\textstyle{S^{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\otimes-}
\textstyle{P_{k-1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\otimes-}

where the bottom row is the cofiber sequence induced by the standard inclusion
\mathbb{CP}^{k-2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}
.

\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
for negative
k
is also understood by using the above Fact
A.20
. We have (here
D
denotes the dual in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
and
D_{S}
denotes the dual in
\mathrm{Spectra}
, see Notation
1.1
(
15
))

(A.27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\simeq D(\mathrm{TJF}_{-k})[1]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes D_{S}(P_{-k})[1]

by the dualizability of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
in (
2.31
).

Here, we give the sketch of the proof of this fact, in order to make the meaning of this cell structure clear, and also to prepare for the analogous argument showing the cell structure of
\mathrm{TEJF}
in Proposition
B.34
below.

Sketch of Proof of Fact
A.20

[
BM25
]
.

Consider the following cofiber sequence of pointed
U(1)
-spaces,

(A.28)

\displaystyle S(kV_{U(1)})_{+}\to S^{0}\xrightarrow{\chi(kV_{U(1)})}S^{kV_{U(1)}}.

Apply the
U(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-homology functor
\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes-\right)^{U(1)}
to get the fiber sequence

(A.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\Sigma\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}_{+}\to\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{k}

where we have used that
S(kV_{U(1)})
is a free
U(1)
-space with
\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}=S(kV_{U(1)})/U(1)
, so that we can apply the Adams isomorphism

(A.30)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes S(kV_{U(1)})_{+}\right)^{U(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\Sigma\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}_{+}.

We know by
[
GM23
]
(also see Fact
6.5
(2)), that we have
\mathrm{TMF}^{U(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\oplus\mathrm{TMF}[1]
,
and we can verify that the first arrow in (
A.29
) is given by tensoring
\mathrm{TMF}
to the map

(A.31)

\displaystyle\Sigma\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}_{+}=\Sigma\mathbb{CP}^{k-1}\sqcup S^{1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}\sqcup\mathrm{id}_{S^{1}}}S^{0}\sqcup S^{1}.

This gives the desired result.
∎
