If we invert the prime
2
, we get the following simple result.

Proposition A.39

(The structure of
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
after inverting
2

[
LTY
]
)
.

After inverting
2
, the
\mathrm{TMF}
-module structure of
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
is identified as follows.

(1)

The stabilization-restriction sequence (
A.1
) for
k=3
splits at
\mathrm{TJF}_{3}
,

\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[6]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{b}}

Here we have denoted
\mathfrak{b}\in\pi_{6}\mathrm{TJF}_{3}
which gives a splitting. This element is characterized by the Jacobi Form image as

(A.42)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{JF}}(\mathfrak{b})=\frac{1}{2}\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}}.

(2)

For
k\geq 4
, setting
k^{\prime}:=\lfloor(m-1)/3\rfloor
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules,

(A.43)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\simeq\mathrm{TJF}_{k-3k^{\prime}}[6k^{\prime}]\oplus\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{TMF}_{1}(2)[6i].

We have more to say on this decomposition after our analysis of the relation between
\mathrm{TEJF}
and
\mathrm{TJF}
in Section
B.4
.

The more detailed computation of homotopy groups of
\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
will appear in upcoming works by Bauer-Meier and Tominaga
[
BM25
]

[
Tom
]
.
In Section
7.2
of this paper, we use the following computational result by Bauer
[
Bau
]
on the order of the attaching element
x(k)\in\pi_{2k-1}\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}
introduced in (
A.15
).

Proposition A.44

(
[
Bau
]
)
.

The order
d_{SU}(k)
(Definition
7.34
) of the The attaching element
x(k)\in\pi_{2k-1}\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}
in (
A.15
) is given as follows.
We have

(A.45)

\displaystyle d_{SU}(1)=\infty,

and for
k\geq 2
, we have

(A.46)

\displaystyle d_{SU}(k)=2^{\alpha(k)}\cdot 3^{\beta(k)}

with

(A.47)

\displaystyle\alpha(k)=\begin{cases}3&k\equiv 1,2,5\pmod{8}\\
2&k\equiv 6,7\pmod{8}\\
1&k\equiv 3,4\pmod{8},\\
0&k\equiv 0\pmod{8}.\end{cases}\quad\beta(k)=\begin{cases}1&k\equiv 1,2\pmod{3}\\
0&k\equiv 0\pmod{3}.\end{cases}

Remark A.48

(Easy estimates of
d_{SU}(k)
)
.

The proof of Proposition
A.44
depends on spectral sequence computations. But without such effort, we can give a substantial lower bound to
d_{SU}(k)
’s in an elementary way, using our knowledge of the cell structures of
\mathrm{TJF}
. Here we present such a result with a proof.

Claim A.49

(Easy estimates of
d_{SU}(k)
)
.

We have

(A.50)

\displaystyle d_{SU}(1)=\infty,\quad d_{SU}(2)=24,

and for
k\geq 3
, we have

(A.51)

\displaystyle\left.2^{\alpha^{\prime}(k)}\cdot 3^{\beta^{\prime}(k)}\right|d_{SU}(k),

with

(A.52)

\displaystyle\alpha(k)=\begin{cases}3&k\equiv 1,5\pmod{8}\\
2&k\equiv 2,6,7\pmod{8}\\
1&k\equiv 3,4\pmod{8},\\
0&k\equiv 0\pmod{8}.\end{cases}\quad\beta(k)=\begin{cases}1&k\equiv 1,2\pmod{3}\\
0&k\equiv 0\pmod{3}.\end{cases}

Note that, compared to the result in Proposition
A.44
, the estimate in Claim
A.49
is sharp except for the case
k\equiv 2
(mod
8
) for
k\geq 10
, and off by the factor
2
for those cases.

Proof of Claim
A.49
.

We use the knowledge of cell structures of
\mathrm{TJF}
explained in Section
A.2
.
(
A.50
) follows by (
A.36
) and (
A.37
).
Let us prove the case for
k\geq 3
.
First, the estimate on the
3
-valuation is easily obtained by Proposition
A.39
.
So let us focus on the
2
-valuation. We separate the case of
k
even and odd.

First, let
k:=2k^{\prime}
be an even integer for
k^{\prime}\geq 2
.
Consider the composition

(A.53)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[4k^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{x(2k^{\prime})\cdot}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k^{\prime}-1}[1]\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k^{\prime}-1}/\mathrm{TJF}_{2k^{\prime}-3}[1]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}/\eta[4k^{\prime}-3],

where we have used Fact
A.20
that
\mathrm{TJF}_{m}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes P_{m}
with
P_{m}:=\mathrm{cofib}(\Sigma\mathbb{CP}^{m-1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}}S^{0})
, and the composition (
A.53
) is obtained by tensoring
\mathrm{TMF}
with the following,

(A.54)

\displaystyle S^{4k^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{}\Sigma P_{2k^{\prime}-1}\xrightarrow{}\Sigma P_{2k^{\prime}-1}/P_{2k^{\prime}-3}\simeq\Sigma^{3}\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}-2}/\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}-4}\simeq S^{4k^{\prime}-1}\cup_{\eta}S^{4k^{\prime}-3}.

By the commutativity of (
A.20
), we see that the composition (
A.54
) is the stable attaching map of the top cell of the truncated complex projective space
\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}-1}/\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}-4}
. It is known
[
Mos68
, Theorem 5.2 and its proof]
(also see the cell diagrams in Figure
1
) that this map factors through the bottom cell as,

(A.55)

\displaystyle S^{4k^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{k^{\prime}\nu}S^{4k^{\prime}-3}\xrightarrow{}S^{4k^{\prime}-1}\cup_{\eta}S^{4k^{\prime}-3},

after tensoring
\mathrm{TMF}
, it is easy to see by using
\eta^{2}=12\nu
in
\pi_{3}\mathrm{TMF}
that the composition (
A.53
) represents an element of order exactly
\frac{12}{\gcd(k^{\prime},12)}
in
\pi_{3}\mathrm{TMF}/\eta
.
This means that the order of the element
x(k)
is divisible by this number, proving the case of
k
even.

Finally let us prove the case of odd
k=2k^{\prime}+3
with
k^{\prime}\geq 0
. Similarly to the above proof for the even case, let us consider the composition

(A.56)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[4k^{\prime}+6]\xrightarrow{x(2k^{\prime}+3)\cdot}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k^{\prime}+2}[1]\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k^{\prime}+2}/\mathrm{TJF}_{2k^{\prime}}[1]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[4k^{\prime}+5]\oplus\mathrm{TMF}[4k^{\prime}+3],

where, as before using
\mathrm{TJF}_{m}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes P_{m}
, we see that (
A.56
) is obtained by tensoring
\mathrm{TMF}
to

(A.57)

\displaystyle S^{4k^{\prime}+6}\xrightarrow{}\Sigma P_{2k^{\prime}+2}\xrightarrow{}\Sigma P_{2k^{\prime}+2}/P_{2k^{\prime}}\simeq\Sigma^{3}\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}+1}/\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}-1}\simeq S^{4k^{\prime}+5}\oplus S^{4k^{\prime}+3}.

Again, (
A.57
) is the stable attaching map of the top cell of
\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}+2}/\mathbb{CP}^{2k^{\prime}-1}
. It is known
[
Mos68
, Theorem 5.2 and its proof]
(also see the cell diagrams in Figure
1
) that this map represents the class
(\eta,k^{\prime}\nu)\in\pi_{1}S\oplus\pi_{3}S
for
k^{\prime}\not\equiv 0\pmod{4}
and
(\eta,2k^{\prime}\nu)\in\pi_{1}S\oplus\pi_{3}S
for
k^{\prime}\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.
After tensoring
\mathrm{TMF}
, we see that the composition (
A.56
) represesnts the class of order exactly
\frac{24}{\gcd(12,k^{\prime})}
. This means that the order of the element
x(2k^{\prime}+3)=x(k)
is divisible by this number, proving the case of
k
odd. This completes the proof of Claim
A.49
.
∎

A.3.
The relation with (classical) Jacobi Forms

As explained in Section
2.2.2
, Jacobi Forms are identified with the
U(1)
-equivariant Modular Forms.
Recall our notation
\mathrm{JF}_{k}
in (
2.57
) (Note that we are imposing integrality, and note also for our degree convention).
We have multiplication maps
\mathrm{JF}_{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{MF}}\mathrm{JF}_{m}\to\mathrm{JF}_{k+m}
, which makes
\mathrm{JF}_{\bullet}:=\oplus_{k}\mathrm{JF}_{k}
into a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathrm{MF}
-module ring.
Concretely, we have
By
[
Gri20
, Theorem 2.7]
we have the generator-relation expression,

(A.58)

\displaystyle\mathrm{JF}_{\bullet}=\mathrm{MF}[a:=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}},\phi_{0,1},\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}},\phi_{0,2},\phi_{0,4},E_{4,1},E_{4,2},E_{4,3},E_{6,1},E_{6,2},E^{\prime}_{6,3}]/\sim,

where for the relation we refer to
[
Gri20
]
.
The notation
f_{w,i}
denotes an elements of weight
w
and index
i
, so that
f_{w,i}\in\mathrm{JF}_{2i}|_{\deg=2w+4k}
, and we have employed the notation
a:=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}
as introduced in (
1.67
).

The generator
a=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathrm{JF}_{1}|_{\deg=0}
in (
1.67
) is of particular importance.
It vanishes at order
1
at the zero section of the universal elliptic curve, and nowhere vanishing outside. This means that the multiplication by
a
gives an isomorphism of line bundles

(A.59)

\displaystyle a\cdot\colon\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}}(e)\simeq\mathcal{A}(\xi)\otimes\omega^{-1}\quad\mbox{ in }\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}),

where
\xi=[\overline{V}_{U(1)}]\in[BU(1),P^{4}BO]\simeq\mathbb{Z}
is the generator and
\mathcal{A}(\xi)
is the corresponding Looijenga’s Line bundle (Definition
2.49
).
Thus, for each nonnegative integer
k
we have an isomorphism

(A.60)

\displaystyle a^{k}\cdot\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}}(ke))\simeq\mathrm{JF}_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}.

Now we can introduce the connection with Topological Jacobi Forms. We have a canonical map
e_{\mathrm{JF}}\colon\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\to\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=\bullet}
which fits into the commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{-k}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\bullet}\Gamma(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(ke))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathcal{E})^{*}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}};\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}}(ke)\otimes\omega^{\bullet/2})}

This allows us to regard
\oplus_{k}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}
as a spectral refinement of the graded ring of integral Jacobi Forms.

The stabilization-restriction fiber sequence (
A.1
) fits into the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TJF}_{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{U(1)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\bullet}\mathrm{TJF}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{U(1)}^{e}}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\bullet-2k}\mathrm{TMF}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{MF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{k-1}|_{\deg=\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a\cdot}
\scriptstyle{a^{-k+1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{k}|_{\deg=\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{z=0}\colon\phi(z,q)\mapsto\phi(0,q)}
\scriptstyle{a^{-k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{MF}|_{\deg=\bullet-2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}}((k-1)e)\otimes\omega^{\bullet/2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}}}(ke)\otimes\omega^{\bullet/2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{z=0}\circ(a^{k}\cdot-)}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}},\omega^{\bullet/2-k}),}

where the bottom left arrow is the canonical inclusion.
In particular, we have

(A.71)

\displaystyle a=\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}=e_{\mathrm{JF}}\left(\chi(V_{U(1)})\right)\in\mathrm{JF}_{1}|_{\deg=0}.

This is a special case of (
2.65
).

Appendix B
On
\mathrm{TEJF}
:= the
Sp(1)=SU(2)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}

In the main body of this article, the spectrum
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
, which is defined to be
Sp(1)
-equivariant twisted
\mathrm{TMF}
and called
Topological Even Jacobi Forms
, appeared as the domain of the
Sp(1)
-topological elliptic genus
\mathrm{Jac}_{Sp(1)_{k}}\colon MTSp(k)\to\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
.
In this section, we study this spectrum, which itself is of independent interest.

Remark B.1
.

The content of this Appendix
B
is an original new result of this paper.

B.1.
The definition

Definition B.2

(
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
)
.

Let
k
be any integer. We define

(B.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}:=\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)},

where
V_{Sp(1)}
is the fundamental representation of
Sp(1)
.

Remark B.4
.

Note that we do NOT define
\mathrm{TEJF}_{m}
for odd
m
.

We employ this terminology because
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
is regarded as refining the module of integral
even
Jacobi Forms.
Recall that we have defined in Example
2.69
the sub-
\mathrm{MF}
-module
\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}\subset\mathrm{JF}_{2k}
by

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}

\displaystyle:=\{\phi(z,\tau)\in\mathrm{JF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}\ |\ \phi(z,\tau)=\phi(-z,\tau)\}=(\pi_{m}\mathrm{JF}_{2k})^{\mathbb{Z}/2},

(B.6)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrm{JF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}&m\equiv 0\pmod{4}\\
0&m\not\equiv 0\pmod{4}.\end{cases}

where
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts on
\mathrm{JF}_{2k}
by
\phi(z,\tau)\mapsto\phi(-z,\tau)
.

\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}
is identified as integral
Sp(1)
-equivariant Modular Forms as explained in Example
2.69
.
We get the
Sp(1)
-equivariant character map
e_{\mathrm{EJF}}
in (
2.71
),
which is compatible with the character map for
\mathrm{TJF}
, so that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\pi_{m}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{EJF}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}}
\textstyle{\pi_{m}\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{\mathrm{JF}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\ \mbox{\tiny for }m\equiv 0\pmod{4}}
\scriptstyle{0\ \mbox{\tiny for }m\not\equiv 0\pmod{4}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{JF}_{2k}|_{\deg=m}}

As indicated above, the map from
\mathrm{EJF}_{2k}
to
\mathrm{JF}_{2k}
is just the inclusion of the direct summand.
However, as we will see in Section
B.4
below, the upper horizontal arrow in (
B.1
) does not split; rather, we show that it fits into a fiber sequence involving another copy of
\mathrm{TEJF}
(Proposition
B.80
). This creates nontrivial torsion elements in cokernels of the upper horizontal arrow in (
B.1
), which is exactly the origin of the refined divisibility result of Euler numbers for
Sp
-manifolds in the main text Section
7.2
.

As explained in Section
2.3.1
, the
RO(G)
-graded
\mathrm{TMF}
are special cases of twisted genuinely
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
. In general, it is expected that genuinely
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
can be twisted by a map
BG\to P^{4}BO
. In the case
G=Sp(1)
, we are lucky enough that the representations
kV_{Sp(1)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(Sp(1))
exhaust all the expected twists as follows.

Lemma B.11
.

We have

(B.12)

\displaystyle[BSp(1),P^{4}BO]\simeq H^{4}(BSp(1);\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z},

and the element
\mathrm{tw}(\overline{V}_{Sp(1)})\in[BSp(1),P^{4}BO]

represents a generator of (
B.12
).

Proof.

The first claim follows from
Sp(1)
being a compact connected simply connected simple Lie group, and the second claim follows from the fact that the second Chern class of
V_{Sp(1)}
is the generator of
H^{4}(BSp(1);\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

This entitles us to say that
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
completes the list of all the geometrically twisted
Sp(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
.

Remark B.13
.

Concretely,
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
is identified with what is often called “
Sp(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
twisted by
k\tau\in H^{4}(BSp(1);\mathbb{Z})
” where
\tau
is a generator of
H^{4}(BSp(1);\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. The integer
k
is also often called the “level”. But we need to be careful about the degree, since
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}=\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes S^{kV_{Sp(1)}}\right)^{Sp(1)}
and
S^{kV_{Sp(1)}}
is of dimension
4k
; for example, to compare with the degree convention of
[
TY23
]
, we have

(B.14)

\displaystyle\pi_{m}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}=\mathrm{TMF}_{Sp(1)}^{4k-m+k\tau}\mbox{ in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{tachikawa2023anderson}{}{}]}.}

B.2.
Basic properties

The structure of
\mathrm{TEJF}_{\bullet}
is parallel
\mathrm{TJF}_{\bullet}
, reviewed in Section
A.1
.
The Euler class of the fundamental representation
28
28

28

For a physical interpretation of the genuinely equivariant Euler class
\chi(V)\in\mathrm{TMF}[V]^{G}
, see Remark
2.63
. In particular, the element
\chi(V_{Sp(1)})
is supposed to be physically interpreted as “quaternionic
1

(B.15)

\displaystyle\chi(V_{Sp(1)})\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}

restricts by
\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}
to
\chi(V_{U(1)})^{2}\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{TJF}_{2}
, so that we have

(B.16)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{EJF}}\left(\chi(V_{Sp(1)})\right)=a^{2}=\left(\phi_{-1,\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}=\left(\theta_{11}(z,\tau)/\eta(\tau)^{3}\right)^{2}\in\mathrm{EJF}_{2}|_{\deg=0}.

where we are using the notation
1.67
.
We have the multiplication map

(B.17)

\displaystyle\cdot\colon\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\otimes_{\mathrm{TMF}}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2m}\to\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k+2m}

so that
\oplus_{k}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
can be regarded as a evenly graded ring object in

.

B.3.
The cell structure of
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}

In this subsection, we determine the structure of
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
as a
\mathrm{TMF}
-module.
As we will see,
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
turns out to have a surprizingly simple structure;

Proposition B.34
.

(1)

For any integer
k\geq-1
, we have an equivalence of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules,

(B.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}:=\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}[-4]

Here we note that we are using
\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}
, NOT
\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}_{+}
. In particular, for
k=-1
we have

(B.36)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{-2}=0.

Moreover, the isomorphism (
B.35
) is compatible with the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence in (
B.2
) in the sense that the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(2)

For

, we have

(B.41)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_TEJF_duality_app}}}{{\simeq}}D(\mathrm{TEJF}_{-2k-4})[-5]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_TEJF_cellstr}}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}\otimes D_{S}(\mathbb{HP}^{-k-1})[-1].

Remark B.42

(Various equivalent cell structures for
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
(remark added for arXiv v2))
.

In the upcoming work by T.Bauer and the second author
[
BYa
]
, we show that actually there are other, a priori different-looking
\mathrm{TMF}
-cell complexes which turns out to be all equivalent to
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
:

(B.43)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}[-4]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes D_{S}(\mathbb{HP}_{-k}^{0}),

as well as

(B.44)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{\infty}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}^{\infty}[-4]\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes D_{S}(\mathbb{HP}_{-\infty}^{0})\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\sslash\nu

where the
\sslash
denotes the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-quotient, apearing in
[
Dev22
]
.
In the paper
[
BYa
]
, we will give independent proof of the all the isomorphisms above, without using the proof provided here. In particular,
[
BYa
]
contains a proof for Proposition
B.34
above, which is conceptually better than the one provided here.

Proof.

The proof is parallel to the proof of Fact
A.20
by Bauer-Meier sketched there.
Let
k\geq-1
. Consider the following cofiber sequence of pointed
Sp(1)
-spaces,

(B.45)

\displaystyle S((k+2)V_{Sp(1)})_{+}\to S^{0}\to S^{(k+2)V_{Sp(1)}}.

Tensoring
S^{-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}}
to the above sequence and applying the
Sp(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-homology functor
\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes(-)\right)^{Sp(1)}
, we get a fiber sequence

(B.46)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes S^{-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}}\otimes S((k+2)V_{Sp(1)})_{+}\right)^{Sp(1)}\to\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}\to\mathrm{TMF}[(k+2)V_{Sp(1)}-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}.

By the Adams isomorphism and the fact that
S((k+2)V_{Sp(1)})_{+}/Sp(1)\simeq\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}
, we get

(B.47)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{TMF}\otimes S^{-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}}\otimes S((k+2)V_{Sp(1)})_{+}\right)^{Sp(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}_{+}^{k+1}

On the other hand, we claim that we have

(B.48)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}\simeq D(\mathrm{TMF}^{Sp(1)})\simeq D(\mathrm{TMF})\simeq\mathrm{TMF}.

Here
D(-)
denotes the dual object in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{TMF}}
. The first equivalence follows from Fact
2.29
and the second equivalence follows from Fact
6.5
(1).
Moreover, we have

(B.49)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}[(k+2)V_{Sp(1)}-\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}]^{Sp(1)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}+5]^{Sp(1)},

since
[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}}]=2\cdot[\overline{V_{Sp(1)}}]\in[BSp(1),BO\langle 0,\cdots,4\rangle]
and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{Ad}_{Sp(1)}=3
.
Rewriting the fiber sequence (
B.46
) by the isomorphisms (
B.47
), (
B.48
) and (
B.49
), we get a fiber sequence

(B.50)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TMF}\otimes\mathbb{HP}_{+}^{k+1}\xrightarrow{\rm{ev}_{+}}\mathrm{TMF}\to\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{Sp(1)}+5]^{Sp(1)}

Here the first arrow can be identified by the evaluation at the basepoint because of the following observation
29
29

29

The arXiv v1 of this article contained a mistake in the logic here, but the result is unchanged.
:
In general, for any ring spectrum
A
and
M\in\mathrm{Mod}_{A}
, suppose an
A
-module morphism

(B.51)

\displaystyle f\colon A\oplus M\to A

satisfies that

(B.52)

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{A}=\left(A\xhookrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{A}\oplus 0}A\oplus M\xrightarrow{f}A\right).

Then
f

is equivalent, by an
A
-module automorphism of
A\oplus M
, to the morphism
\mathrm{id}_{A}\oplus 0
.
We apply this observation to
A=\mathrm{TMF}
and
M=\mathrm{TMF}\oplus\mathbb{HP}^{k+1}
with the morphism
f
being the first map of (
B.46
). This map satisfies the above condition (
B.52
) by the case
k=-1
.
This implies the first statement of Proposition
B.34
(1). The second statement of (1) follows directly from our construction of the isomorphism (
B.35
). (2) is obtained by combining the duality statement in Lemma
B.27
and (1) of this proposition which we have just proved.
This completes the proof of Proposition
B.34

∎

The stable attaching map
\widetilde{y}(k)
in (

) of

.

.

.

(Definition

\displaystyle\left.\frac{24}{\gcd(k,24)}\right|d_{Sp}(k).

Proof.

and the commutativity of diagram (

).

B.3.1.
\mathrm{TEJF}
at odd primes

If we invert the prime

,

’s look even more simple. First, if we localize at a prime

simply gives a decomposition

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\right)_{(p)}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k}\mathrm{TMF}_{(p)}[4i].\quad p\geq 5.

Now consider the case including

. We get, for each odd prime

,

(B.68)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{4}\right)_{(p)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_TEJF4}}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{TMF}\otimes(S^{0}\cup_{\nu}S^{4}\cup_{2\nu}S^{8})_{(p)}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}_{1}(2),

[

]
.
We know that

).
In particular, the connecting element

\mathrm{TEJF}_{6}
,

\textstyle{\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{4}\right)_{(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{Sp(1)})\cdot}
\textstyle{\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{6}\right)_{(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{e}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}_{(p)}[12]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{c}}

here we denoted an element
\mathfrak{c}\in\pi_{12}\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{6}\right)_{(p)}
which gives a splitting. Note that the character
e_{\mathrm{EJF}}(\mathfrak{c})\in\mathrm{EJF}_{6}|_{\deg=12}
of this element should satisfy

(B.71)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{z=0}\circ e_{\mathrm{EJF}}(\mathfrak{c})=1.

By inspecting the generators of
\mathrm{EJF}_{6}|_{\deg=12}
and using (

(B.72)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{EJF}}(\mathfrak{c})=\left(\frac{\phi_{0,\frac{3}{2}}}{2}\right)^{2}.

Proposition B.73

(
\mathrm{TEJF}
localized at prime
3
)
.

For each integer
k\geq 3
, we have the follwoing decomposition of
\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\right)_{(3)}
as a
\mathrm{TMF}_{(3)}
-module: Setting
k^{\prime}:=\lfloor(k+1)/3\rfloor
, the map

(B.74)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{c}^{k^{\prime}}\cdot)\oplus\ \bigoplus_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}-1}\left((\mathrm{stab})^{k-3i-2}\circ\mathfrak{c}^{i}\cdot\right)\colon\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2(k-3k^{\prime})}\right)_{(3)}[12k^{\prime}]\oplus\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}-1}\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{4}\right)_{(3)}[12i]\to\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\right)_{(3)}.

is an equivalence of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules.
Here, the map consists of the multiplication by the element
\mathfrak{c}\in\pi_{12}\mathrm{TEJF}_{6}
given in (
B.3.1
).
This means that, using (
B.68
), we have an isomorphism of
\mathrm{TMF}_{(3)}
-modules,

(B.75)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\right)_{(3)}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{TMF}_{1}(2)[12i]\bigoplus\begin{cases}\mathrm{TMF}_{(3)}[12k^{\prime}]&k\equiv 0\pmod{3},\\
\left(\mathrm{TMF}/\nu\right)_{(3)}[12k^{\prime}]&k\equiv 1\pmod{3},\\
0&k\equiv 2\pmod{3}.\end{cases}

In particular, the torsions in the homotopy groups are given by

(B.76)

\displaystyle\left(\pi_{\bullet}\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\right)_{(3)}\right)_{\rm tors}\simeq\begin{cases}\left(\pi_{\bullet-12k^{\prime}}\mathrm{TMF}_{(3)}\right)_{\rm tors}&k\equiv 0\pmod{3},\\
\left(\pi_{\bullet-12k^{\prime}}\left(\mathrm{TMF}/\nu\right)_{(3)}\right)_{\rm tors}&k\equiv 1\pmod{3},\\
0&k\equiv 2\pmod{3}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
A.39
in
[
LTY
, Appendix A]
, so we only give a sketch here and leave the details to the reader. We claim that the map

(B.77)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}\cdot\oplus\left(\mathrm{stab}\right)^{k-2}\colon\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-6}\right)_{(3)}[12]\oplus\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{4}\right)_{(3)}\to\left(\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\right)_{(3)}

is an equivalence for any
k\geq 2
. This claim is shown by the induction on
k
, using the fact that (
B.77
) is compatible with the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence (
B.2
). The first statement of the Proposition follows by applying this claim repeatedly. The remaining statements follow from the fact that
\pi_{*}\mathrm{TMF}_{1}(2)
is torsion-free.
∎

B.4.
Comparison to
\mathrm{TJF}

In this subsection, as promised in the paragraph after the diagram (
B.1
), we study the restriction map

(B.78)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}\colon\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}.

The statement uses the Euler class of the adjoint representation of
Sp(1)
,

(B.79)

\displaystyle\chi\left(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))\right)\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{TMF}[\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))]^{Sp(1)}\simeq\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{4},

where we have used the string orientation of
\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))-2V_{Sp(1)}
and the
Sp(1)
-equivariant sigma orientation.

Proposition B.80
.

For each integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following fiber sequence of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules.

(B.81)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2n-4}[5]\xrightarrow{\chi(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1)))\cdot}\mathrm{TEJF}_{2n}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}}\mathrm{TJF}_{2n}\to\mathrm{TEJF}_{2n-4}[6]

Proof.

We follow a similar strategy as the proof of Proposition
4.78
.
First observe that we have an isomorphism of
Sp(1)
-spaces,

(B.82)

\displaystyle Sp(1)/U(1)\simeq S(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))).

Thus we have the following cofiber sequence of pointed
Sp(1)
-spaces,

(B.83)

\displaystyle(Sp(1)/U(1))_{+}\to S^{0}\xrightarrow{\chi(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1)))}S^{\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))}.

Taking the smash product with
S^{-nV_{Sp(1)}}
, we get the following fiber sequence of
Sp(1)
-spectra,

(B.84)

\displaystyle(Sp(1)/U(1))_{+}\wedge S^{-nV_{Sp(1)}}\to S^{-nV_{Sp(1)}}\xrightarrow{\chi(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1)))}S^{-nV_{Sp(1)}+\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))}.

By (
4.83
) we have an isomorphism of
Sp(1)
-spectra,

(B.85)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{U(1)}^{Sp(1)}\left(S^{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}(nV_{Sp(1)})}\right)\simeq(Sp(1)/U(1))_{+}\wedge S^{-nV_{Sp(1)}},

Thus, applying
Sp(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
-cohomology to (
B.84
), we get a fiber sequence

\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[nV_{Sp(1)}-\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1))\right]^{Sp(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1)))\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[nV_{Sp(1)}\right]^{Sp(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}\left[\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}(nV_{Sp(1)})\right]^{U(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2n-4}[5]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(\mathrm{Ad}(Sp(1)))\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TEJF}_{2n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TJF}_{2n}}

∎

Corollary B.90
.

The restriction map

(B.91)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{Sp(1)}^{U(1)}\colon\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\to\mathrm{TJF}_{2}

gives an isomorphism between
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}/\nu
in (
B.64
) and
\mathrm{TJF}_{2}\simeq\mathrm{TMF}/\nu
in (
A.34
).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
B.80
applied to
k=1
and the fact that
\mathrm{TEJF}_{-2}=0
in Proposition
B.34
.
∎

Corollary B.92
.

If we invert the prime
2
, the fiber sequence (
B.81
) splits at
\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}
,
so that we have an isomorphism of
\mathrm{TMF}
-modules,

(B.93)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}\oplus\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-4}[6]\simeq\mathrm{TJF}_{2k}.

Proof.

This is because, after inverting
2
, we have
\pi_{5}\mathrm{TEJF}_{4}=0
by Section
B.3.1
. Thus the element (
B.79
) vanishes and get the desired splitting.
∎

Remark B.94
.

Propositions
B.73
and Corollary
B.92
explain the decomposition of
\mathrm{TJF}
at odd prime in Proposition
A.39
in a nice way. Namely, the
\mathrm{TMF}_{1}(2)
’s appearing in the decomposition (
A.43
) is most naturally regarded as
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2}
. The components labeled by even
i
correspond to the first direct summand
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k}
in (
B.93
), and those labeled by odd
i
correspond to the second direct summand
\mathrm{TEJF}_{2k-4}[6]
.

Appendix C
A toy model: The topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera

In this section, we give a toy model of the construction of the main body of this article
30
30

30

The authors thank Thomas Schick for suggesting this toy model.
.
We replace the genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
with the genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-theory with the standard equivariance. The construction here should be regarded as being obtained by replacing elliptic curves by the multiplicative group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
in the construction, so we name them as
topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera
.
We construct a morphism of spectra of the form

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}}\colon MT(H,\tau_{H})\to\mathrm{KO}[\tau_{G}]^{G},

where
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}
is a set of data introduced below, and
G,H,\tau_{G},\tau_{H}
are included as ingredients of the data
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}
.

C.1.
The definition of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}

We start with the main construction of this section, which is compared to Section
3.2
in the main part.
Assume we are given a set of data, which we symbolically denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}
.

•

Fix compact Lie groups
G
and
H
, together with
\tau_{G}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G)
and
\tau_{H}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(H)
.

•

Fix an integer
d
and a group homomorphism
\phi\colon G\times H\to O(d)
. We denote the corresponding
d
-dimensional orthogonal representation by
V_{\phi}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{O}(G\times H)
.

•

We assume that
\dim\tau_{H}=0
and
d=\dim\tau_{G}
31
31

31

This assumption is technical. In general, we can just add trivial representations to
\tau_{G}
or
\tau_{H}
to reduce to this case.
.

•

Fix a
spin
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on the virtual representation

(C.2)

\displaystyle\Theta:=V_{\phi}-\mathrm{res}_{G}^{G\times H}(\tau_{G})-\mathrm{res}_{H}^{G\times H}(\tau_{H})\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(G\times H).

I.e., we assume that the composition

(C.3)

\displaystyle BG\times BH\xrightarrow{\Theta}BO\to P^{2}BO

is nullhomotopic, and
\mathfrak{s}
is a choice of its nullhomotopy.
32
32

32

The Postnikov truncation
P^{2}BO
of
BO
is the obstruction space of spin structure. We have a fibration

(C.4)

\displaystyle BSpin\to BO\to P^{2}BO.

We can regard
\Theta
as a vector bundle over
BH
with a
G
-action, where the space
BH
is equipped with the trivial
G
-action. Then the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
above induces the
G
-equivariant spin structure on the virtual vector bundle
\Theta
on
BH
. The
G
-equivariant Atiyah-Bott-Shapiro orientation gives us the following equivalence of
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-module spectra,

(C.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ABS}(\Theta,\mathfrak{s})\colon\mathrm{KO}\otimes BH^{V_{\phi}-\tau_{H}}\simeq\mathrm{KO}\otimes BH_{+}\otimes S^{\tau_{G}}.

Definition C.6

(Definition of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}}
)
.

Assume we are given a set of data
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}
listed above.
Consider the following map in
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
:

(C.7)

\displaystyle MT(H,\tau_{H})=BH^{-\tau_{H}}\xhookrightarrow{\chi(V_{\phi})\cdot}BH^{V_{\phi}-\tau_{H}}.

Here,
MT(H,\tau_{H})
is regarded as a spectrum with trivial
G
-equivariance, and
V_{\phi}
is regarded as a
G
-equivariant vector bundle over
BH
. The map is given by the inclusion of the zero section of
V_{\phi}
.
After tensoring
\mathrm{KO}\in\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
, we get, again in
\mathrm{Spectra}^{G}
,

(C.8)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq_JacKO_def_1}\xrightarrow{u\otimes\mathrm{id}}\mathrm{KO}\otimes BH^{V_{\phi}-\tau_{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{ABS}(\Theta,\mathfrak{s})}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{KO}\otimes BH_{+}\otimes S^{\tau_{G}},

by (
C.5
).
Take the genuine
G
-fixed point of the composition of (
C.7
) and (
C.8
), and define
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}}
to be the following composition.

\textstyle{MT(H,\tau_{H})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}}}
\scriptstyle{\eqref{eq_JacKO_def_2}\circ\eqref{eq_JacKO_def_1}}
\textstyle{\left(\mathrm{KO}\otimes BH\otimes S^{\tau_{G}}\right)^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(BH\to\mathrm{pt})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[\tau_{G}]^{G}.}

Remark C.13
.

Actually, Definition
C.6
above is the analogy of the “alternative definition” of topological elliptic genera, given in Proposition
3.20
and Remark
3.58
.
Note that we cannot give the analog of Definition
3.53
since that definition relies on the dualizability of genuinely equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
in Fact
2.29
. As noted after Fact
2.29
, we do not have such a dualizability in equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-theory.

C.2.
Example: The
U
-and
O
-topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera

Here we introduce a
twin
of examples—
(U,U)
,
(O,SO)
—where the general construction of Section
C.1
applies. The content of this subsection is compared to Section
4
in the main body of the article, where we construct
trio
of examples of topological elliptic genera.

C.2.1.
Definitions

Definition C.14

(The topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
genera
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(n)_{k}}
and
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(n)_{k}}
)
.

For each
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we define the morphisms

(C.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(n)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MT(U(k),n\overline{V}_{U(k)})\to\mathrm{KO}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)},,

(C.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(n)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MT(SO(k),n\overline{V}_{SO(k)})\to\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(n)}]^{O(n)},,

by applying the general construction in Definition
C.6
to the following data. Here, for each group
K
appearing below, the notation
V_{K}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(K)
denotes the fundamental (a.k.a. defining, or vector) representation.

•

For (
C.15
), the data
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(n)_{k}}
consists of

(C.17)

\displaystyle G:=U(n),\ H:=U(k),\ \tau_{G}:=kV_{U(n)},\ \tau_{H}:=n\overline{V}_{U(k)},\ V_{\phi}:=V_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{U(k)}

so that
\Theta_{U(n)_{k}}=\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{U(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(n)\times U(k))
, with its spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
obtained by Proposition
C.21
below.

•

For (
C.16
), the data
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(n)_{k}}
consists of

(C.18)

\displaystyle G:=O(n),\ H:=SO(k),\ \tau_{G}:=kV_{O(n)},\ \tau_{H}:=n\overline{V}_{SO(k)},\ V_{\phi}:=V_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}V_{SO(k)}

so that
\Theta_{O(n)_{k}}=\overline{V}_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{V}_{SO(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(n)\times SO(k))
, with its spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
obtained by Proposition
C.27
below.

A particularly important case is
n=1
, where we get

(C.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(1)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MTU(k)\to\mathrm{KO}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)},,

(C.20)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(1)_{k}}

\displaystyle\colon MTSO(k)\to\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(n)}]^{O(n)},.

Here the necessary spin structures are provided by the following. For the case of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(n)_{k}}
, we have

Proposition C.21
.

The virtual representation

(C.22)

\displaystyle\Theta_{U(n),U(k)}=\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{U(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(U(n)\times U(k))

has an
SU
-structure
\mathfrak{s}_{U,U}
, thus in particular a spin structure. Moreover, it is unique up to homotopy.

Proof.

The existence of an
SU
-structure is verified by the vanishing of the first Chern class. The uniqueness follows from
H^{1}(U(n)\times U(k);\mathbb{Z})=0
.
∎

In order to state the proposition regarding the case of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(n)_{k}}
, we need a little preparation. Consider the following group homomorphisms,

(C.23)

\displaystyle\alpha_{G}

\displaystyle\colon O(n)\hookrightarrow U(n),

(C.24)

\displaystyle\beta_{H}

\displaystyle\colon U\left(\lfloor k/2\rfloor\right)\hookrightarrow SO(2\lfloor k/2\rfloor)\hookrightarrow SO(k),

where
\alpha_{G}
is induced by
\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and
\beta_{H}
is induced by forgetting the complex structure of
\mathbb{C}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
to regard it as the real vector space
\mathbb{R}^{2\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
, and the second arrow is nontrivial only for
k
odd.
Then we can easily verify that

Lemma C.25
.

We have the following canonical isomorphism in
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}\left(O(n)\times U\left(\lfloor k/2\rfloor\right)\right)
,

(C.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}\left(\overline{V}_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{V}_{SO(k)}\right)\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}}\left(\overline{V}_{U(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}_{U\left(\lfloor k/2\rfloor\right)}\right).

Now we can state the proposition.

Proposition C.27
.

The virtual representation

(C.28)

\displaystyle\Theta_{O(n),SO(k)}=\overline{V}_{O(n)}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{V}_{SO(k)}\in\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(O(n)\times SO(k))

admits a spin structure, and there is, up to homotopy, a unique choice
\mathfrak{s}_{O,SO}
which admits the following equivalence of spin structures when restricted to
O(n)\times SO(k)
,

(C.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}(\mathfrak{s}_{O,SO})\simeq\mathrm{res}_{\alpha_{G}\times\mathrm{id}}(\mathfrak{s}_{U,U}).

Here we are using Lemma
C.25
, and the string structure
\mathfrak{s}_{U,U}
on
\Theta_{U(n),U(\lfloor k/2\rfloor)}
is the one in Proposition
C.21
.

Proof.

The existence of a spin structure is checked by the vanishing of the first and second Stiefel-Whitney classes.
The second claim follows by the fact that the map

(C.30)

\displaystyle BO(n)\times BU(k^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}BO(n)\times BSO(2k^{\prime})

for any
k^{\prime}\geq 1
is
3
-connected, so that giving a spin structure on
\Theta_{O(n),SO(k)}
is equivalent to giving a spin structure on
\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{id}\times\beta_{H}}(\Theta_{O(n),SO(k)})
.
∎

C.2.2.
Structures in the twins

The families of examples constructed in Section
C.2.1
get unified via the
structure maps
relating each other.
They consist of
external
and
internal
structure maps.

The external structure: relating
(U,U)
and
(O,SO)

The external structure relates
U
-and
O
-topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera. In this case, we simply have the following statement;

Proposition C.31

(Compatibility of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(n)_{k}}
and
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(n)_{k}}
)
.

The
U
and
O
-topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera are compatible in the sense that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{MT(U(k),n\overline{V}_{U(k)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(U(k)\hookrightarrow SO(2k))_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{U(n)_{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}^{O(n)}_{U(n)}}
\textstyle{MT(SO(2k),n\overline{V}_{SO(2k)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{O(n)_{2k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{TMF}[2kV_{O(n)}]^{O(n)}.}

The proof is analogous to the corresponding Proposition
4.38
.
Note that the choice of spin structure in Proposition
C.27
is made precisely to make this compatibility result hold.

The internal structure: relating different
(n,k)

Now we introduce the
internal
structures in the twin, which relates different pairs of parameters
(n,k)
. Fixing
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
to be any one of
(U,U)
,
(O,SO)
, and introduce the structure maps for the domains and codomains of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}
, respectively.
Actually, for the domain, the structure maps for
MT(\mathcal{G}(k),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{G}(k)})
’s are exactly the same as the one we introduced in Section
4.2.2
, forming a
stabilization-restriction fiber sequence
of tangential bordism spectra (Proposition
4.116
).
So here we focus on the structure maps for the codomain, the twisted equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-theories.

As we have remarked in Remark
4.80
, we can apply the definition (
4.76
) and (
4.77
) replacing
\mathrm{TMF}
to
\mathrm{KO}
.
Let us set
\mathcal{G}
to be one of
U
or
O
, and
N=2,1
, respectively. We get the maps

(C.36)

\displaystyle\mathrm{stab}:=\chi(V_{\mathcal{G}(n)})\cdot

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\to\mathrm{KO}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)},

(C.37)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{G}(n)}^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{KO}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\to\mathrm{KO}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}+Nk]^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}

We call the maps (
C.36
) and (
C.37
) the
stabilization
and
restriction
maps, respectively.
We get the following.

Proposition C.38

(The stabilization-restriction fiber sequence of equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
)
.

The maps (
4.76
) and (
4.77
) form a fiber sequence of
\mathrm{KO}
-module spectra,

(C.39)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{stab}]{\chi(V_{U(n)})\cdot}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{U(n)}]^{U(n)}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{res}]{\mathrm{res}_{U(n)}^{U(n-1)}}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{U(n-1)}+2k]^{U(n-1)},

(C.40)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{O(n)}]^{O(n)}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{stab}]{\chi(V_{O(n)})\cdot}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(n)}]^{O(n)}\xrightarrow[\mathrm{res}]{\mathrm{res}_{O(n)}^{O(n-1)}}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(n-1)}+k]^{O(n-1)}

Thus we get the diagram consisting of the structure maps,

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[(k+1)V_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)(V_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}+N)]^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[k(V_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}+N)]^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[(k+1)(V_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}+N)]^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}

where each pair of consecutive horizontal and vertical arrows form a fiber sequence.
Particularly important case is the case of
n=1
. Here let us focus on the case
\mathcal{G}=O
. The stabilization-restriction fiber sequence becomes

(C.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}}\mathrm{KO}[k]\xrightarrow{w(k)\cdot}\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{O(1)}+1]^{O(1)},

where we defined
w(k)\in\pi_{k-1}\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}
by the above fiber sequence of
\mathrm{KO}
-modules. We call it the
attaching element
, by analogy of the corresponding elements (
7.33
) in the main body.

We can identify the case
\mathcal{G}(n)=O(1)
with a familiar sequence connecting
\mathrm{KO}
and
\mathrm{KU}
, as follows.

Proposition C.46

(stabilzation-restriction fiber sequence for
O(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
)
.

(1)

We have
[B\mathbb{Z}/2,P^{2}BO]\simeq\mathbb{Z}/4
, and the class
[\overline{V}_{O(1)}]\in[B\mathbb{Z}/2,P^{2}BO]
of the fundamental representation represents a generator. In particular, we have

(C.47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KO}[(k+4)V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\simeq\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(1)}+4]^{O(1)}.

(2)

Let us use the identification

\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\simeq\mathrm{KO}\oplus\mathrm{KO}

corresponding to the decomposition
\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\simeq\mathbf{R}\mathrm{O}(O(1))=\mathbb{Z}[\underline{\mathbb{R}}]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[{V}_{O(1)}]
.
The diagram (
C.2.2
) of
\mathrm{KO}
-modules for
n=1
and
\mathcal{G}=O
is identified as follows.

\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}\oplus\mathrm{KO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\oplus(-\mathrm{id})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\beta\circ c}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KU}[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{R\circ\beta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\oplus(-\mathrm{id})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[4]\oplus\mathrm{KO}[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[2V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[3V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[4V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\simeq\mathrm{KO}[4]^{O(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[1]}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[2]}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[3]}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[4]}

Here,
c\colon\mathrm{KO}\to\mathrm{KU}
is the complexification,
\beta\colon\mathrm{KU}\simeq\mathrm{KU}[2]
is the Bott periodicity,
R\colon\mathrm{KU}\to\mathrm{KO}
is the realification.

(3)

For each integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, attaching elements (
C.45
) are identified as follows.

(C.54)

\displaystyle y(4m)

\displaystyle=0\in\pi_{-1}\mathrm{KO}[-V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\simeq\pi_{-1}\mathrm{KO},

(C.55)

\displaystyle y(4m+1)

\displaystyle=(0,1)\in\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\simeq\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}\oplus\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO},

(C.56)

\displaystyle y(4m+2)

\displaystyle=\eta\in\pi_{1}\mathrm{KO}[V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\simeq\pi_{1}\mathrm{KO},

(C.57)

\displaystyle y(4m+3)

\displaystyle=1\in\pi_{2}\mathrm{KO}[2V_{O(1)}]\simeq\pi_{0}\mathrm{KU}.

Proof.

(1) is a classical result which is not difficult to check directly. So we omit the detail here. Let us prove (2).
First, let us consider the rightmost square of (
2
).
The restriction map gives

(C.58)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}=\mathrm{id}\oplus\mathrm{id}\colon\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\simeq\mathrm{KO}\oplus\mathrm{KO}\to\mathrm{KO},

so by Proposition
C.38
we get the isomorphism
\mathrm{KO}[4]\simeq\mathrm{KO}[3V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}
and the commutativity of the rightmost square of (
2
).

For the leftmost square, we use the self-duality of stabilization-restriction fiber sequence. It is the
\mathrm{KO}
-version of Proposition
4.84
. There we have used self-duality result of equivariant
\mathrm{TMF}
in Fact
2.29
, but since we are dealing with the finite group
O(1)
, the
O(1)
-equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-theory is also self-dual and the analogous statement holds. In particular, we get the fiber sequence

(C.59)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KO}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}_{e}^{O(1)}}\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{stab}}\mathrm{KO}[V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}.

We know that, under the identification
\mathrm{KO}^{O(1)}\simeq\mathrm{KO}\oplus\mathrm{KO}
as above, the transfer map is identified as
\mathrm{id}\oplus\mathrm{id}\colon\mathrm{KO}\to\mathrm{KO}\oplus\mathrm{KO}
.
This gives an isomorphism
\mathrm{KO}\simeq\mathrm{KO}[V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}
with the commutativity of the leftmost square of (
2
).

Now let us prove the commutativity of the middle-left square of (
2
). In order for this, we use the model of twisted equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-theory in terms of twisted group algebras. See
[
Gom23
]
for details.
In general, for a discrete group
G
, an element
\omega\in[BG,P^{2}BO]
defines a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded twisted group algebra

\mathbb{R}_{\omega}[G]
. The corresponding
\omega
-twisted
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{KO}
-spectrum is realized by the space of Fredholm operators on
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded Hilbert spaces with an action of
\mathbb{R}_{\omega}[G]
.
In the case where the element
\omega
lifts to an element
\omega\in H^{2}(BG;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, the algebra
\mathbb{R}_{\omega}[G]
has the trivial
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading, and explicitly given by twisting the multiplication by
\omega
as
g\cdot h=\omega(g,h)gh
, where we are regarding
\omega
as a
\pm 1
-valued group
2
-cocycle on
G
.

In our case, the middle twist
\omega:=[2\overline{V}_{O(1)}]\in[BO(1),P^{2}BO]
is the image of the nontrivial generator of
H^{2}(BO(1),\mathbb{Z}/2)
. We immediately see that we actually have an isomorphism
\mathbb{R}_{\omega}[O(1)]\simeq\mathbb{C}
of algebras over
\mathbb{R}
.
Thus we get the identification
\mathrm{KO}[2\overline{V}_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\simeq\mathrm{KU}
, together with the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[2\overline{V}_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{O(1)}^{e}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KU}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}}

Now we invoke of the classical fact that the following is a fiber sequence (e.g.,
[
Bru12
]
),

(C.64)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KO}[1]\xrightarrow{\cdot\eta}\mathrm{KO}\xrightarrow{\beta\circ c}\mathrm{KU}[2]\xrightarrow{R}\mathrm{KO}[2].

Combining this fiber sequence and commutativity of (
C.2.2
) and Proposition
C.38
gives the commutativity of the middle-left square of (
2
).

For the remaining middle-right square in (
2
), we again use the self-duality argument. We know that the two stabilization maps neighbouring
\mathrm{KO}[2V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}
in (
2
) are
\mathrm{KO}
-linear dual to each other, up to degree shift by
4
. On the other hand, we observe that the fiber sequence (
C.64
) is also self-dual in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{KO}}
, where
\beta\circ c
is identified as the dual to
R
. This means that the commutativity of the middle-right square in (
2
) follows from that of the middle-left square which we have already proved. This completes the proof of Proposition
C.46
(2).

(3) follows directly from the analysis so far in the proof of (2).
y(0)
and
y(1)
are obvious. The identifications of
y(2)
follow from the fiber sequence (
C.64
). This finishes the proof of Proposition
C.46
.
∎

Going back to the general situation, the compatibility of the topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera and the internal structure maps is stated as follows.

Proposition C.65

(Compatibility of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}
and internal structure maps)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
be either one of
(U,U)
and
(O,SO)
.
The following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{H}(k-1),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k-1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k-1}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{G}(n)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{stab}}
\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{H}(k),n\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(V_{\mathcal{H}(k)})\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{G}(n)_{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n)}]^{\mathcal{G}(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\mathcal{G}(n)}^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}
\textstyle{MT(\mathcal{H}(k),(n-1)\overline{V}_{\mathcal{H}(k)})[Nk]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)_{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KO}[kV_{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}+Nk]^{\mathcal{G}(n-1)}}

The proof is analogous to the corresponding Proposition
4.144
.

As a corollary, we get the statement corresponding to Corollary
4.153
. In particular, we get the following relation with the Euler numbers:

Corollary C.72

(The restriction of
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{G(1)}
is the Euler number)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
be either one of
(U,SU)
and
(O,SO)
. Correspondingly we set
N=2,1
, respectively. For any closed manifold
M
with a strict tangential
\mathcal{H}(k)
-structure
\psi
(Definition
2.118
—so that in particular
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M=Nk
), the composition

(C.73)

\displaystyle\Omega_{Nk}^{\mathcal{H}(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{PT}}}{{\simeq}}\pi_{Nk}MT\mathcal{H}(k)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}_{G(1)_{k}}}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{G(1)}]^{G(1)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{G(1)}^{e}}\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}.

sends the class
[M,\psi]\in\Omega_{Nk}^{\mathcal{H}(k)}
to the Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)\in\mathbb{Z}=\pi_{0}S\xhookrightarrow{u}\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}
.

C.3.
Application: Divisibility constraints of Euler numbers for oriented manifolds

In the main body of this paper, we derive interesting divisibility results of Euler numbers out of topological elliptic genera.
Now that we got the relation between
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}
and Euler numbers in Corollary
C.72
, we can think about a similar application to derive the divisibility of Euler numbers.
We see below that this indeed gives a neat divisibility result, still provable by another elementary method. Here let us focus on the case of
O(1)
-topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera.

Let us introduce the following notation.

Definition C.74
.

For each positive integer
k
, define
d^{\mathrm{KO}}_{SO}(k)
to be the order of the element
w(k)\in\pi_{k-1}\mathrm{KO}[(k-1)V_{O(1)}]^{O(1)}
in (
C.45
).

Here, by Proposition
C.46
, we explicitly know

(C.75)

\displaystyle d^{\mathrm{KO}}_{SO}(k)=\begin{cases}1&k\equiv 0\pmod{4},\\
\infty&k\equiv 1,3\pmod{4},\\
2&k\equiv 2\pmod{4}.\end{cases}

The divisibility argument is based on the observation that, by the long exact sequence associated to the stabilization-restriction fiber sequence (
C.45
), we have

(C.76)

\displaystyle d^{\mathrm{KO}}_{SO}(k)\cdot\mathbb{Z}=\mathrm{im}\left(\mathrm{res}_{O(1)}^{e}\colon\pi_{k}\mathrm{KO}[kV_{O(1)}]\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\right)

On the other hand, by Corollary
C.72
, we know that, for any oriented closed manifold
M
with
\dim M=k
, the Euler number
\mathrm{Euler}(M)
is contained in the right hand side of (
C.76
). This implies that we have

(C.77)

\displaystyle\left.d^{\mathrm{KO}}_{SO}(k)\,\right|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

Combining (
C.75
), we deduce

Proposition C.78

(A divisibility constraint of Euler numbers from
\mathrm{Jac}^{\mathrm{KO}}
)
.

For any oriented closed manifold
M
with dimension
\dim M\equiv 2
(mod
4
), we have

(C.79)

\displaystyle 2\,|\,\mathrm{Euler}(M).

This result itself can be proved directly, as follows. We have

(C.80)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Euler}(M)\equiv\sum_{i=0}^{\dim M}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}H^{i}(M;\mathbb{R})\pmod{2}.

If
M
is oriented Riemannian and of dimension
2
mod
4
, the intersection pairing gives a skew-symmetric nondegenerate pairing on
\oplus_{i}H^{i}(M;\mathbb{R})
. Equivalently, we have a complex structure on this vector space. This means that the total dimension should be even, proving that
\mathrm{Euler}(M)=0
.

Actually, this direct proof is essentially related to our proof using
O(1)
-topological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-genera. Namely, the right hand side of (
C.76
) for
k\equiv 2
(mod
4
) is identified with

(C.81)

\displaystyle\mathrm{im}\left(R\colon\pi_{0}\mathrm{KU}\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{KO}\right)

by our analysis in the proof of Proposition
C.46
. This is
2\mathbb{Z}
because complex vector spaces have even real dimension.
